#8466. Animal Attack Roundup, Nov. 4

While they predominate the media reports, most animal attack victims are not tourists unwisely attempting to take selfies with wildlife. People who live and work every day among animals are the ones who most often face the consequences.

As expanding populations push human settlements further into wildlife habitat, we can expect to see more conflicts. Here are some recent incidents.

Photo: Wikimedia Commons/Tisha Mukherjee, CC BY-SA 4.0

In Indonesia, 24-year-old Selviana Ambu Kaka was attacked by a crocodile while washing clothes in the Bondo Kodi River. Her fiancé and his sister witnessed a large crocodile emerging from the water, biting Ambu Kaka on the leg, and dragging her into the river. They immediately alerted nearby relatives and authorities, and a search commenced.

The people of Southwest Sumba, the closest town, organized and combed the area with nets. When they found a large crocodile matching witness descriptions, they hauled it onto shore and killed it. The animal measured nearly 4.9 meters long.

Early on the morning of October 24, Ambu Kaka’s remains were found approximately 30m from the attack site. She is one of many such victims in Indonesia, which sees more saltwater crocodile attacks than any other country.

Habitat destruction is largely behind the massive number of human-crocodile conflicts. It’s an understandably hard sell, however, for conservation workers to convince locals that preventing attacks means protecting the perpetrators.

The victim had recently returned from Jakarta to become formally engaged on October 30.

Photo: Wikimedia Commons/ Rohit Gangwal, CC BY-SA 4.0

A 65-year-old farm laborer, Tulsi Rama Sawar, was killed by a leopard in state of Maharashtra, just north of Mumbai, on November 1. The noise of the attack summoned help from the locals. By the time they arrived, the leopard had fled, but Sawar was deceased. Her case comes less than a month after a leopard killed a 52-year-old woman in the same area.

Town residents are demanding immediate government action to capture the animal. If their demands for cage traps and cameras are not granted, locals threaten collective protest. The attacks represent a threat to their lives, but also their livelihoods, as farmers are unable to work the fields out of fear of attack.

Elsewhere, in Uttar Pradesh near the Nepal border, a 45-year-old farmer named Kennu fought off an attacking leopard, sustaining serious injuries. The man was working in a sugarcane field when the predator leaped out from hiding. Kennu fought back, and the animal retreated long enough for him to flee home, where family members brought him to medical care. Forestry officials have urged agricultural workers to avoid working in the fields alone.

Farm workers are not the only leopard victims, however. Early in the morning of November 4, a 15-year-old girl was dragged from her home in the Shahdol district of central India.

Saraswati Singh Gond was sleeping in bed beside her sister on their veranda, which did not have a door. A leopard entered during the night, not even waking her sister. Singh Gond’s body was found approximately 300m from the house. The search for the leopard continues.

Photo: Wikimedia Commons/Shadow Ayush, CC BY-SA 4.0

Leopards are only one of the wildlife threats that rural Indians face. A 55-year-old woman was gathering mushrooms in Utterakand in northern India on October 30 when she was attacked and killed by a wild elephant. Devaki Rana was lifted up and thrown to the ground, her son reported.

The incident occurred in one of India’s elephant corridors, which allow highly endangered Asian elephant herds to move between their fragmented habitats. The corridors are essential to conservation, but also present a concentrated area where attacks are more common.

Another fatal encounter took place in the state of Chhattisgarh in east-central India. A man of between 35 and 40 years of age, traveling by motorcycle with his nephew, came upon an elephant standing in the road. It immediately charged them, killing the uncle, while his nephew managed to hide beneath a parked truck. Eventually, the elephant left, and he was able to escape.

Locals believe that the elephant in question is one known as Kabra Kaan, which they see frequently and which has been connected with earlier incidents. The Forestry Department, however, has not confirmed this.

Elephant corridors allow them to pass between the remaining forested areas. Photo: Wildlife Trust of India

In Japan, brown bear attacks have been rising for several years. As in many places, human settlements are encroaching on brown bear territory. A decrease in farmland has left no boundary between the urban and the forested worlds. The government has responded with controversial bear culls, despite the concerns of conservation workers.

The latest incident is very different from the increasingly common attack stories. Late on the night of October 30, an express train running on Hokkaido’s Soya Line collided with a large brown bear. The body of the bear was trapped under the train, and the wheels derailed.

A brown bear became trapped beneath the wheels of Sarobetsu No 3. Photo: Hokkaido Railway Company

None of the crew or 32 passengers were injured in the crash. However, fearing more bears were in the area, officials kept everyone on the train overnight, until they could be safely extracted the following morning. Company officials have confirmed that this is the first bear-collision derailment in their nearly 40-year history.

Lou Bodenhemier holds an MA in History from the University of Limerick and a BA in Creative Writing from the University of Arizona. He’s interested in maritime and disaster history as well as criminal history, and his dissertation focused on the werewolf trials of early modern Europe. At the present moment he can most likely be found perusing records of shipboard crime and punishment during the Age of Sail, or failing that, writing historical fiction horror stories. He lives in Dublin and hates the sun.

#8461. Young Climbers Bag New Routes, Including First Ascent, in Rolwaling

An energetic foursome of young French climbers had a busy time recently in Nepal’s Rolwaling Valley, east of the Khumbu. Eric Bazon, Pierre Girot, Sophie Jacob, and Kilian Moni did two new routes, including a first ascent of a 5,205m rocky spire and one very difficult line. They then climbed another peak on a “rest” day.

As soon as the climbers reached Rolwaling, they had to change their primary goal, which was in poor condition. Their acclimatization was also progressing more slowly than expected. Instead, they focused their attention on a ridge they saw from their base camp every morning. On October 20, Bazon, Jacob, and Girot set off toward the summit, while Moni waited for them at the base.

“Before long, the climbing turned vertical, and the ridge became razor-sharp and exposed,” Bazon wrote. The climb took three days on the ridge, bivvies included, and led Bazon, Sophie Jacob, and Pierre Girot to the west summit of ChekiGo (6,128m). It took another full day and one more bivouac to reach base camp.

Cheki Go’s main summit was first climbed in 2005 by Bruce Normand of the UK and Oliver Von Rotz of Switzerland via the west face. A Spanish team led by Mikel Zabalza first reached the west summit via a direct route up the west face in 2017. The team had just returned from bagging the first ascent of Jugal II.

“It was quite an intense experience for a ‘secondary’ project: one that combined rock climbing on mediocre granite, improvised protection, and steep snow ridges, all while hauling our ropes on our backs,” said Bazon.

The ridge was technical enough to be a good start: 5c on rock and M4 on mixed terrain. In Alpine grades, the team assessed it as TD (very difficult).

Since the weather forecast looked good for another four days, the team decided to continue climbing. They split into two teams: Bazon and Girot headed for a peak previously climbed by elite American alpinists Domen Kastelic and Sam Hennessey in 2014: Chukyima Go (6,259m). Like their predecessors, the French team approached the west face from the Naa Valley in Rolwaling.

“There was this direct, narrow, and obvious couloir that simply begged to be climbed,” Bazon reported.

The climbers at an open bivy. Photo: Eric Bazon

The next morning, they found the west face in great condition, which allowed them to climb a direct route to the summit along rock cracks, steep ice, and varied mixed terrain.

It took them two days (October 27-28), with one cold bivy on the face with their feet in their backpacks, to complete the route’s 23 pitches. Their West Face Direct route is 900m long, and the team assessed it as ED (extremely difficult), with M6 on mixed sections, and an Alpine ice grade of AI5.

The photo below shows that the new French route, TchikitaMa Go, runs to the right of the previous line opened by the two Americans.

Left: the new route opened by the French team, compared, right, to the 2014 line by Domen Kastelic and Sam Hennessey. Photo: Eric Bazon/PlanetMountain

Meanwhile, Sophie Jacob and Kilian Moni went for an unclimbed peak that also rose right in front of their base camp: 5,205m Janjmu. The pair completed the first ascent on an aesthetic 600m line up the north face. They called the route The Eternal Stars (“Les etoiles eternelles”) after some friends who had recently perished.

“It felt just like being at home in the Alps, with the added challenge of high altitude,” the climbers reported. “We pushed ourselves to the limit, both physically and mentally, across demanding yet incredibly logical pitches.”

But this was still not enough for the pair. They squeezed in a final climb of Yalung Ri in what they called “a contemplative, active recovery” day. They then finished the trip by paragliding from the summit.

Kilian Moni flies from the top of Yalung Ri. Photo: Kilian Moni

#8457. Duo Completes Baffin Island Circuit By Skiing, Hiking, and Packrafting

Caleb McDaniels, 25, and Henry Penfold, 22, have completed their impressive expedition around Baffin Island. The Americans combined skiing, hiking, and packrafting to almost circle the world’s fifth-largest island. McDaniels told ExplorersWeb they did about 20% skiing and the rest was evenly split between backpacking and packrafting.

The pair began and ended in Iqaluit, Nunavut’s capital. The final section of their trip was also the longest, a 1,130km stretch with no villages after Igloolik to resupply. They made light work of it, knocking it out in 40 days and bringing their total distance to 3,262km over 145 days, from their late May start to their return to Iqaluit in mid-October. By then, the oncoming Arctic winter gave them few hours of light to work with.

The pair’s completed route around Baffin Island. Photo: Caleb McDaniels

With no resupply points, the pair left Igloolik with 82kg of food. They hoped to do it in seven weeks but brought even more food, because they weren’t sure how long that giant giant section would actually take, explained Caleb McDaniels.

“With no way to carry that much weight in our packs, we loaded up our packrafts and paddled the first 400 miles [644km]. Then, for the final 300 miles [483km], we heaved on our packs to walk overland,” they explained.

The paddling portion went quickly, aided by some ingenuity. “Much of the time, we were able to sail using our trekking poles as masts and panels cut out of an old tent for sails. Speeding along at over five mph, we did some incredible downwinding, spray flying from our bows.”

Using makeshift sails. Photo: Caleb McDaniels

They also effectively halved the 480km trek by packrafting over two large lakes, including Nettilling, the world’s largest lake on an island. Yet there was no way to ease the final 160km slog into Iqaluit.

Postholing through early winter snow back to Iqaluit. Photo: Caleb McDaniels

“It was more of a postholing session — we slowly picked our way across snow-covered boulder fields and partly frozen marshlands. Constantly breaking through thin ice over the tundra marshes, we walked in our drysuits to keep our legs and feet dry. Each morning required half an hour of chipping ice and melting out our shoes, which invariably froze solid,” they said.

McDaniels and Penfold had planned a roughly circular route around the island, but “we weren’t trying to circumnavigate Baffin,” McDaniels told ExplorersWeb. Two previous parties had circumnavigated the island by dogteam, but McDaniels and Penfold’s route was driven by aesthetic considerations rather than to satisfy a catchy trip definition.

Slogging overland. Photo: Caleb McDaniels

They chose their route after looking at satellite images and enjoyed both spectacular scenery and some special wildlife sightings, including at least one that was a little too close for comfort.

“One morning, as I went to fill our water bottles, I stumbled upon a sleeping polar bear hidden behind a low rise. Only a few yards away, it lifted its massive white head and glared at me for a moment before returning to its nap in the sun,” McDaniels wrote. “I carefully retreated behind the rise and yelled over to Henry to get the shotgun and flares out just in case. We quickly packed our boats and got on the water.”

In Foxe Basin, they almost collided with a sleeping walrus, and on Nettilling Lake, they met one of the resident seals. “He was immensely curious and not at all afraid. He swam around our boats for half an hour, playing with our stern lines and swimming back and forth between us. At one point, I put my hand into the water, and he brushed it with his nose!”

Martin Walsh is a writer and editor for ExplorersWeb.

Martin spent most of the last 20 years backpacking the world on a shoestring budget. Whether it was hitchhiking through Syria, getting strangled in Kyrgyzstan, touring Cambodia’s medical facilities with an exceedingly painful giant venomous centipede bite, chewing khat in Ethiopia, or narrowly avoiding various toilet-related accidents in rural China, so far, Martin has just about survived his decision-making.

Based in Da Lat, Vietnam, Martin can be found in the jungle trying to avoid leeches while chasing monkeys.

#8454. Americans Bag Major First Ascent in Pakistan

Americans Dane Steadman and Cody Winckler, awarded with a Piolet d’Or in 2025 after the first ascent of Yashkuk Sar, have done a similar monster 1,800m-long new route on the north face of Sosbun Brakk (6,414m). This time, Grant Stewart joined them for this off-season climb in the Karakoram.

This first ascent of the peak’s north face included difficulties up to M7, WI5. The climbers had to overcome several obstacles before even setting foot on the face. Like many other alpine-style teams this season, they were originally planning to climb another peak. But instead of adapting because of washed-out trails, they had bureaucratic issues. Their permit, obtained 10 months earlier, was revoked some weeks before their departure. They had to quickly find a different goal.

Meanwhile, the airline misplaced Winckler’s baggage — every adventurer’s nightmare. Somehow, the outfitter sent it to base camp some days later. Then, Steadman had to overcome serious Acute Mountain Sickness in base camp. Their acclimatization was hampered by freak spells of bad weather. Finally, the line on Sosbun Brakk they initially chose was too hard and risky because of a hanging serac they only spotted after they began.

The team’s lonely base camp. Photo: Dane Steadman

“Back at BC, it was time to rest, regroup, and contemplate a totally different line that we’d seen from on the wall, which gave us new hope,” Steadman recalled.

Off to the north face. Photo: Dane Steadman

On October 1, the three climbers set off on that potential new line. “Instead of angling left up hard mixed ground above the cone as before, we made an arcing traverse right, across an ice apron, to gain runnels on the right side of the face,” Steadman explained.

The team gained 800m on that first day and set their first bivouac on a snowy arete, just in time to be slammed by a violent storm that kept them in their tent the whole next day.

Steep mixed terrain. Photo: Dane Steadman

A tarp to protect the team’s bivouac from falling spindrift. Photo: Dane Steadman

“In the morning, we made a gamble for the top by leaving the tent and starting up a massive gully system,” Steward wrote. “At its top, we reached another dead end in a huge serac barrier. To escape this, Dane committed to a visionary pitch, casting off up a blank slab to eventually reach an ice smear.”

Chaotic, difficult rocky sections. Photo: Dane Steadman

“A huge serac to our right and smooth slabs out left posed a serious question; eight meters of sideways teetering on tiny gneissic edges and a miraculous rock thread were the answer,” said Steadman.

“We continued up the massive hanging glacier to reach the hallucinatory final headwall of steep ice/mixed climbing and mushroom-weaving lunacy,” Stewart continued. “One final traverse with 1,800m of exposure under our feet and a magically appearing, dead-vertical ice tongue led us to the summit ridge.”

A totally vertical section. Photo: Grant Stewart

“The wild terrain on the upper mountain took longer than we wanted,” Steadman admitted. By the time they finally reached the summit, it was 4 pm, the storm was coming the following day, and they still had a long way (and many rappels) down.

Summit picture on Sosbun Brakk. Left to right, Dane Steadman, Grant Stewart, and Cody Wrincker. Photo: Dane Steadman

“The sun would set around 6 pm, so we threaded the first of 35 rappels and started down. Long after dark, we finally arrived back at our bivy ledge at around 5,800m,” Steadman recalled.

The storm was not supposed to start until noon the following day, but it was already snowing at 8 am, with still 1,200 vertical meters of descent to go.

Rappelling down the route. Photo: Dane Steadman

“Before long, we were caught in a proper storm,” Stewart explained. “We committed to a faster and hopefully safer descent by rappelling down the west rib to the Sokha/Sosbun col. By the time we reached the col, we could barely communicate over the screaming southwest wind.”

After some scary rappels in high winds down the serac band, the climbers finally reached a sheltered section of the face. They still had to deal with heavy spindrift coming from above until finally, at around 4 pm, they touched down on the glacier. And yet, the adventure was not over — especially for Grant Stewart:

Following Dane, I took maybe 10 steps when I felt the ground under me crack. Instinctively, I jumped and landed on my stomach as a huge crevasse swallowed the ground behind me. Soaking wet, we began postholing into the wan light of a mesmerizing white out.

[We stuck] to the center of the glacier as avalanches rumbled down all around in the whirling snow. After a strange eternity, the ragged colors of a tent appeared and we were greeted by ecstatic dancing, big hugs, and endless cups of chai from (our BC crew) Ibrahim and Idrees.

Happy climbers and base camp crew after a safe return from the summit. Photo: Dane Steadman

The team didn’t report their success and the details of the climb until they returned home. And then, the first words of appraisal were not for the route itself, but for the mountains and people of Gilgit-Baltistan.

“It’s a unique place…where continents and cultures collide. Trips there, while often difficult, are rewarding in ways hard to describe simply,” Steadman said. This was his third trip to the Karakoram, a place he has fallen in love with.

Angela Benavides graduated university in journalism and specializes in high-altitude mountaineering and expedition news. She has been writing about climbing and mountaineering, adventure and outdoor sports for 20+ years.

Prior to that, Angela Benavides spent time at/worked at a number of local and international media. She is also experienced in outdoor-sport consultancy for sponsoring corporations, press manager and communication executive, and a published author.

#8450. Weekend Warm-Up: Assynt

Assynt, subtitled “Home is where the croft is,” is a short documentary that brings viewers to the Scottish Highland community of Assynt, where residents live on traditional crofts.

The croft is a uniquely Scottish term, describing the smaller, less fertile plots of land that poorer people were forced onto, following the infamous Highland Clearances of the 18th and 19th centuries. Crofters lived on their crofts and farmed for subsistence, grazing sheep on shared land. Unable to support themselves on the croft alone, most also worked in fishing, kelp, or peat to support themselves.

It was a very hard life. But Lewis MacAskill, whom the viewer is soon introduced to, doesn’t see it that way. He is one of just over a thousand residents of Assynt, who chose to stay and continue the crofting tradition.

“I regard the croft as an extension of who I am as a person,” MacAskill explains.

The small community of Assynt. Photo: Screenshot

MacAskill introduces us to some of the other members of the community. Anna is a sheepdog owner and trainer, whom we see herding sheep using the same techniques people here have used for centuries. Stuart is a passionate fisherman, who shares his love of Assynt’s natural landscape with his young son.

We also meet some of the creatives who have, MacAskill says, been drawn by the unique landscape and material culture. We see a potter using local materials, a fiddler, and a spinner on her spinning wheel.

The spinning wheel came to Europe from the Middle East by the 13th century, and was widely adopted by the 18th century, replacing the older drop spindle and distaff. Photo: Screenshot

MacAskill moved to the croft a decade ago, and doesn’t regret it. Having grown up visiting every summer, he has always felt drawn to the land and knows others feel the same. But a small, isolated community like Assynt is constantly in danger of depopulation. MacAskill hopes that people keep choosing to move back and stay, heeding the same call he did.

The film ends on this note, with a montage of scenes from life in Assynt and the landscape it sits in, while an elderly crofter sings a traditional Scottish song of homecoming.

Lou Bodenhemier holds an MA in History from the University of Limerick and a BA in Creative Writing from the University of Arizona. He’s interested in maritime and disaster history as well as criminal history, and his dissertation focused on the werewolf trials of early modern Europe. At the present moment he can most likely be found perusing records of shipboard crime and punishment during the Age of Sail, or failing that, writing historical fiction horror stories. He lives in Dublin and hates the sun.

#8449. 10 of the Hottest Hunting Suppressors on the Market in 2026

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 18, 2026 2:15 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

The latest for gun hunters and competitive shooters.

It’s never been easier to get a suppressor for your hunting rifle. But choosing one, for a first-time buyer, can be intimidating. There are thousands of options on the market and narrowing it down to the best can for your hunting rifle isn’t easy if you don’t know what to look for. So many new suppressors are hitting the market that what was a top-of-the-line can a couple years ago is just average today.

I’ve been shooting every new suppressor I can get my hands on for years, and these are the best ones that I have used.

This is one of the best hunting rifle suppressors I have ever used. Like many others that are leading the pack, it’s 3D printed from titanium, making it both strong and economical. It measures 5 inches long and weighs just 5.75 ounces. Despite its lightweight profile, it’s very quiet. In fact, it’s substantially quieter than several other popular suppressors — something I noted on a recent antelope hunt.

This is one of my top picks because it’s simple, rugged, quiet, and you’ll hardly notice it on your muzzle. It is direct thread only and, because threads are cut into the suppressor itself, they cannot be changed like a HUB-compatible suppressor. For a hunting suppressor, that’s a feature, not a bug. At $900, this is a really good value. Many top-level hunting suppressors will cost you $1,000 or more.

In the year since its introduction, Airlock has released 30-cal and 7mm versions of the Zero Gravity, as well as a 3.7-ounce Zero Gravity Nano, which is designed for bolt-action centerfire .22 cals like the 22 ARC, 22 Creed, and .22-250 Rem. It’s slightly larger than a rimfire suppressor, but satisfyingly quiet.

The Nomad Ti XC is another excellent option for a hunting suppressor. Its 3D printed design allows for modern baffles and reduces weak spots. The front and rear of the can are threaded and it is compatible with HUB rear mounts. I prefer using a Silencerco Ti direct thread adapter on the rear, and the standard .308 front cap. Other companies like Salmon River Solutions are making accessories like a directional brake that threads into the front cap to further reduce recoil and tame down cartridges like the .300 PRC. As pictured, the suppressor weighs less than 10 ounces and works wonderfully for anything 30-caliber and under. A nice feature is the inclusion of deep tool notches which makes removing the HUB mounts easy without worry of damaging the suppressor.

Another great option, if you want a shorter overall length for your rifle, is the Nomad TiOTB, which has a chamber that extends back over the barrel. This suppressor has the same interchangeable endcaps, but works exclusively with a thread-over-brake mount.

I’ve been shooting the TiTrex 300 Ti for several months now, and this is one of the best options for a general-purpose hunting suppressor out there. After all that shooting, it still only weighs 9.5 ounces, and it offers great mounting and front-cap modularity.

A couple years ago, the Silencerco Scythe Ti was one of few .30-cal silencers that light, but it’s been plagued by weld failure issues. The TiTrex 300 Ti is also made of titanium, is slightly lighter, and uses HUB mounts at the rear and an open-source front cap that works with the Silencerco Bravo front mounts. You can use a normal endcap or the short endcap brake, which helps reduce recoil, and both have great spanner wrench notches. It’s also compatible with some aftermarket Endcap brakes like the Salmon River Solutions Grim, which can help cut recoil even more.

The TiTrex has a 3D-printed body and baffle system that is rugged, durable, and even full-auto rated. This is a great option to use with a front brake as a competition can, but that can also work well on your hunting rifle. It would also be a solid choice as your first suppressor. A unique feature is that the serialized portion of the suppressor is a stainless-steel band at the rear of the can, that can be moved to a different core if you manage to wear the original out or damage it somehow.

There are three big trends in hunting suppressors in 2026, and the Buckskin 30 from Tenet tackles all of them. First, it’s 3D printed. Additive manufacturing has allowed companies to quickly develop and prototype suppressor models while reducing weak points like welds on thin titanium.

Second, it incorporates a front brake. Suppressors reduce recoil by slowing gases as they exit the muzzle, and incorporating a brake on the front tames them even further. The Buckskin features a built-in brake that is non-adjustable and won’t come loose.

Third, it’s light. It’s printed with titanium and mine weighs 8.5 ounces — making it the lightest .30-cal suppressor I own. I’ve used this suppressor on my 20-inch 300 PRC, several 6.5 Creedmoors, and have been putting a lot of rounds through it on a 16-inch 7mm Backcountry. It does a great job at reducing recoil while adding minimal extra weight.

If you want more adjustability and increased recoil reduction, the Tenet Limited 30 is another suppressor to look at. These are more expensive, but feature a removable front brake that cuts your rifle movement dramatically. Mine has a two-port brake and, while the first generation of brakes tended to blast your face with gas, the recently-released gen 2 brake has small steps in the curved brake to disrupt that gas impulse. It’s much more pleasant. If spotting your impacts with larger cartridges is important to you, a can like this fits the bill.

In the days of year-long waiting periods, modularity was king for hunting suppressors. While modular cans have their place, a simple dedicated hunting silencer is hard to beat. This fully 3D-printed titanium can from Unknown Munitions weighs about 11 ounces and features a reflex design, meaning the rear portion of the suppressor goes back over the barrel. That reflex expansion chamber allows a shorter portion — about four inches — to stick out in front of your muzzle. This improves balance and gives you a more compact package than the average 8-inch suppressor. The reflex design also creates a noticeably duller sound signature, whereas some produce a very sharp, snappy sound.

These are available in .30 caliber as well as 6.5mm and 6mm options, and models and fit over 0.75-inch diameter steel barrels and the OGL model fits larger carbon-fiber barrels. This suppressor isn’t modular, so you need to ensure compatibility before you order. Also check out their Reaper suppressor, which I haven’t had the chance to use, but should be another top contender.

I’ve used several of B&T’s Print-X series of titanium 3D-printed suppressors and have been impressed by all of them. Their modular VERS36 Ti model, however, deserves special recognition. To my knowledge, this is the lightest modular 36-caliber suppressor on the market, and it’s way cheaper than similar, more primitive cans were several years ago. If you’re wanting to get a suppressor to start out with, or to use on multiple hunting rifles, this is your jam.

It’s got 36-caliber baffles and comes with a .360 front cap. The rear is HUB-compatible and B&T makes nice titanium direct-thread adapters. You can get smaller front caps as well. If using on smaller cartridges or you just need a shorter overall package, the front section is removable. This is a flow-through design with spiral baffles that works well on bolt actions and gas-guns alike. It’s ideal for cartridges like the .350 Legend, but will work on anything with a .360-diameter bullet or smaller. I received mine through Silencer Central and it can be delivered to your door.

The StingerWorx Evo Hunter is a lightweight suppressor that’s more affordable than the 3D printed titanium cans, and it’s also much more serviceable. A downside of most printed suppressors is that there isn’t a great way to remove large amounts of carbon buildup from the baffles. Soaking and ultrasonic cleaners help, but a lot of shooters want a suppressor that can be disassembled and manually cleaned.

The Evo Hunter is just over 10 ounces, and it gets there by using multiple materials. It has a titanium blast baffle and chamber to provide a long service life, but uses an aluminum baffle stack downstream of that — where heat and abrasion isn’t as intense. If damaged, that front baffle stack is replaceable. Additionally, the Evo Hunter is compatible with StingerWorx’s front brake if you’re looking for additional recoil reduction. It’s got HUB threads at the rear and the blast chamber is deep enough to work with a variety of over-brake HUB mounts that you might already have. For the light weight, versatility, and serviceability, this can is a great value.

There are lots of suppressors out there and, because many of them are so similar, lots of good ones can get lost in the mix. Because we cover a lot of leading-edge ultralight cans, don’t think that we don’t pay attention to budget options, too. There are bottom-tier suppressors that are very cheap — often made of mostly aluminum — that will work, but can also leave buyers dissatisfied. When it comes to bang for your buck, stainless steel is still one of the best materials for a more affordable can. The first suppressor I ever bought was a large .30-cal stainless can. It was durable and affordable and its weight was the only downside.

The Banish HNT 30 SS is a great example of a modern great value. It’s compact and, at 13.8 ounces, not excessively heavy. It’s suitable for virtually any hunting rifle .30-caliber and smaller, and similar in size and form to the lightweight Banish Backcountry titanium can.  It’s got hub threads at the rear and comes with a direct-thread adapter. If you’re looking to try a suppressor for hunting and want a good-quality can, this is a great place to start.

If you’re looking for maximum suppression of your big-bore cartridges, the Goliath Gen 2 from Liberty Suppressors is an excellent option. This beast of a silencer is 10.25 inches long by 2 inches in diameter and designed specifically for cartridges like the .458 Socom, .450 Bushmaster, and .45/70 Gov’t. While big, this can is relatively light. It’s got twice the volume of suppressors like the Silencerco Hybrid 46, but weighs only about 4 ounces more (22 ounces total). This is a perfect fit for anyone hunting with a short-barreled .458 or .450 Bushmaster, or a trapper-style .45/70.

The suppressor has a titanium sleeve and titanium baffle stack that’s removable and serviceable so you can shoot cast lead bullets and clean out lead fouling — unlike sealed suppressors. I’ve had a Liberty .22 rimfire can for many years, and this is essentially a much larger version of that. I’ve been shooting it on my .45/70 and it’s working wonderfully on my .338 ARC SBR with subsonic ammo.

One of the best values in lightweight modular suppressors is the BDE from Primary Weapons Systems. I’ve been shooting the 5.56 model for the last several months, and it combines smart manufacturing with durability, value, and the kind of modularity a lot of hunters want. The BDE has hub threads at the rear, a blast chamber segment, and a 3D-printed main baffle segment. Forward of that, it has removable individual threaded baffle segments topped with an end cap.

This is a sealed suppressor, but baffles are designed to allow gasses to flow better and reduce backpressure, making a great option for both a bolt gun and AR-style semi-auto. Each segment of the can has wrench notches making disassembly easy, and you can customize to the platform you’ll be shooting. This has been living on my .223 practice bolt-action and the irregular surface contours do seem to help break up mirage. The BDE is also available in .30- and .36- caliber models, all with customizable baffle configurations.

Picking through the many different suppressor options can be daunting, and most good suppressors perform similarly. To find one that you’ll be happy with, consider details like construction and weight, but also the level of modularity you desire, or whether you’re just looking for a good value. If one of these isn’t quite what you’re looking for, I hope that seeing these options and features will help you home in on the model that you will be most satisfied with. The suppressor market is constantly evolving, and we will continue to use and test suppressors with the goal of helping you make the most informed decisions possible.

The OL editors pick the best deer hunting rifles on the market.

#8445. Florida Man Finishes Four Corners Walk Across America

After over three years of trekking 20,000km back and forth across the continental United States, endurance athlete Kyndal Ray Edwards has completed his ambitious Four Corners Walk. His route took him from coast to coast, then corner to corner, starting in Florida and ending in Maine, where a crowd of supporters met him last week.

Kyndal Ray Edwards, 33, is originally from Orange Park, Florida. After struggling with drug addiction, mental illness, and the justice system during his early adulthood, he decided to become an endurance athlete and mental health advocate. On January 1, 2022, he began his walking project from the beach in Jacksonville.

Over the next 15 months, Edwards walked to Long Beach, Washington, traveling 5,298km from the Atlantic to the Pacific. His plans expanded, and he undertook the challenge of walking to all four corners of the contiguous United States. From Long Beach, he headed to the northwesternmost point, Cape Flattery, also in Washington. After trekking south to San Diego, the next corner he headed for was all the way back in Florida, the southeast extremity.

Along the way, Edwards posted frequently on social media about his journey and his recovery from mental illness.

On October 25, Edwards reached West Quoddy Head Lighthouse in Lubec, Maine. This is the northeast corner of the U.S., and the finish line for Edwards. He walked a total of 19,544 kilometers in 1,022 days, averaging nearly 20km per day.

Edwards and supporters at the finish line. Photo: Kyndal Ray Edwards

A cross-country walking journey, Edwards says, has been his dream since 2013. But with the end of his Walking Across America project, Edwards is already ruminating on future challenges. Online, he has proposed going on an adventure with a stranger in all 50 states, running more ultra marathons, and undertaking endurance challenges on every continent. However, he has yet to commit to one.

The end of Kyndal Ray Edwards’s journey came only days before Karl Bushby, another long-distance trekker, completed his 28-year Goliath Expedition. The pair also happen, Edwards pointed out, to share a birthday.

#8443. Old-School Whitetail Wisdom for Stalking Cornfields During Deer Season

Published Nov 18, 2026 4:02 PM EST

There are two things that my friend Harvey Wilcourt loves more than anything else. One is ice-fishing, and the other is eating peanuts. It might not sound as though that bears any relation to whitetail deer hunting, but one time it actually helped me to collect a very big buck.

Harve and I had scheduled a day of winter fishing for walleyes, and the plan was to meet at a well-known parking area near the lake and then decide upon a particular frozen-over bay that might offer the best results. As it happened, however, car trouble delayed my arrival by more than an hour, so naturally, when I finally reached our meeting place, Harve had already headed out onto the ice-shrouded lake.

At first, I shrugged my shoulders, not knowing in which direction he might have headed. Then, I happened to look down at the ground near his parked vehicle, and guess what I spotted? Right, peanut shells! Right then, a wide smile came across my face because I instantly knew that tracking Harve would be easy.

In most places, the trail was relatively thin, but occasionally I’d come to an abandoned hole in the ice where shells littered the entire area. Then, the trail would lead off into the distance to another hole, and then still another. Eventually, I spied my partner in a distant channel, and we spent the remainder of the day fishing together. Without the discarded peanut shells showing the way, I might have never located Harve and might have ended up fishing solo that day.

The irony is that several years later, I duplicated the same type of feat, but this time the trail eventually led me to an eight-point buck. To the best of my knowledge, whitetails don’t maintain any particular fetish for peanuts, but they do love corn. And after filling their paunches in a cornfield, it’s quite common for them to carry off a cob in their mouths and continue eating from it as they travel to a nearby bedding site. Sometimes, they’ll finish with the cob along the way and let it fall to the ground right there. Other times, they’ll carry the cob all the way back to the actual bedding location, perhaps to snack upon later.

In any event, I had been hiking along the edge of a large cornfield near my home in southern Ohio, and in coming upon a threadbare trail exiting from the corn, I had found a partially eaten cob. Recalling my amusing experience with Harvey, I decided to follow the trail, and just inside the leading edge of a nearby beech forest I discovered another cob on the ground, and then still another. All of the cobs were in various stages of deterioration, so I knew that they had been dropped at different times over a period of weeks. Large, fresh deer tracks of uniform size and large droppings provided additional clues, and eventually, more than 300 yards into the timber, I came upon a lone bed. That confirmed I was in a buck’s bailiwick, because had there been tracks of varied sizes and several beds, I could have inferred that the deer were only does.

After resting the area for two days, I snuck back into the spot and quietly fabricated a makeshift blind behind a convenient blowdown overlooking the trail. Later, when there was just enough light to make a shot, a gray shadow materialized in the distance, traveling right along the trail. My arrow caught the eight-pointer at the base of the neck, and after sprinting 50 yards, the buck collapsed in a heap.

It’s no secret that bucks and corn go together like bacon and eggs, and we have plenty of all of those commodities, especially throughout the Midwestern agricultural belt. In fact, after talking recently with my local extension agent, I learned that in 1986, in the nine major farming states stretching from Ohio to Minnesota, more than 46 million acres of land were planted to corn.

Moreover, acquiring hunting permission in agricultural regions is invariably easier than it is on other privately owned lands because of the extensive damage often inflicted by wild animals. In Wisconsin alone, it was estimated that during 1986, deer destroyed more than $22 million worth of corn, and this cost each farmer an average of $490. Add to this the combined losses of alfalfa, soybeans, truck garden crops and other foods that whitetails regularly dine upon, and it should come as no surprise that though a majority of farmers certainly enjoy seeing deer on their land, they don’t take kindly to whitetail populations reaching such levels that they severely cut into the farmers’ annual incomes.

The exasperating problem that most hunters face is how to effectively hunt those often mammoth and intimidating cornfields.

The first task is to confirm that deer are indeed using the corn, and this is easily ascertained by merely hiking the perimeter of the field and looking for tracks entering and leaving the food plot. Obviously, the best time to do this is either after a rainy period, when the ground is soft, or after several days of light snowfall.

If the region has been experiencing several weeks of dry weather, however, and the terrain is like hardpan, tracks are not easily detected. It is in this case that other types of insight must be applied to the hunting situation. For example, one time I discovered a cornfield where many stalks had been knocked down and countless partially eaten ears were on the ground. Initially, I thought that I had found a mother lode of deer activity, but I was dead wrong.

As I learned later, several raccoons had been at work. The telltale giveaway — pointed out to me by a friend who is a serious naturalist — should have been the fact that the ears were only partially eaten. Raccoons rarely eat an entire ear of corn; instead, they knock down a cornstalk and bite off just the tender tip of the ear, then move on to the next stalk.

If the cornfield borders a climax forest, corn-hungry squirrels may also give the false impression that deer have been at work. But in this situation, the animals climb the stalks, nibble away at the ears, stuff their cheeks and then retreat to their dens, where the scavenged kernels are placed in storage. This leaves the ears still attached to the stalks but gives them a ragtag, moth-eaten appearance.

If deer are working the corn, however, the clues to look for in the absence of tracks are scattered cobs on the ground that have been cleaned of almost every kernel of corn. Also keep on the lookout for droppings.

Once it has been determined that deer are hitting a particular cornfield on a regular basis, the best mode of hunting must be decided upon. And contrary to what you might expect, the best method of hunting is not necessarily the tactic you might like to use most, but rather the one that most appropriately falls in line with the agricultural cropping practices in your region.

In many areas, conventional harvesting methods are engaged in sometime during October or November, when the corn is brown and cured. The picking operation leaves the cornfield in a stubble-like condition, yet there remains on the ground plenty of spillage residue to attract the interest of local deer. Under these conditions, with the previously standing cornstalks no longer present and the concealing cover therefore eliminated, midday hunting is virtually out of the question. The animals will predictably visit the corn only in the early morning hours or at dusk. If hunting pressure is intense in the region, the deer may even be strictly nocturnal in their feeding.

Conversely, in many other regions, it is becoming an increasingly popular agricultural practice to leave corn standing in the field for as long as possible. With grain-elevator storage costs escalating like never before, and future market prices always uncertain, many farmers nowadays are simply delaying their harvesting either until such time as they have a specific need for the corn for stock feed or until the price increases, which it often does in February or March. Consequently, the hunter now finds that he can effectively hunt the cornfields not only early and late in the day, but also during the midday hours.

Regardless of whether a solo hunter elects to use bowhunting equipment, a rifle, a slug-loaded shotgun, a muzzleloader or a handgun, there are four options to choose from when hunting standing corn: sneak-hunting in and around the corn; sneak-hunting in and around nearby heavy-cover regions; taking a stand designed to intercept the animals coming to the corn; or taking a stand designed to intercept the animals as they leave the corn.

Still again, the right method to use may not necessarily be the hunter’s favorite way to hunt, but it must be geared to existing conditions. The foremost determinant that I rely upon to make my decision is the presence or absence of wind — assuming there is standing corn, of course.

On a windy day, I do not want to be in a tree stand. Wind is the result of downdrafts of cold air meeting warm air. Therefore, no matter which direction the wind is coming from, it will have a tendency to force scents down toward the ground, and this will readily give away a hunter’s location. Compounding the problem is the fact that deer do not like to move on windy days because the wind disallows the effective use of their sense of hearing. Calm days, on the other hand, or those during which the wind is only slightly and gently blowing from one specific direction are ideal for quietly waiting on stand for the deer to walk by.

Just the opposite is the case when hunting in and around the cornfield itself. Now is when a hunter wants the windiest conditions possible. This is because when whitetails bed during the midday hours, they feel most secure and safe by retreating into the heaviest, thickest cover they can find. In many agricultural regions, the terrain surrounding cornfields, such as gullies and forest lands, simply does not offer hiding cover that is as thick as that in the cornfields themselves. Consequently, the whitetails will commonly bed right in the cornfields in these areas.

Now it becomes obvious why the very time to hunt these cornfields is during periods of high winds. It is because under blowing conditions, a cornfield is a very noisy place. The leaves are dry and brittle later in the season, and the loud rustling and crackling noises that they make in the wind cover up so much human error in prowling around that a hunter can quite easily walk right up on deer without them suspecting his presence.

Moreover, a hunter doesn’t have to worry about his scent when hunting in a cornfield. Wind in a cornfield is a tricky thing. It hits the stalks and broad leaves and bounces every which way. As a result, deer in a cornfield may catch a hunter’s scent, but in most cases they will not be able to determine the direction from which it’s coming. As a result, they lock up, or freeze, in position because they know that if they attempt to run, they may blunder right into trouble. And if the hunter then happens to step into the right row of corn, he may have an easy shot at a stationary buck.

In going back to calm days, during which patiently waiting in a tree stand or ground blind is the preferred method of hunting either standing corn or stubble fields, there are two tricks that I use to properly locate my stand. First, it’s necessary to engage in enough scouting to know in advance where trails are located. It must be remembered, however, that it’s quite common for deer to leave a cornfield via a different trail than the one they used to enter the field, assuming, of course, that the deer are bedding somewhere other than in the corn itself. Therefore, a morning stand to intercept the deer leaving the corn will undoubtedly be in a different place than an evening stand that has been set up to ambush deer coming from their bedding areas to the corn.

Thus, what a hunter initially wants to do after locating trails is to ascertain what time of day they’re being used. The direction in which tracks are pointing is a sure clue — if the tracks are pointing toward the corn, they were probably made in the evening; if they are directed away from the corn, they probably indicate an exit trail that the animals use in the morning. Also, the hunter should be on the lookout for corncobs. Because deer obviously do not carry cobs from their bedding region back to the cornfield, but do carry cobs from the field to their bedding sites, the presence of intermittent cobs along the trail indicates in no uncertain terms that the travel route is a morning trail.

As a general rule, I do not like for either a morning stand or an evening stand to be too close to a cornfield where deer are feeding. If possible, depending upon his evaluation of the terrain, a hunter should try to situate his stands or blinds 100 to 200 yards from the cornfield so that he can overlook the exit and entrance trails that the deer are using to enter or leave the food plot.

There are several good reasons for this strategy. To begin with, the largest bucks in any given region do not like to expose themselves unnecessarily in broad daylight, especially in regions where hunting pressure is intense. They can pretty much be counted upon to leave the cornfield at the very hint of early morning’s first light. Because it may still be too dark to shoot accurately, the hunter wants to be farther back down the trail that the deer are using so that by the time they reach the stand location, it will be fully light enough to see.

Conversely, in the evening hours, the very largest bucks seldom enter a cornfield when the sky is still brightly illuminated. They like to linger back in the shaded, heavy cover of their bedding areas, sometimes not actually entering the cornfield until after full dark. Naturally, therefore, if a stand or blind is positioned close to the cornfield, by the time the deer reach the location, it will be too dark to see.

In all of this, it should be kept in mind that though the hunter doesn’t want to position his stand too close to the cornfield for either a morning or an evening watch, neither does he want his stand too close to the bedding area that the animals are using. This is simply too risky because the animals are very likely to detect the hunter as he tries to approach his stand site; and if they are spooked from the very region that they previously considered the safest place to be, they may not return for many days.

In fact, it’s worth mentioning that during the course of scouting, I always move slowly but make plenty of noise. Sometimes, I even whistle to myself as though I’m engaged in some type of routine outdoor work such as that of a surveyor or forester. Whitetails are accustomed to this type of minor disturbance, and they will simply circle around the intruder. And because the deer have the opportunity to hide or sneak away undetected, they’ll still have full confidence in the security of their chosen area, prompting them to return shortly after I’ve left.

Later, when I return to begin my hunting vigil, I stalk my stand. I actually sneak up on it just as though I were stillhunting through the woods. By doing this, the chances of any nearby deer recognizing my presence are much lower.

Pursuing whitetails when they’re actually in the corn is one of deer hunting’s most challenging and exciting endeavors. But the hunter should remember two things: If the terrain surrounding the cornfield is devoid of thick security cover, meaning that the corn itself is the very best place to hide and bed, the deer will probably cling to the corn all day and every day. This is especially the case if water is readily available in rain-filled puddles or tractor ruts. The second thing to remember — again — is that the windiest days are the best days to hunt.

Simply sneak-hunting around the perimeter of the cornfield can put venison on the meat pole, particularly during the early and late hours of the day, when the deer are feeding and have a tendency to work along “edges.” I’ve most often found this to be the case when the cornfield is bordered on one or more sides by lush hay meadows planted to some legume such as alfalfa, red or white clover or lespedeza. This is because it’s rather uncustomary for whitetails to gorge themselves on a single food type, as they are opportunists in their feeding habits and like variety in their diets. Therefore, if you know of a cornfield bordered by some other equally preferred food, you’ve hit paydirt!

In sneak-hunting around the perimeter of a cornfield, a hunter shouldn’t expose himself in the open by remaining along the outside edge. Instead, he should stay one or two rows inside the edge of the cornfield — just far enough in so that he’s concealed yet can still see along the outside edge and into the adjacent, secondary food plot. The only exception to this rule is when the cornfield butts up directly against a brushy thicket or other bordering cover. In this case, the hunter can remain along the outermost edge of the corn and move slowly along with minimal chance of detection.

If the entire outside perimeter of the cornfield has been worked and no deer have been seen, it’s now time to assume that the whitetails are deeper within the confines of the corn, where they are most probably either feeding or bedding.

If the hunter is familiar with the terrain from previous scouting missions — before the corn was planted — he should try to recall the locations in the field where there were rock piles, soupy swales, sand pockets and the like. These exist in many fields, and farmers simply plow and plant around them. Once the surrounding corn has grown high, however, such places are hidden from view, and they are the very types of locations where deer commonly like to bed. The hunter should sneak up on these places during windy conditions, and he might very well shoot an unsuspecting deer right in its bed.

If firearms are being used and several hunting partners are working together, staging drives is an excellent way to push deer through the corn to the opposite end of the field, where standers are likely to have shots. But there are a few important points to bear in mind when using this method. If the cornfield is large, it is imperative that a large number of drivers — spaced no more than eight to 10 rows apart — be employed, because otherwise the deer, reluctant to leave the security of the cover, will tirelessly circle and sneak back between the drivers. Consequently, if there are only a few hunters participating in the drive, only smaller cornfields where the animals can be moved effectively in a predetermined direction should be hunted.

Also, in the interest of safety, drives in cornfields should ideally include stand hunters placed in tree stands, or at least on some other elevated vantage points such as hillsides or knolls. This way, they will be able to look down and through the rows of corn to see both the escaping deer and their approaching partners. Likewise, when they shoot from such higher locations, the angles of their shots will be acutely downward, rather than horizontal.

Bowhunters can also enact drives in cornfields, but instead of hunting parallel with the rows in an attempt to push deer to standers in the distance, they should hunt perpendicular to the rows. In this type of situation, no hunters need be placed on stand.

Along the edge of the cornfield, the bowhunters should be spaced about 100 yards apart. The team leader at one end of this drive line should then use his arm to signal the first hunter on the far end to enter the field. As this hunter enters the first row, he should look right and left, then move on to the second row, and so on. After about 30 seconds, the drive master should then motion the next hunter to enter the cornfield in the same manner. What results is that instead of a straight and even drive line moving through the field, there is a diagonal shift across the field. Therefore, if a hunter enters a row and sees a deer he wants to shoot at, he can do so to either his right or left with complete confidence in safety, knowing that his partners to either side are far ahead of or behind him. If he does not wish to shoot, the deer will usually run parallel to the rows and eventually offer a shot to one of the next hunters in the line.

Finally, one of the shrewdest ways I know to evaluate potential cornfield hunting situations, especially in unfamiliar regions, is to go to a local airport and hire a small plane to fly over the terrain. Most pilots charge an average of $30 per hour, and if two hunters split the expense, the cost per person is not prohibitive. But most important, in one hour of flying about 500 feet above the ground, a hunter can learn more about an area than he can in two weeks of driving back roads and scouting on foot. A topographic map should be brought along, and the location and configuration of every cornfield in the region should be marked. Adjacent terrain and cover should be carefully examined, and it then should be easy to determine with a high level of accuracy how deer are living, moving and feeding in each specific region.

Read Next: Old-School Whitetail Wisdom for Deer Hunting When the Weather Finally Turns Cold

Believe me, if there are cornfields in the region you plan to hunt, you can be sure that bucks are in the corn — if not continuously, then at least intermittently. In either case, you should make the effort to be on hand, too, and when you leave, the chances are excellent that you’ll be dragging your deer behind you.

This story, “Deer By The Ear,” appeared in the November 1988 issue of Outdoor Life.

#8442. Adventure Links of the Week

When we’re not outdoors, we get our adventure fix by exploring social media and the web. Here are some of the best adventure links we’ve discovered this week.

Yosemite Visitors Join Forces To Perform Extraordinary Rescue on Half Dome: When a Polish hiker slipped while descending the cables on Yosemite’s Half Dome, she slid down the steep granite face and was left clinging to the rock. Her fiancé then lost his footing while trying to reach her.

A group of strangers came together to rescue the pair. Using ropes, slings, carabiners, and climbing harnesses, the group formed a human chain to reach both stranded hikers and pull them to safety.

The Electric Arms Race Has Gone Too Far: Tim Shuff questions the growing use of electric motors in outdoor adventure. He argues that the speed and convenience they create undermine traditionally self-propelled activities like paddling and cycling. Today, you can even buy battery-powered SUPs.

While recognizing that electric assistance can make outdoor sports more accessible, he thinks that its wider use will change wilderness areas, reshape trails, and create pressure to build increasingly powerful equipment.

Jan Spicka descends from Huascarán Sur. Photo: Matej Zak

A Czech Alpinist Describes How He Survived a Nightmare on Peru’s Highest Mountain: Climbing Peru’s Huascarán Sur turned into a tragedy for a group of five friends last July. Czech alpinist Jan Spicka describes how he and partner Matej Zak reached the 6,768m summit before he plunged into a hidden crevasse on the descent.

Matej’s quick reactions saved his life, but their three friends, Saul Mendoza, Chicho Ugarte, and Arti Salas, disappeared while attempting to descend by a different route. With no official mountain rescue in Peru, friends and volunteers pitched in. After 24 hours in the freezing conditions, they knew it was unlikely they would see their friends again. GPS tracks and later searches suggest they had fallen through a collapsing snow bridge.

He Felt Stuck in New York. So He Started Walking to California: Feeling trapped by unemployment and a lack of opportunities in New York, 29-year-old Chantz Rivera began walking across America. He set off with a tent, sleeping bag, and some supplies in a shopping cart. Over 149 days, he walked to California, pushing his cart 32 to 40km a day along highways and through small towns, documenting his progress for his growing number of online followers.

This Mountain Race Makes Runners Carry 60kg of Beer and Supplies Uphill: Slovakia’s annual Sherpa Rallye is one of the world’s unique mountain races. Competitors have to haul heavy loads of supplies up a 5.7km rocky trail to a remote hut in the High Tatras, nearly 2,000m above sea level.

Men carry 60kg, including a 30-liter keg of beer, while women carry 20kg, with the loads secured to wooden frames. The race was started in 1985 by mountain porter Viktor Beranek. The event brings together the region’s nosiči (traditional mountain porters) and also helps stock the remote hut ahead of winter.

A Hiker Slid Hundreds of Meters Down Mount Timpanogos: A female hiker was seriously injured after slipping off the trail near the summit of Mount Timpanogos in Utah. She slid several hundred feet down the steep mountainside before landing on a narrow cliff ledge. Newly released helicopter footage shows her being hoisted to safety. She was taken to hospital and is reportedly recovering.

We Need to Move Past the ‘Shut Up and Die Like a Man’ Mentality in the Mountains: Climber and paraglider Will Gadd reflects on the psychological impact of mountain accidents. Climbing culture often glorifies the dangers people face in the mountains while discouraging them from admitting they are struggling.

Gadd discusses the deaths of two close friends and challenges the underlying expectation that climbers should simply suck it up and carry on. After decades in the mountains, he has seen that the consequences of an accident extend far beyond the immediate incident, affecting survivors and their families and friends for years.

Two Hikers Dead in Bear Attack in British Columbia: Two hikers, identified as 48-year-old Jennifer Balcarras and her 20-year-old daughter Selah Reed, have died in a suspected grizzly attack near the Snooka West Trail in Bella Coola, British Columbia. Their bodies were found during a search after the pair were reported missing by their family.

This comes just a year after a separate grizzly attack in the same area severely injured four people, including three children. The trail remains closed as authorities continue their investigation.

Rebecca McPhee is a freelance writer for ExplorersWeb.

Rebecca has been writing about open water sports, adventure travel, and marine science for three years. Prior to that, Rebecca worked as an Editorial Assistant at Taylor and Francis, and a Wildlife Officer for ORCA.

Based in the UK Rebecca is a science teacher and volunteers for a number of marine charities. She enjoys open water swimming, hiking, diving, and traveling.

#8438. Antarctica 2026-2027: Expedition Roundup

Here are the Antarctic expeditions for 2026-27. There will be more, as a few still scramble to raise the money or choose to keep their plans to themselves until the last minute. Because of the long charter flights, an expedition to Antarctica, even a “tourist” Last Degree trip, costs close to $100,000 — more, if you want to do something unusual. These are the journeys announced so far. Skiers hit the ice later this month.

Hercules Inlet is the common starting point for South Pole skiers. Skiers need to travel around 1,130km, averaging around 20km per day.

Brit Nathan Lumb originally planned a fully unsupported round-trip to the South Pole but scaled back due to “expedition logistics and funding realities.” He will now ski solo and unsupported from Hercules Inlet to the South Pole. Lumb previously completed a crossing of Norway’s Finnmarksvidda with guiding company Ousland Colliard.

Indian Neeraj Choudhary also trained with Ousland Colliard, crossing the Greenland Ice Sheet in 2024. He hopes to become the second Indian to ski solo and unsupported to the South Pole, following Satish Gogineni, who became the first in 2024-2025.

Satish Gogineni at the South Pole in 2025. Photo: Satish Gogineni

American Lucas Czarnecki will also ski solo and unsupported and claims he will livestream every moment of his journey: “What sets South Signal [his expedition name] apart from other polar expeditions is my commitment to bringing you along for every moment of this extreme adventure. Each day, I will be live-streaming my experience directly from the ice, sharing the raw reality of life on the polar plateau.”

Another American, Meghan Buchanan, will also tackle the route. She plans to start early, leaving for Antarctica on November 12.

Finally, German Dirk Langer will also attempt the route unsupported.

A Berkner Island start means a few extra kilometers, with skiers required to cover around 1,400km to the South Pole.

Max Worsley of the UK and Martin Nesse of Norway also plan to start in early November, but from the edge of Berkner Island. Max is the son of Henry Worsley, who died following an Antarctic expedition in 2015-2016. Max intends to follow much of his father’s final route.

At least two expeditions will begin at the Messner start, 911km from the South Pole. James Garnett of the UK and Norwegian Marius Rehn will each ski solo and unsupported.

Satish Gogineni will be back in Antarctica this year, hoping to build upon his Hercules Inlet experience. Gogineni’s 2026 plan is more ambitious: a solo, 2,000km journey from the outer edge of the Filchner–Ronne Ice Shelf to the inner edge of the Ross Ice Shelf via the South Pole. Gogineni is billing this as a “world-first human achievement” and the “longest solo traverse ever completed on the Antarctic continent.”

If successful, his route would indeed be longer than the truncated crossings of Colin O’Brady and Lou Rudd in 2018. However, his distance would still be well short of Borge Ousland’s 1997 expedition. Ousland became the first person to cross Antarctica solo and unsupported, though he occasionally used a rudimentary handheld kite known as a ski sail. Ousland skied for 65 days and covered 2,845km. At the time, sails were not considered “support.”

Last year, O’Brady tried a significantly longer expedition than his 2018 effort, attempting to ski from the edge of the Ross Ice Shelf to the tip of Berkner Island on the edge of the Ronne Ice Shelf, a total distance of over 2,800km. He eventually ended his crossing attempt after 100 days, with roughly 40% of his total distance remaining.

A map by PECS (the Polar Expeditions Classification Scheme) showing previous solo Antarctic crossing routes. Photo: PECS

Gogineni’s main challenge might be the weight of his sled. With so much weight spread over two sleds, O’Brady was forced to double carry in soft snow on the Ross Ice Shelf last year. A double carry involved shuttling half of his gear forward, then returning to pick up the rest. This meant he had to cover 15km for every 5km of progress. Gogineni says his sled will weigh 181kg.

Mardi Philips, Ida Olsson, and Nia Owens are calling themselves the Antarctic Valkyries and aim to become the “first all-female team to reach the South Pole of Inaccessibility.” They will start at Thorshammer Station (a spot near the Russian Novolazarevskaya Station in Queen Maud Land) and plan to kite-ski 2,500km, first aiming for the Pole of Inaccessibility (the middle of the Antarctic continent), and then continuing to their finish point at the South Pole.

The Antarctic Valkyries proposed route. Photo: Antarctic Valkyries

A Spanish scientific expedition will cross Antarctica by a new route in a unique wind-powered vehicle.

Part of the wind-sled involves a series of tents. Photo: La Vanguardia

Their “wind-sled ” is 15m long and consists of four modules. It is propelled using 20 kites of various sizes. The largest kite measures 150m² and the smallest 5m². The four-man team plans a 3,400km, 85-day journey from near Novolazarevskaya Station to the Italian research station Concordia, known as Dome C, on the Antarctic Plateau.

The wind-sled team’s proposed route. The green return route is planned for a future expedition. Photo: La Vanguardia

Meanwhile, three Argentine kayakers are preparing to paddle roughly 900km along the western coast of the Antarctic Peninsula, from Argentina’s Esperanza Base to San Martín Base. Diego Linares, Juan Tomaselli, and Javier Siede will start their journey in January and plan to spend 30 to 40 days on the water.

Martin Walsh is a writer and editor for ExplorersWeb.

Martin spent most of the last 20 years backpacking the world on a shoestring budget. Whether it was hitchhiking through Syria, getting strangled in Kyrgyzstan, touring Cambodia’s medical facilities with an exceedingly painful giant venomous centipede bite, chewing khat in Ethiopia, or narrowly avoiding various toilet-related accidents in rural China, so far, Martin has just about survived his decision-making.

Based in Da Lat, Vietnam, Martin can be found in the jungle trying to avoid leeches while chasing monkeys.

#8437. Early Amazon Black Friday Deals on Trail Cameras from Moultrie, Tactacam, SpyPoint, and More

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 18, 2026 6:18 PM EST

Trail cameras are an invaluable source of intel for deer hunting these days, and they’re also just plain fun. The tech powering modern trail cameras is evolving at a rapid clip, and there’s no excuse for running shoddy cameras theses days. #8434. A Risky Summit Push on Everest

It’s now or never for the Russian team on the West Ridge of Everest. Andrey Vasiliev, Sergey Kondrashkin, and Vitaly Shipilov have set off for the summit without oxygen, hoping the weather will hold. They are completely alone on the mountain, and the push is full of risk.

Everest is still loaded with fresh snow after two cyclones, and there are only three of them to break trail and find the best route above 7,500m. That was their highest point during their earlier venture in October.  In addition, the wind may rise dramatically on Thursday, Vitor Baia, a Portuguese high-altitude meteorologist, told ExplorersWeb.

“It looks like we have a weather window of about a week, followed by a significant intensification of the wind,” leader Andrei Vasiliev told Anna Piunova of Mountain Club.

The question is exactly when the wind will increase, and whether the climbers will have enough time to summit and descend to a safer altitude.

“They have three days of weak wind, until Wednesday [November 11], but then the wind will increase sharply,” said Baia.

He says the best summit day will be Tuesday, with clear skies and weak winds. “Then they could use Wednesday to descend,” he added. “[That day will have] some clouds but still acceptable wind speeds.”

The situation will worsen remarkably on Thursday, when the wind will shift from the west to the northwest and increase dramatically to 50 kph at 8,000m and 60kph at the summit.

In addition, avalanche hazards are still high due to the large amounts of snow after the latest storm two weeks ago. Finally, as winter approaches, it’s getting colder every day, the climbers reported.

After their first trip up ended in a tactical retreat as the cyclone hit, they retreated to Kathmandu to recover from some health issues. Now they are fit again and ready for a second try. They returned to Base Camp last Thursday. Not surprisingly, they found it covered in a thick new layer of snow. Breaking trail, they sank at some points up to their waists, Vasiliev reported.

“Our high-altitude boots were in a cache two hours ahead of us, so we used duct tape to seal our pant cuffs to our trekking boots, trying to keep the snow out,” Kondrashkin reported. They eventually reached the cache and continued up in an exhausting 14-hour-long effort to a second cache (their Camp 1), where they had left their tent and sleeping bags.

They were near an avalanche-prone area and several snow slides swept nearby, prompting them to wait a day before continuing. Meanwhile, Shipilov showed signs of food poisoning. Kodrashkin recalls that Saturday morning started with the following conversation:

“Vitalya, how are you feeling? Are we heading up?”

That answer needs some clarification. A professional like Vitaly Viktorovich [Shipilov] really only has two modes: “We’re going” and “I’m dying.”

In fact, Shipilov had a fever and acute pain in the lower back, so the team headed down to their base in Lobuche. As this was going on, the snow had time to sluff off from the previously avalanche-prone area.

After just one night of rest, Vasiliev confirmed they were going up again. If all went according to plan, they will spend tonight back in their tent above the Lho La. Unfortunately, the climbers have not shared their tracker showing their current location and progress. On their previous trip up, they reached some 7,500m on Everest’s West Shoulder, near the beginning of the West Ridge and the potential detour to the Hornbein Couloir.

Everest’s North Face from the base of the West Ridge. Photo: Sergey Kondrashkin

Prior to that, Angela Benavides spent time at/worked at a number of local and international media. She is also experienced in outdoor-sport consultancy for sponsoring corporations, press manager and communication executive, and a published author.
