#8433. Winners of the Banff Mountain Film Festival Announced

The annual Banff Centre Mountain Festival gathers mountain luminaries from across the world at the Banff Centre for Arts and Creativity in Alberta. There, a celebration of mountain culture recognizes the best outdoor films and books of the past year. The climax of the festival took place this past weekend, and the winners of the 51st annual Banff Mountain Film Competition have now been announced.

The Grand Prize went to Listen, directed by Taliesin Black-Brown. In this short documentary, sound engineer Hank Lentfer records the sounds of nature around his Alaska home, while caring for his mother with dementia. As Lentfer documents the degradation of the natural world, we witness the slow loss of his mother. Drawing together these two threads, the film explores connection despite, and through, loss, using sound.

Rose Bush, an Oscar Award-winning director of photography serving on the competition’s jury, called Listen “a film that engages human-caused environmental devastation — the existential dilemma of our time — at its nucleus with overwhelming love and hope.”

The film also won the Grand Jury Prize at the Tribeca Film Festival.

In addition to a grand prize, the festival honors films in several categories. The winning film in the Adventure category was The Navigator, a feature-length work from director Jordan Manley. The film follows Nainoa Thompson, who was the first Native Hawaiian in six centuries to make the journey from Hawaii to Tahiti in a traditional voyaging canoe. Now, almost five decades later, Thompson and his crew make the journey again, using only traditional navigation techniques.

The best film in the Mountain Culture category was Land of Canaan, produced and directed by Maggie Lemere. Lemere’s film follows a group of Palestinian olive farmers, who work to protect their ancient olive groves amid the destruction by Israel. The film “illuminated the vital relationship of courage, vision, hope, and traditional ecological knowledge,” juror Rose Bush said, “in service of the essential value of sovereignty via mountain culture.”

In the Environment category, director James Dawson’s winning comedic documentary Derek vs Derek follows two Devon farmers, both named Derek. One Derek is an ardent dairy farmer while his environmentalist neighbor begins rewilding his land. A feud naturally ensues.

The Arc’teryx-sponsored Best Climbing Film was Zindabad, directed by Tom Fish. Fish follows Pakistani climber Mueez Ud Din into the Karakoram, alongside his uncle Javed Ali and French climber Mathieu Maynadier. Ud Din explores a remote, unknown glacier system, while the film examines what it means to be a Pakistani climber among peaks long claimed by international outsiders.

Directed by Alexis Berg, the Mountain Sports winner, Roots, follows trail runner Ruth Croft as she examines her future as an elite ultra-trail runner after an injury. Roots is “about an inner journey of healing and acceptance, rooted in the essential joy of running outdoors,” explained juror David Arnaud.

The Snow Sports winner centers on American skier Jessie Diggins. Diggins is the most decorated American cross-country skier ever, but Threshold, directed by Torsten Brinkema, reveals the deep personal struggles behind that success. Juror Beki Henderson noted that “the jury particularly commends Jessie Diggins for her extraordinary courage and honesty in sharing her story.”

There were more mountain films this year than you can even imagine. With 610 film submissions and 83 finalists, it’s no wonder that there are still more category winners.

Best short film winner Hello, Beautiful follows climber Alannah Yip as she struggles with identity, training for the Olympics while enduring medical hair loss. Director Darcy Hennessey created a film which “reminds us that personal transformation does not always come from pushing harder,” juror Kerry Stauffer explained.

For best feature film, the competition announced Ceremony, directed and produced by Banchi Hanuse. The film tells the story of the disappearance of the ooligan, a small fish, from British Columbia’s Bella Coola River. Jurors praised the film’s use of watercolor animation, personal stories, and community broadcasts to explore the importance of a seemingly unimportant little fish.

Director Marco Tribelhorn’s 75 seconds won the Creative Excellence Award. The film documents free-skier Jacob Wester’s 75-second-long battle for survival while trapped in an avalanche.

“A compelling visual treatment brings to life an experience that is impossible to imagine,” said Kerry Stauffer.

Last but not least, the Audience Choice Award went to Only Music // Snake River. Director Justin Fountain’s documentary follows a band that ditches the tour bus for a journey down the Snake River, seeking connection with strangers.

Tickets for the international film festival are now available. We have also covered the recently announced Banff Mountain Book Award winners.

At the present moment he can most likely be found perusing records of shipboard crime and punishment during the Age of Sail, or failing that, writing historical fiction horror stories. He lives in Dublin and hates the sun.

#8430. A Routine Invasive-Species Survey in Lake Ontario Turned Up Something Much Stranger

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 18, 2026 7:24 PM EST

The hottest fishing news, tips, and tactics

Routine research in the Great Lakes system morphed into an unusual catch this fall.

A U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service survey crew caught a rare yellow burbot in October, according to a statement last week. The fish came from Oswego Harbor, New York, which is on the southeastern side of Lake Ontario. It’s a significant find for more than just its color variation.

“The finding is notable because burbot populations in the lake are extremely low,” the agency wrote on Nov. 10. “The species typically inhabit deeper, cooler waters than those found within the harbor.”

Running the full length of an arm, the off-colored native burbot had xanthochromism, a genetic mutation that causes an animal’s pigment to be excessively yellow or orange. In fish, it displays on their scales and fins, but it can also show up on furred animals. It’s less common than albinism in the wild, but it’s also not unheard of.

A 6.6-foot orange-Creamsicle nurse shark turned up with the same condition, plus albinism, in 2024 in Costa Rica, and the first case of xanthochromism in a sunset-colored snook in the Gulf was documented in 2024.

The variation might be appealing to fishermen, but it’s less fun for the fish themselves. Their bold coloring reduces their ability to hide within their habitat, which means they’re easy prey and often have shorter lifespans.

The deadly-bright hues look similar to banana trout, but that’s a different scenario.

Palomino trout, or bananas, are popular stockers for pond practice. They’re also covered in shades ranging from yellow to orange, but they’re bred that way on purpose. Hatcheries breed them from a mutant rainbow trout strain that lacks the dot pattern but keeps the bow’s notorious red lateral stripe. Despite the golden color, they aren’t related to California golden trout. The strain traces to a single yellow-mottled rainbow fingerling found at a West Virginia hatchery in 1955 and was introduced to anglers in 1963.

Read Next: These Are the Biggest North American Catfish of All Time

As for the yellow burbot in Oswego, it wasn’t released. Its body condition was in decline, so the fish was sent to Cornell University College of Veterinary Medicine to be studied. It’s one of many fish analyzed during the region’s Aquatic Invasive Species Survey. The goal of the survey is early detection of a problem throughout the many bodies of water in the Great Lakes system, including harbors and rivers. Researchers use electrofishing, fyke nets, and gill nets to collect specimens for the survey.

Kris Millgate is a multimedia journalist based in eastern Idaho. She’s a fly fisher, a bird hunter and an avid seeker of awkward moments. Read and watch more of her work at tightlinemedia.com

#8426. A 3,300Km Walk Across Japan in Winter

Japanese publisher Katsunari Tanaka has set himself the challenge of hauling a handcart 3,345km from Japan’s northernmost tip to the southern edge of Okinawa in winter.

Tanaka, 49, set off from Cape Sōya in Hokkaido on October 7 and is heading south to Cape Hedo in Okinawa. He hopes to complete the traverse in 100 days over five months. As he will have to contend with some challenging winter conditions along the way, he has described it as “playing chase with winter.”

Rather than carrying everything on his back, he is pulling a handcart that weighs around 100kg. He also has a support team with a vehicle in case of emergencies. At some points, different members of his team have walked with him for a day or so. His daily target is around 35km, with rest days and organized stops built into the schedule.

The first few weeks have already brought strong headwinds, rain, and some steep climbs, making progress difficult at times. Temperatures have dropped close to freezing. At the end of October, he crossed a mountain pass between Yoichi and Iwanai. There, headwinds stirred up the gravel as he walked. Then the snow began. In the end, he took refuge with his support team until the weather improved.

Despite the conditions, Tanaka reached Hakodate Harbor on November 3, having covered 621km across Hokkaido. From Hakodate, he is crossing the Tsugaru Strait by ferry to Aomori, then will continue south along the Sanriku coast to Sendai.

From there, he follows the Pacific coast through Fukushima to Tokyo before joining the Tokaido route toward Osaka before heading west to Kyushu. A second ferry crossing brings him to Okinawa, where he will walk to his final destination, Cape Hedo.

Along a road in Hokkaido. Photo: Katsunari Tanaka/Instagram

This is not Tanaka’s first experience of traveling Japan with a handcart. Around a decade ago, he spent three years visiting all 47 prefectures, selling books directly to people he met along the way. Loading his publications onto a cart, he covered the country between 2013 and 2016, eventually selling 13,477 books. At the time, many people questioned what he was doing and whether books could really be sold on the street. But he says the experience shaped his approach to business and his belief in the power of personal connections.

Tanaka insists that he is not an adventurer. Instead, he is traveling the country as a “merchant.” This time, he is making the journey to support his mentor, Arthur Hollands, a Japanese evangelist known for undertaking lengthy walks while carrying a large wooden cross. Along the route, Tanaka plans to stop at 11 locations to give lectures. He welcomes people to join him for sections of the walk.

Rebecca McPhee is a freelance writer for ExplorersWeb.

Rebecca has been writing about open water sports, adventure travel, and marine science for three years. Prior to that, Rebecca worked as an Editorial Assistant at Taylor and Francis, and a Wildlife Officer for ORCA.

Based in the UK Rebecca is a science teacher and volunteers for a number of marine charities. She enjoys open water swimming, hiking, diving, and traveling.

#8425. The Burris Buck: How I Tagged the Biggest Mule Deer of All Time

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 19, 2026 9:30 AM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

This story, “Greatest Mule Deer of Them All,” appeared in the December 1975 issue of Outdoor Life. Burris’ buck remains, all these years later, the No. 1 typical mule deer buck ever taken by a hunter, according to the Boone and Crockett Club. The antlers are now owned by Cabela’s. Burris tagged the buck on Oct. 19, 1972 in Dolores County, Colorado.

I was walking the edge of a small canyon in Colorado’s San Juan National Forest when a rifle roared half a mile or so ahead of me. Till then I’d figured on stillhunting to a plateau covered with scattered oak brush 400 yards north of me. A clump of brush there would have been a fine place to take a stand.

When I heard the shot I decided to get to a vantage point on the plateau as fast as I could, so I took off at a dead run. I was almost on the plateau when I noticed movement in the oak brush and aspens on the other side of the canyon. I slammed to a stop and stared at the biggest deer I had ever seen. His body was enormous, and his rack seemed large enough for an elk.

He wasn’t running flat out, but I knew I had to shoot in a hurry. The range was about 250 yards. The buck was moving up the canyon and away from me when I found him in my Leupold 3X-to-9X scope, I put the crosshairs on his back and touched off my Sako .264. He went down in his tracks. My big-game handloads consist of 125-grain Nosler bullets backed with 66.5 grains of No. 4831 powder. It’s a very potent load.

Related: My First Buck Was the Biggest Typical Mule Deer Ever Taken with a Bow. I Almost Discarded the Antlers

I watched him for a while to make sure he wasn’t going to get up, and then I ran down a side canyon and up the other side of the big one to the fallen buck. When I ran my hands over the huge 18-point rack, I knew the antlers were very good. I was sure the deer would score high in the Boone and Crockett Club’s record book.

If someone could have told me that my 1969 Colorado buck wasn’t even good enough to make the minimum score for listing in the record book, I wouldn’t have believed him. That’s the way it turned out, but a still bigger deer was ranging the same stretch of country.

I’m 37. My wife and I live in Seguin, Texas, with our 17-year-old son and two daughters, aged 15 and eight. I’m a construction superintendent with an outfit that builds shopping centers, high-rise office buildings, and similar units.

Except for 3½ years spent in the U.S. Air Force, I’ve lived in Texas all my life. I began hunting when I was seven. My dad got me started and taught me to be a good hunter. I killed my first Texas whitetail when I was 13, and I’ve been nuts about deer hunting ever since. Though many of our Texas deer are so small we call them jackrabbits, we do have some large whitetails in the southern brush country. Some of those bucks field-dress at 200 pounds or a bit more, and some have exceptional antlers. Many years of hunting brush-country deer gave me trophy-hunting fever.

I have always wanted to hunt Dall or Stone sheep in Alaska or British Columbia, but I’ve never had the money or the time. I finally decided that the next-best thing was to stay with deer and hunt trophy antlers.

My interest in camping had a lot to do with that first mule-deer hunt in Colorado in 1969. My friends and I have several four-wheel-drive vehicles and an assortment of camping gear. Charlie Fuller of Floresville, and Jack Smith of San Antonio, do a lot of hunting with me. Jack is my age. He’s a carpenter. Charlie, a few years younger, has a fertilizer-application operation. Both like to hunt deer the way I do, and they like to camp. Early in 1969 we decided to hunt in the mountains where we could camp on public land and do without guides and outfitters. We call it a “poor-boy hunt.”

I did quite a bit of research on trophy mule deer before we chose the San Juan National Forest. From what I’d read in Outdoor Life and other outdoor magazines, I knew that many giant bucks were taken not too far from the Four Corners, where Utah, Arizona, New Mexico, and Colorado touch.

I also bought a copy of the 1964 edition of the Boone and Crockett Club’s book, Records of North American Big Game. Study of the section on mule-deer trophies backed up my contention that southwestern Colorado would be a good place to go. We knew there were better bets, such as the Jicarilla Apache Reservation in New Mexico, but we couldn’t get permits, and we wanted to go into country where we could camp on public land. We eventually drove to Cortez, Colorado, and hunted northeast of town.

The three of us all got bucks that first year, and my big buck had a rack that measured 41 inches across the outside spread. Each antler had “cheater” points extending horizontally from the outside curves of the main beams. Though the rack had a very unusual spread, the tines weren’t very long, and that’s why my trophy didn’t make the record book.

When I killed the deer, I didn’t know much about official scoring systems for big game, but I did know that where there was one grandote (a Mexican term we use for big bucks) there should be more. We didn’t need a better reason to head back into the same area. We’ve never been sorry.

The second year, I got a buck with a 33-inch outside spread. The third year, I was lucky enough to come up with another good deer. Every season I’ve hunted in those mountains, except one, I’ve taken a buck with an outside antler spread of 30 inches or better, and other members of my party have taken very good bucks too. Many hunters in the area kill deer with spreads as narrow as 12 inches. Every fall I pass up many deer to get the best one I can find.

Most of the men in our group hunt that way. We all love the mountains, and we like to hunt hard from dawn to dark. We don’t want to miss one minute of hunting on these trips. None of us will take a small buck unless time is running out on the last day.

I don’t want to imply that we’re trophy crazy. Much of our pleasure derives from the planning and preparation that goes into our hunts and the talkfests that go on for months after each trip. We make the most of camp life too. I’m usually the cook because I enjoy it. We come into camp each evening, warm up by our wood stove in the cooktent, swap stories about what happened during the day, and take our time over meals while we plan the next day’s hunt. Then we sack out early enough for a good rest so that we can do it all over again.

We go back into the Cortez area each year because it has proved itself, and because our camping gear is suited to that kind of country. We’re not equipped to backpack into remote areas or pack in with horses.

Our cooktent, which also serves as a community area, is a 14 x 16-foot wall tent. We have 9 x 12 Coleman sleeping tents. Everybody contributes something to our outfit. We found out during our first trip that it’s best to take plenty of blankets and raingear.

On the front end of our trailer is mounted a large box in which we pack all our food and some other items. If the weather is warm during our hunt, we have all our venison butchered, wrapped, and quick-frozen in Cortez. We pack the meat and dry ice in the box for our trip home. If the weather is cold, we hang our field-dressed deer from a game pole and then haul them home whole for butchering there. Either way, the venison we put in our freezers provides delicious meals far into the winter.

We make our trips from Texas to Colorado with two 4WD’s. The big unit is my three-quarter-ton Dodge Power Wagon equipped with a winch. Behind the Dodge we tow a Jeep on a 20-foot trailer. We usually ride in the wagon and pack the Jeep and the trailer with gear.

Our average cost per man totals about $155, and that includes Colorado’s nonresident deer license ($50). A big chunk of the rest of each man’s expenses goes for gas. We drive straight through from Texas. That takes 20 to 24 hours, depending on driving conditions. We use the Jeep each day to put men out in hunting areas, so we burn a lot of gas. We bring plenty of steaks and a normal supply of groceries such as beans, potatoes, flour, canned goods, breakfast mixes.

I don’t want to imply that we’re trophy crazy. Much of our pleasure derives from the planning and preparation that goes into our hunts and the talkfests that go on for months after each trip. We make the most of camp life too.

We plan to arrive in our hunting area the Thursday before the Saturday opener of the deer season, which is always in late October. It takes two days to set up camp, get organized, and do a little scouting. We figure on hunting three to five days, but we do not set up a hard-and-fast schedule.

As few as two men have gone to Colorado with me and as many as eight, but everything seems to go along smoothest with about four in the party. In 1972, the year I shot the world-record mule deer, there were four of us. Jack Smith went along, but Charlie Fuller couldn’t get away. Robbie Roe and Bruce Winters were my other partners. I knew them through my job, and we had done a lot of hunting together.

We drove up into Colorado and camped in the same spot I’d picked out four years earlier. I knew the hunting country fairly well by that time, and I had selected five specific areas that seemed to hold the most deer and the biggest bucks. They’re all laced with relatively thick oak brush, some aspens, and a few pines. Thick oak brush is the key to success. If you’ve got tangles of that stuff and lots of them, you’re going to find deer.

The oak-brush areas most attractive to deer run to broken terrain in the form of canyons and side canyons, plateaus, and mesas. The best technique for hunting that kind of terrain is a combination of stillhunting and standing. I like to stillhunt the plateaus and small canyons. I’ve learned that deer work the small finger canyons and get down into the very bottoms of them. They range back and forth through the canyons, often go up on the mesas and plateaus.

That’s why the combination of hunting techniques works so well. When I’m stillhunting I often spot deer ahead of me or across a canyon or draw. If I take a stand on a good vantage point I may have deer working toward me.

The problem is that deer may use one area today and vacate it tomorrow. So I choose an area and hunt it out. If deer are there, I stick with it; if they aren’t I move to the next area. That isn’t hard to do, because the San Juan country is mostly rolling terrain, and the altitude is only about 8,500 feet where we hunt.

Our first day of hunting in 1972 began in typical fashion. We checked the wind direction before dawn and decided on the areas each man would hunt. Then we piled into the Jeep and headed up a mountain two-track. I dropped Robbie off first and then Bruce. A few miles farther on, Jack got out of the vehicle. Dawn wasn’t much more than a dull-gray promise in the eastern sky. The black void above us was spitting drizzle.

“Robbie and Bruce will walk back to camp whenever they feel like it,” I told Jack. “You want me to pick you up at a set time, or do you want to hunt all day in the rain?”

“I’ll meet you right here at dark,” Jack answered.

I knew he’d say something like that. Jack likes to hunt as much as I do.

We all saw many deer that day, but all the bucks were small. None of us fired a shot.

But I came very close to filling my tag on the second day of the hunt. It drizzled again, and I just ambled along till 10 o’clock. Then I got real serious about my stillhunting. I don’t know why, but the really big bucks in that area seem to move mostly from 10 in the morning till about 1 in the afternoon.

About noon I spotted a deer in thick oak brush 200 yards ahead. We were both on a plateau. The animal was a big one, so I assumed he was a buck, and I started moving in on him. My stalking was quiet because everything was wet, but I couldn’t close the distance very fast in that thick brush, and the deer kept moving away from me as he browsed.

At one point I saw part of his rack, just enough to know the antlers were in the trophy class. The excitement ended when I tried to cut him off at the edge of the plateau. When I got there, he was gone. I’ll never know whether that buck sensed my presence or went off on a predetermined course.

When Jack and I got back to camp two bucks were on the game pole. Robbie had nailed a five-pointer with his 7-mm. Remington Magnum rifle equipped with a Leupold scope. Bruce’s deer was also a five-pointer, but the antlers were heavier. Bruce collected his venison with a Winchester Model 70 .30/06 rigged with a Leupold scope.

When we awoke the third morning, the dad-gummed drizzle was still with us. I suited up in my Browning raingear and filled my pockets with dried fruits and jerky. I knew it would be another long day before I could bake myself beside the wood stove in the cooktent. I dropped Jack off on the trail and then drove on and parked near the area I had hunted the day before.

A slight breeze was coming down off the mountain, so I decided to stillhunt into it and get farther back into rougher country. About 9 o’clock I started into a 400-yard-wide canyon and began working up toward one rim. I was about three-quarters of the way to the top when I spotted two bucks feeding in a clearing 1,000 yards away in the canyon. I stopped and watched them for several minutes because I was astonished that I could see their racks from such a great distance with the naked eye.

Then I looked at them through my Bausch & Lomb 7 x 35 binoculars. Both were definitely in the trophy class. It was fairly late in our hunt, so I decided it was best to cash in on one of them. I’d make the stalk and decide which rack was best when I got within range.

I was about three-quarters of the way to the top when I spotted two bucks feeding in a clearing 1,000 yards away in the canyon. I stopped and watched them for several minutes because I was astonished that I could see their racks from such a great distance with the naked eye.

I eased on through the oak brush for about 200 yards and busted out a doe. She pushed on ahead of me. I didn’t think she would spook the bucks, but I watched them for several minutes to make sure. They continued feeding away from me up the slope. I could keep them in sight at this point because they were still in a clearing.

I eased back into some cover and stalked ahead for about 45 minutes while the drizzle came on stronger and stronger. During that time I didn’t see the bucks, because they had moved into a patch of brush, but I was pretty sure they were still nearby. I would have seen them if they had moved out on that slope. There was a 75-yard-wide opening at the top of the canyon, and I would have seen or heard them if they had run back toward me or along the canyon bottom.

An additional 30 minutes passed before I decided I couldn’t be more than 200 yards from the deer, but I still couldn’t see them — the patch of brush was up to 10 feet tall. Then I busted out a doe again. She crashed along toward the bucks. I figured they’d spook, so I took off at a dead run for an opening 20 yards ahead of me. If I was going to do any good, I had to shoot in a hurry.

Just as I broke into the opening, three deer ran into view. Two were the bucks I’d been stalking; the third was still bigger. He was lagging behind, and even in the split-second I had to shoot, I knew he had the best rack by far.

My handloaded Nosler 125-grain bullet flew 300 yards, ripped into the buck’s heart-lung area, and blew up. He stumbled, hit the ground, and lay still.

When I examined the antlers — six unbelievably massive tines on the right side and five on the left — I knew I had killed the finest trophy buck I’d ever seen, but I had no idea that he was the best typical mule deer ever taken. The enormous body of the deer concerned me most at the moment.

Normally we can drive a vehicle close to our kills and then drag the deer out to the 4WD. But I was way back in rough country, and we couldn’t approach with a vehicle. It would have been impossible for me to drag that deer very far by myself.

I decided to hike out to the Jeep and drive back to camp for help. Since Robbie and Bruce had already killed their bucks, the odds were they would be in camp. They were. We went back to my deer, dressed and quartered it, and packed the quarters and the head out to the vehicle. When we returned to camp we found Jack waiting for us. He needed some help with a heavy-antlered five-pointer he’d killed with his .270 Weatherby Magnum. By the time we got that buck out, the day and the hunt were finished.

When I got home I took my buck’s head and cape to taxidermist Ed Schlier in San Antonio for mounting. He’s an official measurer for the Boone and Crockett Club, so we rough-scored the green antlers. Our preliminary figures indicated my buck would be among the top 10 listed in the typical-mule-deer category. That was exhilarating, but I didn’t think much more about it till one night several months later when my phone rang at 1 a.m.

“Doug,” Ed blurted out, “I think your buck may be the best typical mule deer ever taken. I scored the antlers this evening, and I couldn’t believe it when I came up with a total score of two-hundred twenty-six! That’s nine better than the previous world-record typical mule deer. I’ve been double-checking my figures for hours, but they always come out the same. If they hold up with Boone and Crockett officials, you’ve got a new world record. I figured that news was worth waking you up.”

It sure was, but it was just the beginning.

The North American Big Game Award Program — jointly sponsored by the Boone and Crockett Club and the National Rifle Association — requires that any big-game trophy likely to win an award must be rescored by a panel of judges. Accordingly, I sent the mounted head off to the program headquarters in Washington, DC.

In February 1974 the program coordinator phoned me and invited me to attend the Fifteenth North American Big Game Awards meeting on March 24 in Atlanta, Georgia. I guessed my buck had proven good enough to win some kind of award, but I was too busy to go to Atlanta.

That was my position till I received several more calls from other program officials. They told me that if I missed the awards meeting, I would very much regret it later, so I scraped together some cash, cancelled several appointments, and flew to Atlanta.

At the banquet I was presented with the award for the best typical mule deer taken during the most recent three-year award period. I also received official recognition for taking a new world record. In addition I won the Sagamore Hill Award, which is given only to hunters whose outstanding trophies are of great distinction. This award is the highest given by the Boone and Crockett Club. Only one may be given during any big-game-award period, and only 10 have been presented during the club’s history.

I was stunned. When I think about it, even today, I’m still stunned.

Former shooting editor Jim Carmichel wrote a short sidebar about his predictions for the next record-book mule deer.

Bagging a trophy mule deer hinges on four key factors: 1) luck, 2) hunting skill, 3) a firm determination to pass up lesser deer, 4) a carefully conducted survey of the areas likely to produce big mule-deer bucks at a predetermined point in time. The last three are more or less under the control of the hunter, but the fourth requires a basic understanding of trophy development.

A look at the Boone and Crockett Club’s record book shows that a large percentage of trophy mule deer have consistently been taken from certain fairly well-defined regions. Equally significant is that the trophy heads from these regions have been taken within specific time periods. During the early 1960’s, for example, a good number of high-ranking bucks were taken at the Jicarilla Apache Reservation in New Mexico. It’s no secret that certain areas tend to provide the natural requirements (such as a rich mineral diet) necessary for the development of big antlers, and that certain genetic strains of deer tend to produce big racks. Arizona’s Kaibab area, to give but one example, is one of the regions well known for its ability to grow large-antlered mule deer. However, this doesn’t mean that these areas always abound with record-busting bucks. This is where the vital time element enters the picture.

Read Next: The Story of the Allison Buck, and the Best Opening Morning in Ohio History

If a big-buck-producing area is allowed to rest for a few seasons, with little or no hunting pressure, the bucks grow old and develop larger and larger antlers. Then word gets around about all the big bucks to be found there, and hunting pressure increases. In three or four years all the big trophy bucks have been killed off. When trophies become hard to find, the hunting pressure drops off, and the cycle begins again.

The smart trophy hunter keeps track of the best areas and keeps a discerning eye on the hunting pressure in these places. The idea, of course, is to get there a season or two ahead of the crowd.

#8422. First Ski Descent of Meru Central

Tiphaine Duperier and Boris Langenstein of France have notched another Himalayan first ski descent, this time down the southwest face of Meru Central (6,310m) in India’s Garhwal Himalaya. The skiers are still surprised by how lucky they were.

Duperier admitted they started the expedition with low expectations after several projects earlier in the fall all failed.

“Snow conditions had been unforgiving,” she wrote. “We figured there was only a 3-in-10 chance of skiing the southwest face of Meru.”

But when they got there, the mountain was, they added, “generous” with them.

The three-peaked Meru massif, located in the Gangotri Glacier region of the Garhwal Himalaya, lies between Shivling and Thalay Sagar. It has three peaks: 6,660m Meru South, 6,310m Meru Central, and 6,450m Meru North. The ski descent line is in purple. Photo: Duperier/Langenstein

“We spent six days of reconnaissance and three [days] climbing the peak, [thanks to] an unprecedented stroke of luck in finding a way through the icefall and perfect snow in the upper section,” she explained.

The icefall on Meru Central. Photo: Boris Langenstein

The icefall at the base of the peak was indeed one of the most complex passages. The pair climbed a 15m pitch of WI4 and left a safety rope, which they also used to rappel down after the ski descent, Duperier told PlanetMountain.

Otherwise, the pair of regular expedition and skiing partners climbed alpine-style and pretty fast, reaching the summit in less than eight hours. According to their research, they completed the second-ever ascent of the southwest face of Meru, after an Indian team in 2023.

At the summit on Meru Central. Photo: Duperier/Langenstein

The skiers estimate they covered 1,500m on their descent, with some sections reaching 50 °. Snow conditions were good overall, hard but with a good grip.

“We skied like little monkeys on the wires in Rishikesh,” Duperier posted on social media. “It was just incredible, truly beautiful!”

While the climbers rappelled down the icefall onto the glacier below, the ski descent itself had no rappels or interruptions.

Duperier barely had time to return home to France to pick up a Piolet d’Or for the first alpine-style ascent of the Schell Route on the Rupal Face of Nanga Parbat, followed by the first ski descent from the 8,125m summit.

Tiphaine Duperier at the Piolets d’Or ceremony. Photo: Piotr Drozdz

Angela Benavides graduated university in journalism and specializes in high-altitude mountaineering and expedition news. She has been writing about climbing and mountaineering, adventure and outdoor sports for 20+ years.

Prior to that, Angela Benavides spent time at/worked at a number of local and international media. She is also experienced in outdoor-sport consultancy for sponsoring corporations, press manager and communication executive, and a published author.

#8418. Oregon (Yes, Oregon) Is the Best Hunting and Fishing State in America

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 19, 2026 11:30 AM EST

The hottest fishing news, tips, and tactics

Legs shortened on the tripod shooting sticks, rifle secure in the saddle, I eased the setup onto a small rise in front of me. As I crawled into position, a buck materialized in the scope. It had no idea I was there.

A quick range reading followed by another confirmed the distance, 236 yards. The evening air was dead calm.

When I twisted the scope’s throw lever to 18X, the whitetail filled the field of view. The shot was simple: one bullet from a Browning 6.8 Western dropped the deer, and my 2026 season was off to a good start.

From the oak-studded knob on which I shot, I could see the North Umpqua River meandering through the valley below. It was on this river that I caught my first summer steelhead, at age 4, in 1968. In 1986 I shot my first wild turkey from the same area. That hunt sparked a 40-year passion chasing these grand birds.

As I walked up on the high-racked buck, the river’s unique beauty was captivating. I wasn’t looking at just any whitetail, but a prized Columbia whitetail. Here we have the only huntable population of these special deer in the world. I was exactly one hour and 20 minutes from my home in Western Oregon.

Almost 50 years to the day prior, I shot my very first deer — a Columbia blacktail. That buck came less than 15 miles from my home. Over the decades I’ve shot some dandy blacktails and seen bigger ones that outsmarted me. I rank consistently killing a mature trophy Columbia blacktail as the most challenging of all big game hunting in North America. The habitat they call home is vast, varied and as dense as a tropical rainforest in places.

Less than a week after punching the whitetail tag I was in Oregon’s Coast Range. I saw seven bears in four days before shooting a mature, sleek-coated beauty. My wife and I love bear meat; she even renders the fat for cooking. It’s great this time of year.

In the spring I hunted bears in the same area of the Coast Range. I passed 27 bears in seven days before pulling the trigger on a nice bruin.

Earlier this month, friends and I got serious about duck hunting public land on nearby rivers, as we always do this time of year. The first two hunts found us targeting wood ducks. I shot seven-bird limits of the gorgeous little ducks each time out. The following trip was a mix of mallards and woodies, and after that, an all-mallard limit. Excellent eating, all of them.

One morning was extra special, as I achieved something I’d been trying to pull off for three seasons. The morning was foggy and wood ducks aggressively poured into the small spread of decoys. Shots were close. I took seven wood ducks along with a pair of honkers. While driving home with the double limit I got a text from a nearby landowner. A big flock of turkeys was back on his property. He asked if I’d come shoot some, as they were becoming a nuisance.

In recent years Oregon created a special hunt for problematic fall and winter turkeys. It’s called a beardless turkey permit and with it you can shoot three turkeys without visible beards, on each of the three available permits that are purchased over the counter. It’s a depredation hunt that was put in place to deter large flocks of fall turkeys from congregating on private property, causing damage and leaving messy gifts that are a health concern to pets and livestock. Since funding to trap and relocate turkeys was cut in the state, turkey numbers have exploded in many counties. It’s common to see multiple flocks a day of 100 or more turkeys, all fall and winter long, in several places around Oregon.

I hurried home, grabbed Echo, my pudelpointer, and headed out for the turkeys. Echo is almost 13 years old. She’s hunted fall turkeys every year of her life. Oregon is one of a handful of Western states that allows the use of dogs for fall turkey hunting. It’s one of my favorite hunts of the year and Echo loves it. For Kona, my 10-year-old male pudelpointer who I lost to an intestinal torsion this past September, this was his favorite hunt of all. It was hard heading afield without him, for the first time.

But Echo didn’t skip a beat. After some searching we finally located the flock of turkeys. There were 46 of them, all hens, jakes, and jennies. They fed on the edge of a grassy meadow, heading uphill toward a patch of Douglas fir timber. When the time was right, I sent Echo. She ran at the flock as fast as her aging legs could carry her. She’d done this many times in her life. She knew the drill. If all the birds flushed, I’d call her back, we’d sneak into the timber, set up and call the flock back in. That didn’t happen.

This time Echo caught the turkeys by surprise. A few flew into the trees but most burrowed into thick blackberries and tall, green ferns. Echo struck the perfect point and held it until I arrived. Turkeys often hold tight as quail. When I got to Echo, a jake made a run for it. It didn’t make it far. At the shot, the rest of the flock scurried, some running, some taking wing. I shot another bird on the run, then dropped one more from the air. Echo couldn’t gather them fast enough. Finally, my bird trifecta was completed.

We ate birds for dinner that night, and Tiffany whipped up multiple dishes for a big church potluck the following day. Everyone enjoyed the local bounty, and there was an extra sense of pride among the elderly men who used to hunt.

I’ve been fortunate to hunt much of the country, and even lived in Alaska for a stint in the 1990s. When it comes to hunting and fishing, I’ve found no other state that matches what Oregon has to offer. It boasts hunting opportunities for 12 big game species, including four subspecies of deer. It has the best turkey hunting I’ve seen, with 14 tags available a year, including three spring toms. Thirty-three of the 41 recognized waterfowl species can be hunted here, along with bonus Eurasian wigeon which are becoming more frequent. Wigeon are my favorite duck to hunt and they thrive in the valleys and coast.

If you’re into predator hunting, coyotes and bobcats abound statewide. Mountain lions are now classified as big game in Oregon. In the past few years I’ve called in eight cougars, shooting the last one at 12 paces, using a shotgun. Last year I caught 11 different lions on trail cameras in one morning, all within 25 miles of my home–seven were within two miles. Cougars are thick, I wish we could hunt them with hounds as numbers are out of control. But some of our game laws make zero sense.

Most recently Oregon made national news for the infamous Oregon Initiative Petition 28. IP 28 didn’t even appear on the November ’26 ballot. Yet, just about every blurb on the topic for the past year made it appear certain Oregon was going to lose all hunting and fishing privileges. Much of the press and pursuers of clickbait chastised Oregonians, ignorant of the full story and who the players were. In the fine print, IP 28 was absurd not only to hunters and anglers, but cattle ranchers, farmers, pet owners and more folks in our state.

I was stunned at the number of fellow outdoorsmen who threw Oregon sportsmen under the bus, telling them to move from the state. Oregon is not the problem. Our issue is that the state is largely governed by people in three cities. The hunting and fishing population outside those city limits is large, and the many animals thriving in the game-rich state know no such boundaries nor are they aware of the people who set the rules. One hunting or fishing trip to our great state will reveal how special it truly is.

For the past 26 years I’ve made a living as a full-time writer and wildlife photographer. For 14 of those years I hosted hunting and fishing TV shows. I’ve written more than 4,200 magazine articles and 17 books with more in the works. I couldn’t have done it if not for living in Western Oregon, in the little town of Walterville — named after people on my wife’s side of the family.

Minutes from my home there’s something to hunt or fish for every day of the year. I live on the McKenzie River, a tributary of the famed Willamette River. I caught my first spring chinook salmon from the McKenzie in the 1960s. Over the decades, it, along with the Willamette River, have produced thousands of springers and loads of summer steelhead and rainbow trout for my family. Growing up fishing these rivers  spoiled me.

The Willamette River is big, wide, and challenging to fish in places. Downstream from where the Willamette flows into the mighty Columbia River, you’ll find some of the world’s best salmon and sturgeon fishing.

The McKenzie River is a blue ribbon rated trout stream, one of the best in the country. Growing up, word traveled fast when movie stars were on the water, most presenting dry flies to try and entice stunning redsides.

A few miles from my home, Theodore Roosevelt loved to fish for rainbow trout. I know where he stayed and the holes he liked to fish. Rumor has it–from some of my wife’s relatives who homesteaded the McKenzie River Valley in the mid-1800s–it was among Roosevelt’s favorite rivers to fish, both for the trout and the sheer beauty the region offered. Roosevelt would be happy to know that the place he so loved is now teeming with wildlife, including elk that carry his name.

In fourth grade I began trapping. My parents took turns taking me to check traps before school. For sets close to home, I’d ride my bike. Beaver, raccoon, coyote, red fox and bobcat were some of the target species. I skinned the catch after school. This continued into my freshman year of high school, until sports consumed my life. Trapping greatly educated me about the land and the animals that live there. It also allowed me to make some money so I could fish in the summer months, rather than work.

In the 1970s, most duck hunting I experienced was on the rivers. In my formative years Dad, Grandpa and I spent many days together in duck and goose blinds.

Less than an hour from my home the first ring-necked pheasants were introduced to the continental United States. Birds arrived by sea in 1882, from China. Dad and grandpa shared many stories of incredible pheasant hunts they had in the 1950s and ‘60s. “Best pheasant hunting in the country,” Grandpa said. He wasn’t wrong. Today, we don’t pester what few pheasants remain in the valley. But parts of Eastern Oregon are flourishing with ring-necks. A few years back, Echo and I secured a limit of ducks, pheasants and valley quail on one of our best days, ever.

Valley quail and mountain quail thrive around my home. Getting on multiple coveys a day is common. The occasional ruffed grouse is put up in the valley, but they’re more prevalent in the foothills of the Cascades and Coast Range. Just above ruffed grouse in the mountains are blue grouse. When I was a kid we never saw a blue grouse below 2,000 feet. Today we shoot a lot of the big birds at and below 500 feet. More than once I’ve taken valley and mountain quail, blue and ruffed grouse on the same day. Truly special hunts, all of them.

In mid-September band-tailed pigeons are the target. They occupy both mountain ranges and parts of the valley floor. In my days of youth the daily limit was seven, today it’s only two. Still, dogs love these hunts, as does my 86-year-old father. Intercepting speeding birds as they fly ridgelines in search of berries, minerals and water makes for challenging shooting and steep country retrieves.

In the fall we chase Western gray squirrels. Kona loved treeing gray squirrels almost as much as he reveled in fall turkey hunting. The furry tree-dwellers are delicious.

For decades, cackling Canada geese have flocked to the Willamette Valley to graze on rye grass. The Willamette Valley is dubbed The Grass Seed Capital Of The World, and this prime food attracts loads of big game and waterfowl. Minimas — the smallest of the cackling Canada geese — regularly funnel into big decoy spreads in flocks of 10,000 or more. Words can’t be heard over the screaming little geese.

Thirty minutes from home, in multiple directions, is the best duck hunting I’ve found anywhere in the country. One day you’re hunting wigeon and pintails in flooded fields; the next, picking greenheads in tiny creeks surrounded by tall trees. You can regularly shoot a seven drake limit of wood ducks on public waters and private land filbert orchards, what outsiders call hazelnuts. The valleys hold some of the world’s best soil for filbert farming. As these orchards have expanded in recent years, wood duck and mallard numbers continue to grow because they love feasting on the nuts. Today there are more wood ducks than I ever recall seeing in my 51 years of hunting them.

As I finalize these words, I’m preparing for the next several days afield, all near home. Tomorrow I’ll be hunting wood ducks on the river. The day after that, checking my 75 trail cameras set in the nearby hills. And the day after that I head to the Coast Range to hunt secretive Roosevelt elk in the rainforest. Last November I took a 340-class Roosevelt 35 minutes from home, my biggest Roosy bull to date.

There’s no better place to be, doing what I love the most. Oregon is a grand place to grow up, and to grow old.

Scott Haugen grew up in western Oregon, lived a semi-subsistence lifestyle in Alaska’s Arctic, and has traveled to more than 40 countries. Today cott spends more than 200 days a year in the field: hunting, fishing, scouting, working with his dogs, and photographing wildlife. Scott’s wife, Tiffany Haugen, is a nationally-known wild game and fish cookbook author.

#8417. Arctic Town Kills Dozens of Grizzlies Feeding on Dump

For years, the grizzlies near Inuvik in Canada’s western Arctic relied on a stable source of food — the town dump. Generations of bears grew up feeding there. In 2025, wildlife officials found that over 40 grizzlies regularly visited the dump.

In 2025, before the fence was built, trail cameras captured multiple grizzlies hanging around the dump, looking for food. Photo: Government of the Northwest Territories

It was too much. This year, the town built an electric fence around the site, hoping the bears would move on. They didn’t. Some started coming into town looking for food. Others just hung around the fence, searching for a way in.

“They would just do these laps around the dump, and they would just be emaciated, sickly,” said Toby Halle, a Renewable Resources officer based in Inuvik, told the CBC. “They don’t know what to do with themselves. And quite frankly, we don’t know what to do with them.”

Inuvik, population about 3,500, was the first community in that part of the Arctic to try to wean the bears off their dependence on dump food with such a fence, which cost about $700,000. The problem was, the bears had not learned to depend on any other source of food.

“We’ve probably had three to four generations of bears that have been using that landfill,” said local mayor Peter Clarkson. In the end, the town took a drastic solution — but one that was probably unavoidable in the circumstances: They shot 28 bears.

Tragic though this is, officials hope that within the next couple of years, the remaining bears in the area will move on and learn to eat more natural fare.

Many other Arctic towns still have open dumps where bears come to feed. Photo: Government of the Northwest Territories

One of Canada’s premier arctic travelers, he is the author of The Horizontal Everest and Arctic Eden, and has just finished a book about adventures in Labrador. In 2018, he was awarded the Polar Medal by the Governor General of Canada and in 2022, he received the Queen’s Platinum Jubilee Medal for services to exploration.

#8414. Leonard Clark: Explorer, Spy, Liar

Born in 1907, Leonard Francis Clark was an explorer, war hero, and writer who made daring expeditions all over the world before dying mysteriously in Venezuela in 1957.

Today, he isn’t well known, but has this lack of scrutiny been a blessing in disguise for this forgotten explorer’s legacy? His books, filled with adventure and excitement, read like fiction. The more one digs into the forgotten reports and old newspaper articles, the stranger his story, and the more complicated the man.

A Tibetan expedition under Leonard Clark in 1949. Photo: The Marching Wind

The main sources on Leonard Clark are all, well, Leonard Clark. This isn’t necessarily damning, but it is something he shares with most good con artists or cult leaders. The following is a short biography based only on official records, such as draft cards and census data. The first rule of reading historical accounts is that every historical figure is a liar.

Leonard Francis Clark was born in British Columbia, Canada, in January 1907 to two Americans, Maud and Francis Clark. In 1909, they moved back to Maud’s birth state of California, and Francis promptly died.

There are conflicting statements online about which university he attended, but census data shows him living only a few kilometers from the University of California (now the University of California, Berkeley) in 1930, so I’d put my money on that one. He began traveling right out of college, spending three years in Borneo on a hunting trip with his brother, Laurence. By the late 1930s, he was on a National Geographic-funded expedition to the South China Sea.

His article, cryptically titled Among the Big Knot Lois of Hainan, was published in the 1938 September edition. In it, he recounts how he and a small expeditionary force landed in the midst of a deadly cholera epidemic. Relying on hired porters to carry their supplies, Clark and Nicol Smith, another future travel writer, set off into the interior. Soon, malaria and infection laid low most of the team, including, per Clark’s article, Smith.

Again, according to Clark, Smith urged him to take the remaining five porters, the minimum of equipment, and keep going.

Much of the article is dedicated to ethnographic observations, with the typical exoticism and patronizing tone of the era. At one point, Clark noted that the Li people of Hainan reminded him of indigenous Amazonian people and “the men were tall, many six feet.”

A recent study showed that the average height for men in Hainan today is 168.7cm, around 5’6″, and the same study found that average heights had increased over the past century. This is a small nitpick, but he’ll repeat this unlikely claim again in the Amazon. It is worth noting that the “six-foot-tall Indian warrior” was a stock character in contemporary pulp adventure fiction.

There is an interesting contrast between Clark’s account and some of the newspaper reports, especially one from a Virginia paper, which relied on Smith for the story. This article implies Smith did much more than lay around his tent with malaria. Clark’s article claims he was the first white man to enter 47 different villages, while the newspaper says that Smith was the first Westerner to penetrate inland.

There is a third source: the 37-minute silent film compiled from the expedition’s footage and photographs. In it, we briefly see a white man lying under a mosquito net as if ill. But whether this is Smith, Clark — who admits himself to experiencing malaria symptoms on the expedition — or the third westerner on the expedition, one Jack Munroe, the film doesn’t say.

His previous experience in the region led him to return a few years later, this time on behalf of the U.S. Office of Strategic Services (OSS), a precursor to the CIA. His work during the war centered around the China-Burma-India corridor, particularly the Yellow River valley. He was involved in organizing guerrilla activities, building intelligence networks, and navigating the Japanese surrender of Taiwan.

In a later press statement, Clark explained how he had worked with General Dai Li, and that “the Reds had it in for him,” due to the infamously repressive actions Dai Li took against Chinese Communists. Dai Li, whom historian Barbara Tuchman called “China’s combination of Himmler and J. Edgar Hoover,” was killed in a 1946 plane crash. Wide speculation places the blame for this crash on either the Chinese Communist Party or the OSS. This would make things awkward for Clark a few years later.

He continued his government work in the area after the Second World War, sliding right into the developing Cold War. In 1949, Clark led a large, well-funded expedition into Tibet.

It would have gotten him in the history books if Leonard Clark identified a peak higher than Everest. Unfortunately for him, he didn’t. But he certainly proposed that he had, when he claimed that he’d surveyed Amne Machin and found it to be 9,040m high.

Amne Machin is not taller than Everest. Photo: Wikimedia Commons

Clark published his account of the expedition years later, titled The Marching Wind. In it, he wrote with his typical style: daring escapes, stereotypically aggressive natives, and breezy adventure writing. What it did not contain was the data to support his claims.

As soon as he released his first report and measurements, geographers, geologists, and mountaineers pored over them. Bradford Washburn, a cartographer, photographer, and explorer, published a thorough examination of the surveying techniques and questionable results from Clark’s expedition.

A contemporary review in the Alpine Journal, commenting on his photographic evidence, wrote that “anything more unconvincing could hardly be imagined — yet, ostensibly…the mountain was the object of the expedition.” More on that “ostensibly” later. Suffice it to say, Clark’s estimate that Amne Machin could be almost 1,000m taller than Everest was not adopted by geographers at large.

The review concluded that “it can hardly be wondered if mountaineers, whom Mr. Clark rather despises, cannot accept his results.”

Clark blithely remarked that he had “some rather bulky complaints by mountain and other specialists,” but admitted subpar instruments made his results “indecisive.”

A surveying party including Clark, far right, taking observations of Amne Machin. Photo: The Marching Wind

If he doesn’t seem too broken up, it may be because Amne Machin’s height wasn’t the main purpose of his trip. Clark was supported logistically and went on behalf of Ma Bufang, the fiercely anti-communist warlord ruling Qinghai, on the border of Tibet. Anticipating that he could not long hold out against the People’s Liberation Army, Clark proposed that his expedition would map a path of retreat. This would help Ma Bufang’s army cross into Tibet and continue their anti-Communist campaign from the mountains.

Clark envisioned Ma Bufang’s Qinghai, through Tibet, becoming “the bulwark against Red invasion of India.” Clark went on the expedition and did his mapping, but the PLA took Qinghai only months later, and Ma Bufang fled to Hong Kong.

In his introduction to his book, Clark claims that the United States was unaware of his “very slight aid” to Ma Bufang and local anti-Communist guerrilla activities, and he involved himself with the Chinese Nationalist army “as a private American citizen.” If I were to say that I was skeptical of this claim, I would not be the first to do so. But that falls under speculation, so I will say only that I sent a Freedom of Information Act request to the CIA regarding Clark’s work in Tibet. However, the records were not forthcoming.

After returning from the mountains, Clark went to a wedding anniversary celebration in Canton, surrounded by his fellow Allied intelligence members and their set. Things got a little out of hand, and Clark soon found himself in a Cantonese jail with a bullet wound and two manslaughter charges.

Chinese Nationalist soldiers on Clark’s Tibetan expedition. Photo: The Marching Wind

The other two men involved were Harold Harris, an Englishman who died immediately, and Willard Freeman from Massachusetts, who died later from his injuries. Clark also sustained a gunshot wound, but had recovered sufficiently by late November to be moved from hospital to prison.

The three had been drinking together in Freeman’s home when, at about 4 am, servants reported hearing gunshots. Mrs. Maya Freeman entered the room at 10 am to find an odd and grisly scene. Harris, fully clothed, lay dead on Freeman’s bed with three gunshot wounds. Freeman, completely nude, was also lying on the bed, alive with two shots to the abdomen. Clark, in his underwear, was on a second bed, with a single gunshot to the chest. Furniture was overturned, indicating a fight, and all three were covered in scratches. The only weapon found was Clark’s .32 caliber pistol.

Later that month, the Canton District Court denied Clark’s bail request. The court, still trying to determine what exactly happened, focused on the widowed Maya Freeman. Interviewing her for hours, they attempted to make her confess to an affair with Clark. Both of them repeatedly denied it. Clark further denied that he had fired any of the six shots, though he didn’t name the shooter.

Clark’s brother contacted former OSS head General William Donovan, who rallied the State Department. Clark reportedly was concerned that if he wasn’t released before the city fell to the Communists, he would be tortured for information regarding Dai Li and OSS activities. The State Department investigated and, thanks to its intervention, Clark was released.

Clark’s legal troubles were splashed across newspapers worldwide. Photo: The Journal (Caldwell, Ohio) November 24, 1949

What exactly happened in Freeman’s house that night? Was the dispute between the Allied Intelligence men personal, or professional? If the world were ever to find out, it wouldn’t be from Clark. Once freed, Clark returned to California and moved on to his next adventure. It was an ambitious one: Leonard Clark was going to discover the lost city of El Dorado.

He had an old parchment map to the lost city, he claimed, which showed it lay in the upper reaches of Peru. Never mind that it had been pretty well established for over a century that El Dorado was a myth, following the efforts of explorers Alexander von Humboldt and Robert Schomburgk.

Clark’s resulting account, The Rivers Ran East, contains all the genre tropes you would expect from a fictional work in the same setting. Native Amazonians are ferocious cannibals who capture Clark and keep him in a poison snake pit, crocodiles can and must be lassoed (there are no crocodiles in Peru, only caimans, a type of alligator), and there is a beautiful lady explorer, Inez Pokorny, who is secretly part of a London-based international El Dorado-seeking organization. If this organization existed, I have found no trace of it outside of this book.

If I were to list every blatant impossibility in his account, we would be here for days, so I’ll just continue on one particular thread: Leonard Clark seems to have a very loose grasp of which animals are in Peru and how big they are. At one point, he is blinded by a venom-spitting snake. There are three venom-spitting snake species, and none of them are in South America.

His 500-pound (227kg) boars must be peccaries, pig-like animals native to the Americas. The Amazon native white-lipped peccary tops out around 40kg, while the unconfirmed giant peccary still has only been estimated at 50kg. Likewise, his 12m-long pythons must be anacondas, which have a maximum recorded length of eight meters.

Clark claims to have found El Dorado. What he found was a group of Chicham people panning for gold, who traded some of it to Clark and Pokorny. According to his book, Clark traveled under the pretense of investigating botanical medicines, while actually searching for El Dorado.

In fact, as the El Dorado adventures on the page grew more and more ridiculous, I began to wonder if it were not the other way around. Here I have fully entered the realm of speculation. American corporations were, and still are, extremely interested in extracting resources from South America, including pharmaceuticals. In fact, Clark also reported success in this area, claiming to have identified over 60 native medicinals with commercial possibility — as well as Amazonian oil.

In praise of Clark’s work, Baron Wolfenegg, Director of Colonization for the Orient in Peru, accidentally said the quiet part out loud, stating he’d “learned much from this book that will help my work of colonizing this vast jungle.”

On Peru’s Perené River. Photo: The Rivers Ran East

It is practically gauche for explorers to die in their beds, and Clark, who seemed to mold his life to the shape of adventure stories, was no exception. Clark was in Venezuela in 1957, looking, he claimed, for diamond mines. He entered the Amazon basin, never to emerge again.

One history of the OSS offhandedly mentions that Clark died with “two native arrows in his back.” I suspect the explorer’s penchant for sensationalism survived his death. Other contemporary reports describe Clark as having drowned when his canoe overturned.

As I pointed out earlier, most of the information on Clark’s expeditions came from Clark himself. Since he never returned, startlingly little information is available about his death at age 49.

The area around the Orinoco River in Venezuela is, in fact, rich in diamond deposits. Photo: Wikimedia Commons

The line between fact and fiction is notoriously vague in much of the adventure travel genre. Is Herman Melville telling the full truth about his time spent on the island of Nuku Hiva? Did anyone actually complete the “Long Walk” out of a Soviet detention camp, which Slavomir Rawicz falsely claimed as his own tale? How much artistic license and embellishment is acceptable before exploration accounts become outright hoaxes?

I didn’t write this article with the goal of proving Leonard Clark a charlatan. It can hardly be denied that he really was going to these places, at the very least. But there are enough discrepancies and inaccuracies in his accounts to conclude that they should be taken with a grain of salt, and his treasure-hunting pseudo-archaeological tendencies make his anthropological findings, well, questionable.

As a contemporary New Yorker book critic put it, Clark had “the same gift, or weakness, for grandiose verbiage that got Marco Polo in trouble with his fellow Venetians.”

The real story, and one which may never be fully disentangled, lies in why, exactly, Clark was doing his exploring. Somewhere between the romantic adventurer and the cynically colonialist spy using exploration as a guise for the extractive, violent enforcement of Western capitalist hegemony, lies the truth.

“Every explorer has two faces,” Clark wrote in the opening to The Rivers Ran East; “the secret one and the one he shows to the world.”

Lou Bodenhemier holds an MA in History from the University of Limerick and a BA in Creative Writing from the University of Arizona. He’s interested in maritime and disaster history as well as criminal history, and his dissertation focused on the werewolf trials of early modern Europe. At the present moment he can most likely be found perusing records of shipboard crime and punishment during the Age of Sail, or failing that, writing historical fiction horror stories. He lives in Dublin and hates the sun.

#8411. The Dane Steadman Interview: Hard Going and High Stakes in the Karakoram

There is much more to an expedition than summit or failure. In this exclusive interview, American climber Dane Steadman explains why his recent new route on Karakoram’s Sosbun Brakk (6,414m) “was much more difficult than the one on Yashkuk Sar, both on and off the mountain.” Strong words, considering that he and his partners were awarded a Piolet d’Or for Yashkuk Sar.

The one factor on the team’s side was the previous experience of Steadman and Cody Winckler. “It was our third trip, so we knew a lot more about climbing there,” Steadman said. Indeed, after Yashkuk Sar, Winckler and Steadman returned to Pakistan in 2025 and climbed two new routes with Blake Berghoff.

Grant Stewart of Canada, the third member of the Sosbun Brakk team, is a skilled climber but was less familiar with the Karakoram. His most remarkable climbs had taken place in Alaska and at home in the Canadian Rockies.

Comparing the two epic climbs, Steadman highlighted the differences.

“The trip to Yashkuk Sar went incredibly smoothly. Getting there and acclimatizing was great. We had an amazing weather window for the climb. Nothing on it was exceptionally hard, and we did it on the first try, and the descent went without a hitch,” he said.

He explained that the hardest parts of that 2024 first ascent were their lack of experience and the uncertainty that came with it. An unexpected mishap also occurred during the climb itself: a snow mushroom collapsed above them, forcing them to change their ascent route halfway up.

“On the other hand,” Steadman went on, “almost everything about the Sosbun Brakk trip was hard: lost bags, sickness acclimatizing, big storms beforehand and while on the mountain, hard climbing and route-finding, and a very difficult descent through terrible weather.”

The unclimbed north face of Sosbun Brakk. Photo: Dane Steadman

“[Overall,] on the Yashkuk trip, I think we felt for most of the time that we would be able to do it with only some moments of doubt here and there,” Steadman admitted. “On Sosbun, we thought much of the time that we wouldn’t be able to do it for all the reasons above. We were quite surprised [to succeed].”

One unusual aspect of the Sosbun Brakk expedition was its dates. They went to Pakistan and climbed in early fall, two months later than the Karakoram’s typical July-August climbing season. Yet, Steadman says this was planned to account for the changing climate in the high ranges.

Approaching the face. Photo: Dane Steadman

“First, while the Karakoram doesn’t receive as much of the Indian monsoon as the main Himalaya, it is still a monsoonal climate, and I think (and have heard from many locals now) that the weather tends to be much better in the fall than in the summer,” he explained.

“Second, we were concerned about rockfall and generally unsafe conditions on the face, being relatively low and lacking in buttresses. We thought the colder temperatures would make things safer and increase the odds of finding good ice.

“Third, Grant was able to get time off in October more easily than earlier in September. In a perfect world, we probably would have gone one to two weeks earlier, but we worked with what we had.”

Asked about other expeditions planning to visit the Pakistani mountains in the future, Steadman suggests that October could be a good time for certain climbs, but that September is also great. “This year, the weather was much less stable in September-October than I’ve experienced on my previous trips. Things got much snowier than normal for that time of year, [according to locals], but I think the weather was still better than through most of the summer.”

The team planned a single alpine-style push in the purest style. However, when they started on the route, they encountered an impassable hanging serac. They had to retreat and decide whether to try a totally different line or go home.

The team decided to try a route to the right of the originally planned one. But how could they be sure they wouldn’t reach another dead end?

Steadman admitted it was difficult. “The line we ended up climbing was entirely hidden from view at our base camp and where we acclimatized,” he explained. “[It was] an east-facing gash on a north face, which we only saw from the northwest until we tried it. From all the views we had before the first attempt, the central line we first attempted appeared to be the only route that wouldn’t involve climbing huge walls of monolithic gneiss, as it followed ice streaks and sheets for a good amount of it, and only looked to have relatively short cruxes.

At the base, they discovered that a tiny but menacing serac hung over the center of the wall, threatening the lower half. (The upper half appeared protected by an overhanging rock wall above it.)

“So we shifted to a different line to the left, which looked much harder but possible, and planned to rejoin our intended line at mid-height, under the protection of the overhangs.

That attempt didn’t so much dead-end as things compounded, forcing a retreat. “We were tired even on the approach from non-stop efforts to get there and acclimatize. We had some equipment issues, were moving slowly, and most of all, the climbing was damn hard,” he said.

In the end, they chose to rappel down after a two-hour-plus lead climb on a pitch that hadn’t looked hard from below. Dusk was approaching; there was no bivy spot in sight, and they were only 300m up the 1,800m wall.

“However, from our position on that attempt, we were able to see the right-trending, east-facing gash on the right side of the face, which was totally hidden by monolithic rock walls from all our previous vantage points,” he said. “So, after bailing, we felt like the central line would require fresh bodies, minds, and a huge weather window, but the right-hand line would be much more feasible.”

The climbers rested for three days in base camp and then set off again. “We certainly felt that we could get shut down at any number of points, especially at the steep mushroom-choked chimney on the headwall below the junction with the northwest spur,” Steadman admitted. “But it seemed like there was a good chance we could do it, and, most importantly, we felt like we could bail from anywhere [if necessary].”

The summit ridge of Sosbun Brakk after many tough decisions. Photo: Dane Steadman

However, bailing would not be easy. “The lower part would involve rappelling off V-threads, he explained, and if they had to turn back below the headwall, they would need to rappel out right to an icefield.

Route topo: line of ascent in red, line of descent in yellow. Photo: Steadman/Stewart

The trio summited on their fifth day on the wall, at 4 pm. Days were now short, and they still had a long, uncertain descent ahead. The descent began on the line of ascent, but eventually deviated to new terrain.

“We descended the upper half of our route, almost entirely rappelling down the northwest spur, with a little bit of downclimbing/traversing),” Steadman said.

They then reached a point west of their ascent line, where the face was slightly smaller and more covered in permanent ice. “Then we rappelled straight down, diagonaling back left partway down to avoid some serac hazard.”

Descending in worsening weather. Photo: Dane Steadman

Dane Steadman and Cody Winckler of the U.S., together with Grant Stewart of Canada, completed the first ascent of the north face of Sosbun Brakk (6,414m) in Pakistan’s Karakoram last month. The peak had been climbed only once before, by a Japanese team in 1981, from a different, milder side. The new 1,800m route includes difficulties of M7, WI5.

Angela Benavides graduated university in journalism and specializes in high-altitude mountaineering and expedition news. She has been writing about climbing and mountaineering, adventure and outdoor sports for 20+ years.

Prior to that, Angela Benavides spent time at/worked at a number of local and international media. She is also experienced in outdoor-sport consultancy for sponsoring corporations, press manager and communication executive, and a published author.
