#8409. Idaho Wildlife Commissioner Finally Resigns as Plea Deal in Elk Poaching Scandal Draws Hunter Outrage

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 19, 2026 2:47 PM EST

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

The seven-person panel that constitutes Idaho’s Fish and Game Commission is down to six after commissioner Brody Harshbarger resigned Wednesday amid pressure from sportsmen and at least one state lawmaker. Harshbarger took a plea deal on Nov. 9 to dismiss five of seven charges related to illegally killing two bull elk in the region he represents.

“Your service on the commission is concluded, effective immediately,” wrote Gov. Brad Little’s chief of staff, Zach Hauge, in response to Harshbarger’s resignation, obtained by the Idaho Capital Sun. “Maintaining the integrity, credibility and effective operation of the Idaho Fish and Game Commission remains a top priority for Gov. Little.”

Harshbarger was charged in March with seven wildlife violations from the 2025 hunting season, including poaching two bull elk in Fremont County, where he lives. The former commissioner stepped away from commission duties once he was charged, but retained his seat until this week while the case continued, effectively leaving the position vacant.

Related: Idaho Wildlife Commissioner Charged with Poaching Elk, Trespassing, Says He’ll ‘Voluntarily Postpone’ Duties

“I understand everyone deserves their day in court, but commissioners are charged with making and upholding our regulations,” Nick Fasciano, executive director of the Idaho Wildlife Federation, tells Outdoor Life. “And we do expect them to be held to a higher standard. The whole thing is disappointing.”

The commission sets statewide hunting, fishing, and trapping seasons plus bag limits and other regulations based on recommendations and research supplied by Idaho Department of Fish and Game scientists. Each member of the commission can serve two four-year terms, and no more than four commissioners can come from the same political party. Harshbarger is a farmer in Ashton, Idaho. It’s an area known for massive chunks of public land and substantial grain and potato operations.

“I love our wildlife and the community we live in,” Harshbarger’s profile read on the commission’s website before it was removed. “We have a lot of challenges, and I am excited to help continue to solve them. What an amazing place to live and work.”

Harshbarger pled guilty to hunting without a tag and shooting across a road. The remaining five charges, which are being dismissed in exchange for his guilty plea, are two illegal big-game kills, hunting from a vehicle, failure to retrieve game, and trespass (one bull was on private property).

When news of Harshbarger’s plea deal went public, hunters and anglers started contacting regional IDFG offices and the governor’s office to demand his removal. The plea deal means his penalties could be as low as $500 and a one-year hunting suspension when he’s sentenced Dec. 9.

Frustration has also boiled over on social media around Harshbarger’s case.

“Wake up Governor Little and do the right thing,” one Instagram user commented on the news of the plea deal, prior to Harshbarger’s resignation. “You and Senator Risch are already working to sell off our public lands and now you won’t step in to protect our wild game. Shame on you two.”

Harshbarger was appointed by Gov. Little in 2024.

“Public trust is difficult to earn and easily lost,” wrote Idaho state Rep. David Leavitt in an email sent to Little on Tuesday that called for Harshbarger’s dismissal. “Maintaining that trust requires us to uphold the same principles regardless of who is involved. Idaho citizens deserve nothing less.”

Little is also now in charge of appointing the director of IDFG. The commission did that until this year, when Little signed a publicly opposed bill into law changing the procedure from a seven-person decision to a one-person appointment by the governor, with confirmation by the state Senate.

“Commissioners and the director now serve at the pleasure of the governor,” Fasciano says. “The commission is not empowered to boot out one of their own members even if they were so inclined.”

Current IDFG director Jim Fredericks, a career fisheries biologist, was already leading the agency when the new law took effect July 1, making him the state’s last director selected by the commission.

Read Next: Montana Man Banned from Hunting for 20 Years After Bowhunters Watch Him Poach a Trophy Bull

“The actual department of Fish and Game is going to take some heat for this [Harshbarger incident], but they weren’t the decision makers here,” Fasciano says. “The commission and the director were not able to drive a resolution here and they are also not responsible for the sentencing. A lot of frustration is going to be directed toward them, that isn’t their fault.”

Kris Millgate is a multimedia journalist based in eastern Idaho. She’s a fly fisher, a bird hunter and an avid seeker of awkward moments. Read and watch more of her work at tightlinemedia.com

#8405. Natural Wonders: Crowley Lake Columns

It is low tide on Crowley Lake in Mono County, California. The water is still, the trout abundant, and one or two kayakers paddle about, enjoying the lack of tourists. But as you walk along the shore, something out of place appears: spiral pillars and arches that seem left over from some ancient civilization.

Crowley Lake is a man-made reservoir, administered by the Los Angeles Department of Water and Power. It was named after an important figure in the community, affectionately known as the Desert Padre. This beloved Catholic priest worked tirelessly to help a community that experienced water shortages throughout the 1920s and 1930s. Water from the nearby Owen Valley was diverted to Los Angeles, leaving very little for local residents.

Father Crowley made it his mission to bolster tourism in the area. The publicity led authorities to help locals by drawing up plans for a dam and reservoir. When Father Crowley died in a car accident, authorities completed the Long Valley Dam and named the reservoir Lake Crowley. If it weren’t for his intervention, the lake wouldn’t have existed.

As the years passed, visitors continued coming to Lake Crowley to fish, kayak, camp, and hike. Then something weird started to appear on the cliffsides. The banks appeared to be receding and hollowing out, revealing hundreds upon hundreds of spiral columns. Many onlookers believed they were man-made. Some compared the formations to Moorish architecture from North Africa and Spain.

Though the reservoir is man-made, the area’s history dates back millennia. Crowley Lake lies in the wider Long Valley Caldera, one of Earth’s largest calderas.

The caldera sits atop one of three ancient supervolcanoes in the United States. Around 760,000 years ago, this supervolcano erupted and spewed 650 cubic kilometers of rhyolite rock and ash into the air for a week. It covered the land in a blanket of ash and debris over one kilometer thick. And it left behind a 32km long, 18km wide, and 900m deep depression in the earth.

The depression formed when the subterranean magma chamber emptied onto the surface and the remnant shell of a roof collapsed. The blast and pyroclastic flow were so powerful that ash fell as far as Kansas. Along the rim are the lava dome complexes of Mammoth, Glass, and Bald Mountains. The caldera also hosts a cluster of hydrothermally active lakes called the Mammoth Lakes.

The main feature left by the eruption is the Bishop Tuff, a few square kilometers in area. A tuff is a rock of compacted volcanic ash. At the base of this tuff stand the Crowley Lake Columns. They range in thickness, average about six meters high, and are generally reddish, beige, grey, or white with a dark water line at their base. Some lean while others stand straight.

Attached by arches, the columns are so evenly spaced that to some visitors, it resembles an ancient temple. Horizontal cracks about 30cm apart add further detail to the columns. They look like they hold up the cliffs but they simply remained after the softer cliff rock started to erode.

The high levels of silica, sodium, and potassium in the columns make the rock conduct heat well and cool suddenly. This led to the cracks. Similar formations exist in Guatemala, Mexico, and New Mexico.

The few people who frequent Crowley Lake have long known about the columns. Only recently, social media has started to highlight them. This is due to its seclusion on the lake’s shoreline. The only ways to access them are on foot or by boat, kayak, or 4×4. The dirt road on which you turn from Highway 395 is so steep and uneven that it can bog down vehicle. Visitors tend to drive halfway, then walk.

Despite popular speculation that these columns are an example of columnar jointing, they are not. The geological process is different. With columnar jointing, the spaces are not so far apart. A study by geologists at UC Berkeley used a combination of computer models, X-ray diffraction, and electron microscope imagery to determine how they formed. It turns out that the culprit is simply…water.

“We hypothesize that the columns are a result of instabilities at the liquid water and steam interface as cold water seeped into the still-cooling Bishop Tuff,” says Noah Randolph-Flagg, the study’s lead scientist.

The columns formed through hydrothermal convection. Photo: Shutterstock

Essentially, rainwater seeped down into the hot ash and started to boil, producing a mineral glue called mordonite. The boiling water formed a column of steam which carried the mordonite upward through the rock. Thus, the mineral hardened the rock, leading it to remain standing while the surrounding rock eroded. This process is called hydrothermal convection or circulation.

According to Randolph-Flagg, it only took 100 years to form these columns. The study also states that “the liquid water–steam interface probably is the source of the instability that generates columnar flow.”

As for the erosion of the cliffside, the reservoir’s water levels fluctuate throughout the year with the seasons. After the completion of the reservoir, the columns started to reveal themselves. The constant rise and fall as well as a repeating freeze-thaw cycle caused the tuff to recede. It revealed the columns and also hollowed out the cliffside to form shallow caves. Take care when going into these little caves or pushing on the columns, as they might not be as stable. As the cliff continues to erode, we should see more of these columns.

The arches resemble Moorish architecture. Photo: Shutterstock

In terms of the Long Valley Caldera, scientists have closely monitored its activity, which has been increasing since the 1980s. Since it is a one of only 20 supervolcanoes in the world, they have paid close attention to its potential for eruption, despite its status as inactive.

There have been small earthquakes and signs of movement in the earth as if it were “breathing.” This is seen in Yellowstone, another supervolcano. While there are no immediate signs of an impending eruption, the caldera’s history and current activity suggest that it is possible.

Kristine De Abreu is a writer at ExplorersWeb.

Kristine has been writing about Science, Mysteries and History for 4+ years. Prior to that, Kristine studied at the University of Leicester in the UK.

Based in Port-of-Spain, Kristine is also a literature teacher, avid reader, hiker, occasional photographer, an animal lover and shameless ramen addict.

#8402. Everest Winds Force Climbers to Retreat

Despite weather forecasts of high winds on Everest, the Russian team gamely left for the West Shoulder today. But in the end, they couldn’t fight the gale-force winds, which eventually forced them back to their tent at the base of the mountain.

“It’s calm right now, but the forecast is worrying,” leader Andrei Vasiliev wrote yesterday from a snow cave, where Sergey Kondrashkin, Vitaly Shiilov, and Vasiliev had sheltered for the night at 6,400m. Late today, they confirmed that the weather forecast of powerful winds was unfortunately right.

The three climbers had fixed three pitches above them, according to Mountain Club, and were hoping for firmer terrain as they approached the West Shoulder of Everest, en route to the West Ridge.

However, as we previously reported after consulting meteorologist Vitor Baia of Portugal, conditions were good Tuesday and Wednesday, but winds rose dramatically today.

On a previous foray up Everest, one of the climbers heads for the West Shoulder. Photo: Andrei Vasiliev

“The winds are very strong,” Vasiliev reported. “It is impossible to work higher on the mountain.”

Vasiliev said that his team is retreating to a tent at 5,600m. This is below the Lho La Pass, possibly on the Khumbu Glacier beside the vertical section leading to that col.

The question is whether the team will wait there for better conditions, retreat to their base in Lobuche, or call off the expedition.

Some charts still show a chance of variable winds tomorrow, before the jet stream hits the mountain with full force (see below).

Wind levels at around summit altitude on Everest this week, by meteoexploration.com

Baia says the winds will remain high every day until next Tuesday. From Wednesday on, the winds should be weaker.

The Russians set off on their third trip up Everest last Monday, hoping to summit via the West Ridge without oxygen or external support, and with no one else on the mountain.

On Tuesday evening, they checked in with Mountain Club from their tent at 6,000m, where they spent the night. The hard trailbreaking through fresh snow had slowed their pace, but they expected better conditions on the following day, as they started up steeper terrain toward Everest’s shoulder. They knew the snow up there was harder and compacted. They could even see their trails from the previous trip up, so no new soft snow had accumulated.

The Russian team on Everest’s West Shoulder. Photo: Andrei Vasiliev

Yesterday, Vasiliev noted that they had climbed four pitches on old fixed ropes, plus another eight pitches of their own. The team is fixing ropes at the hardest sections, but not entirely along the route. As expected, snow conditions had improved with the more vertical terrain, but as Vasiliev pointed out, “it’s not exactly easy walking yet.”

The Russian leader insists the route to the summit is doable. However, wind delays and a lack of time may ultimately end their attempt.

#8398. Piolet d'Or Team Shares Insider Tips for Climbing in Peru's Huayhuash Range

Marc Toralles, Ruben Sanmartin, and Bru Busom of Spain were awarded a Piolet d’Or for a highly committing new route on Yerupaja Grande (6,634m), the highest peak of Peru’s Huayhuash range. ExplorersWeb asked the young climbers about the ascent and the unique character of the Andes. They offered some tips for climbers looking to add Peru to their list of high-altitude destinations.

In the last few years, the Andes have reportedly been affected by climate change. “The main effect is that the huge snow structures on the summits are exposed to warmer temperatures and become more fragile,” Sanmartin said. “Basically, they fall down more easily.”

“That means that climbers need to pick their route carefully [when judging] what lingers above them,” Buson explained.

Bru Buson, left, and Marc Toralles. Photo: Ruben Sanmartin

This was especially noticeable last year on Siula Grande. “There was a snowfield midway up the face as we trekked by, and on our way back, it was not there anymore,” said Marc Toralles. “Somehow, the melting process in the Andes, with its 5,000m and even 6,000m [altitudes], is similar to what we are seeing every year in the Alps, where we all work as mountain guides.”

For Toralles, the best thing in the Andes is how accessible the mountains are. “It’s so easy to go climbing there,” he said.

There are no lifts or paved roads, but you can go, climb a peak, and return in three or four days. Something like that in the Himalaya would take weeks. All you need is to get to Huaraz by plane, bus, or car from Lima, where you may stay some days to acclimatize (there are many options), and then go to the mountains.

If your planned peak is not in the right conditions, you will surely find some other one to climb. Also, the options are infinite for ice climbing, alpine climbing, rock, trekking, anything.

The team’s base camp. Photo: Spanish Yerupaja Grande team

Red tape is minimal. There are no permits for peaks. Instead, climbers and trekkers pay a small fee to cross the comunidades (the areas) where they want to climb, and climbers can hire arrieros (riders with mules) to help carry equipment and supplies to base camps or higher. On this occasion, the climbers kept their logistics basic, with little beyond a slightly larger base camp tent.

With some of the more accessible places, it is advisable to have someone watching over your base camp while you are away climbing.

Once you leave the beaten track, you’ll be alone. “There are no crowds in Huayhuash,” Buson said. Why aren’t they more popular? The climbers explained that Huayhuash peaks are typically more difficult than popular options in the Cordillera Blanca, also in Peru. But they note this lack of popularity is changing fast. “We are seeing much more people in recent years, both climbing and trekking,” they said.

Thanks to the many possibilities, the three Spanish climbers didn’t need to do much planning. “We know Huayhuash pretty well, we are familiar with the terrain and the climbing style, and we are aware there are always options,” said Toralles, who has climbed there four times.

That said, the line the Spaniards climbed on Yerupaja last year was not improvised. They had that route in their mind for some time. “We heard of someone who had tried but called the attempt off, so the route was there to be climbed,” Toralles said. “In addition, it didn’t require a long time, and the timing worked for me, since I was traveling to Peru anyway to do some guiding.”

Buson and Sanmartin joined immediately. To acclimatize, they had worked double shifts the previous weeks around Chamonix, where both are professional guides. “Working on the Mont Blanc massif intensively was great training and acclimatization,” they said. “We were ready by the time we set foot on the mountain.”

The best time to climb in Huayhuash is July, August, and the first half of September.

Once on the mountain, they carefully checked the conditions of the face and the line they would follow. “In the Andes, you have to assume that the summit ridge snow formations, the famous ‘mushrooms,’ the big cornices, and the seracs on the sides of the face are mostly in a delicate state. You need to assume that you will progress on deep, unstable snow, and the entire structure may have deteriorated and be prone to crumble,” Buson noted.

To deal with them, the climbers used some custom-made tools: They attached alitas (little wings) to their ice axes for a better grip on foamy, loose snow. They also carried small snowshoes for some sections.

Ice axe with ‘alitas’ (wings). Photo: Nick Sheldon/Mountain Project

Conditions in the Andean mountains are always variable. “You’ll find different terrain depending on the altitude and the orientation,” Sanmartin added. “This is why technical routes on these peaks require a high level of commitment, which, as happened on our route, may be even higher than the intrinsic difficulty of the pitches,” Toralles explained.

With this in mind, the Spanish team named the route they opened Essence of Commitment.

Marc Toralles ready for the ridge, with large snow pickets and an ice axe with ‘wings’ attached. Photo: Buson/Sanmartin

In this sort of terrain, it is paramount to stay focused and make the right call between subjective and objective factors. “There are countless reasons to turn around on climbs like this, but one must keep going, except for obvious reasons, such as an injury, a completely impassable section, or a really violent storm,” Sanmartin said.

“That is why it is important to consider all options and potential issues before starting the climb. You need to be sure you like the line and want to climb it.”

Asked whether they reached a point of no return during their climb, Buson admitted they did. “On reaching the ridge, we became aware that trying to downclimb the same route would be quite complex. However, we also saw the route upward was doable.”

The Spaniards did not count on a professional meteorologist. “We had our friends texting us the online, free forecasts over InReach,” they said. “One of the positive aspects of Huayhuash is that good weather periods tend to last for quite a while. Weather windows of five to six days are common.” During their climb, for example, they enjoyed acceptable weather, with some rain and snow but no high winds.

They warn that conditions in the Andes are changing quickly. “You need to observe the mountains and adapt,” Toralles said. “Some people aim for a route based on the information in a guidebook printed 50 years ago, and find a totally different scenario.” He pointed out that Siula Grande looked different in 2025 than it did when he climbed it just three years earlier.

Sanmartin noted that conditions have also changed on normal routes. They are now drier, more complex, and often more hazardous. “In some cases, climbers are abandoning the normal routes up the glaciers and opt for ridges which may be more difficult but in better conditions,” he said.

The climbers think the Andes will not get as crowded as the Himalaya, simply because the logistics are not as advanced — no refuges, no helicopters — and because they don’t have the sizzle of being the tallest peaks on Earth. “But they have incredible potential,” they said.

Prior to that, Angela Benavides spent time at/worked at a number of local and international media. She is also experienced in outdoor-sport consultancy for sponsoring corporations, press manager and communication executive, and a published author.

#8397. Old-School Whitetail Wisdom for Stalking Deer Like a Real Woodsman

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 19, 2026 4:56 PM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

The big whitetail buck and I badly startled each other. In the steady rain patter, he apparently didn’t hear me until I’d stillhunted very close to his cedar clump shelter. I almost came unglued when he exploded into view, too spooked to even snort.

After the rifle’s roar shattered the forest stillness, I heard a couple more rhythmic thumps as the by-now invisible deer continued bounding. So much for my semi-frantic snapshot.

But then came a distant, wood-splintering racket, followed by silence. Without snow, it took awhile to slowly, alertly follow up. I found the heart-shot buck. That terminal crash noise came from his failing to clear a windfall when the carburetor quit for keeps.

That’s stillhunting at its best. Unlike the prolonged tedium of trail watching or waiting out a drive, stillhunting’s uptight anticipation is an ongoing thrill. When the action does come, it’s often sudden, without warning. Don’t hunt this way if you have a weak heart.

Very old-timers like my 87-year-old father tell me that forest stillhunting was far more common decades ago. Maybe today’s urban hunters don’t trust their stealth and woodcraft skills. Although not an easy way to take woodland game, including elk, the bottom-line reality is that stillhunting can produce game when other tactics cannot.

Obviously, a lone-wolf hunter can’t make drives to himself. Even when sufficient manpower is available, effective drives require thorough knowledge of both the terrain and how local deer use it. If game chooses not to move because it fed well last night, lacks sexual ambition just now or doesn’t like the prevailing weather, trail watching is usually hopeless. For that matter, many mule deer and most elk and moose simply don’t stick that much to established trails.

The stillhunter is free of such vagaries. Instead of hoping game will come to him, he goes to it — although stillhunting is not the stalking of game already located. Tactically, he has two top priorities. First, to move quietly and alertly, his course basically dictated by wind, terrain and possibly sun direction. Second, to discover game before it sees, hears or smells him. The stillhunter must play a lone-hand game — two hunters seem to make not twice, but four times the noise of one hunter.

Slow movement is a must. A stillhunter covering ground too quickly simply can’t look well enough for concealed game. He also can’t help making extra noise to be picked up by critters who depend on acute hearing every day of their lives to stay out of trouble. They easily detect high frequency noises (including any rustles, squeaks or metallic clinks from our gear and garb) that we often can’t hear.

The real trick in stillhunting is to do the looking and the moving as two separate functions. If you forget everything else, remember that! When you attempt to look for game and move your feet at the same time, sooner or later you will make unwanted noise — snapping a brittle twig, tripping noisily or whatever.

Don’t follow any set rhythm of taking a set number of steps, then stopping. Instead, consider the area that you can see from where you stand as one vision zone. I never move until this zone, of up to 180° ahead and to both sides, has been totally studied.

A vision zone may range out only 50 feet in dense woods or 200-plus yards in more open country. Depending on the extent of the zone, terrain and cover, it may take 30 seconds or several minutes of methodical looking. Often a promising area is worth the time to just sit for extra eyeballing.

Don’t expect to see a full-blown deer or elk looming up ahead like calendar artwork. Look for just parts of the animal as clues. Most forest visuals are vertical lines — tree trunks, etc. An out-of-place horizontal line may be a log or the outline of a deer’s back. A smudge of dull yellow may be autumn leaves or an elk rump. Wide-field binoculars help greatly on such details.

Once, while hunting in the Cascade Mountains, my eye caught a tiny movement ahead. A broad leaf twitching with the breeze? But there was no breeze and, in that evergreen thicket, no deciduous leaves. I waited some minutes, then spotted the movement again for what it was — a blacktail doe’s ear twitching as its owner listened. When she finally moved, right behind her came a shootable buck.

After searching a visual zone (and listening, too), select a route for the next few feet or yards, based on avoidance of brush contact or other potential noise. Footwork is important. Short steps are quietest. They offer best control of shifting your balance if you ease your foot down and feel a twig likely to snap under full weight. Also, only with short steps can you come down slowly and silently on your toes or the ball of your foot. Long steps force the heel down first, making it much more likely that you will snap twigs or make a soft but tell-tale thud.

Want some expert stillhunting lessons? Simply watch a house cat on the prowl for birds or field mice. Slow movement, long look-listen pauses, obvious patience and great concentration are what make felines the finest stillhunters in nature. Once, in Washington’s mountains, I watched a mountain lion on the prowl. This best-of-all deer hunter resembles a magnified house cat at work.

Big or small, hunting cats are superb at using concealment. You should do likewise. Very often, unseen game ahead will hear you. By moving slowly with minimum noise, you may sound much like just another deer. But the listening quarry will be watching in your direction for visual confirmation. Don’t slow yourself any more than necessary. I use camouflage makeup to make my pale face less visible against dark forest backdrop.

If you can move quietly in some kind of concealment, use that route rather than an easier-looking, more open course. Stick to shadows; avoid sunlit patches. When possible, hunt with the sun behind you. Go over ridgetops with caution, using brush or timber cover. If the hilltop lacks cover, try crawling. Of course, stillhunting must be done into the wind or at least across it — never downwind. Although human sense of smell is pretty pathetic, sometimes the barnyard odor of elk ahead can be detected.

Often a stillhunter has the option of following fresh game tracks. Except under ideal conditions, such as new snow, one-man tracking demands so much attention that the stillhunter is distracted while he should be looking for actual game. More than once, deer or elk have surprised me, costing me a shot simply because I was distracted by some interesting tracks. If you start trailing, remember that the maker of these tracks may be bedded down somewhere ahead and almost certainly watching his backtrail. To get anywhere close to it will require extremely good stillhunting.

The lay of the land can be friend or foe. Ridges and hills offer chances to surprise game on the other side. Flat country is tougher because it offers few elevation advantages to the hunter. Although knowing the area always helps, it’s not truly essential for stillhunting. Once, a young stillhunter in our party, new to the place, nailed a whitetail buck the second morning. A fast learner, he capped that next year by pussyfooting close enough to a Cascades bull elk to take him with a bow.

The inexperienced stillhunter is often tempted to work mature evergreen timber, where dense shade means little underbrush. But closely spaced conifers self-prune their branches profusely, forming a noisy layer of dead twigs. Such forests can be hunted only when there’s enough wind noise to cover the hunter’s snap-crackle-pop progress. Unless soggy from rain or wet snow, dead leaves are another audio booby trap.

Some timber is open enough to have a good growth of clover or grass, particularly attractive to elk. By contrast, dense coniferous forests contain little ground-level browse to attract feeding game, but they often provide good escape cover when hunting pressure is on. In unseasonably warm weather, the cool shade of thick evergreen timber invites game. In cold weather, the evergreen canopy traps radiant ground heat for considerably warmer bedding than more open forests.

Where evergreen timber is lacking, game makes the best of thick brushy cover or tall grasses — the latter is commonly a deer favorite. In real cold or after rain, any warming sun draws game to south or west-facing slopes.

Best time of day for stillhunting depends on several factors. Stillhunting as early as light permits may find game feeding or on the move to bedding. Ground litter then is less noisy, thanks to the night’s dampening dew. But if overnight frost has made fallen leaves ultra-brittle or has crusted the snow, better wait for some midday thawing.

Though rain or a moderate wind helps mask hunter movement sounds, strong winds are a mixed blessing. With their hearing impaired by too much wind, restless animals change location often rather than staying put. That aids the stillhunter, because moving game is far easier to see. But wind-jittery animals, up on their feet and watching for danger they can’t hear, can spot the hunter more readily. Fortunately, a stiff wind covers his extra noise if the stillhunter sticks to thicker cover for extra concealment.

Worst of all are those dead-calm Indian summer days, when even your shadow seems to make a racket in the dried-out forest. The only option, then, is to hunt terrain where game can be spotted at a considerable distance (example: from one ridge to another).

Some cover simply can’t be stillhunted. Swampy terrain is impossibly noisy, all the more if there’s any ice to be broken. Dry, fluffy snow can be fairly quiet for walking. But typical autumn snow, heavy and wet, is composed of oversize, many-pointed flakes. Each step compression-shatters untold thousands of such crystalline flakes. That accounts for the appallingly loud squeakiness. Until it’s settled, breaking its flakes in the process, such snow is no good for stillhunting. The settling process may take two or three days unless a thaw speeds it.

Snow characteristics may vary with terrain. North-facing snow is little affected by the weak sun of late autumn, but snow on southern and western slopes settles quickly with a few hours of sun.

The stillhunter must remember that weather affects game’s likely location. The first serious snowfall often causes deer to hole up in the shelter of thick, low-branching evergreens, where they may stay 48 to 72 hours, believe it or not, even during the rut. Hungry when they finally leave cover, deer then are likely to feed during the day. But until they start moving, the pristine snow will be barren of tracks, leading to the false conclusion that all game has moved out.

Of course, game does move out of high country when big snowstorms hit. Hunting deer and elk on such vertical migration routes is often considered a trail-watching game. The stillhunter can cover more territory to learn where game is moving — and if it’s moving.

Gear and clothing are critical in stillhunting. Soft leather boots with crepe soles are almost moccasin-quiet but treacherous in steep, wet or rocky terrain. Classic, Maine-style, rubber shoepacs make quiet steps. Their rounded sole edges are not as likely to shear-snap noisy twigs as stiffer, sharp-edged soles do. With any other rubber footwear, wear enough socks to prevent empty space around the foot from audibly pumping air at each step.

Harder, composition soles with lugs are necessary to maintain life and limb in rough country. Alas, such footwear is noisier. The only consolation is that in country steep enough to require lug-soled boots, the stillhunter has a longer “working range” across canyons, etc., and is not quite as vulnerable to making noise as the close-range, flatland hunter. At times, I’ve carried soft sneakers in my daypack to slip on after climbing a ridge that demanded lug-soled, ankle-supportive boots. Another useful trick is to wear a pair of knit leg warmers running from knee-height to ankles. These muffle twig noise and grass swish.

Clothing choice is very simple. Whatever the material, it must be soft. Nylon, corduroy and stiff denim are too noisy. Wool and flannel are the great favorites for quiet movement. Some synthetics like orlon and acrylic are almost as good, depending on weave and nap. Rubberized raingear makes less noise than nylon or vinyl. Because a hat’s brim snags noisy branches, wear a cap. Again, wool or knitted acrylic is quietest. I made a simple, woolen pack cover to keep my backpack from imitating a snare drum when I hunker through overhanging branches.

For quiet carrying, a firearm’s barrel length is more critical than weight per se. Although carbines are best, one of my favorite woods rifles has a 24-inch barrel. I find a 26-inch barrel is just too easy to bang against branches. A timber riflescope should have at least a 30-foot field at 100 yards — more would be better, because forest shots are often at close-range game on the run. Yellow shooting glasses contrast brown deer and elk better against evergreen backgrounds.

Pocket hardware like keys, coins and cartridges must be handkerchief-wrapped to eliminate tell-tale clinking. When I rendezvoused with my deer-hunting daughter in the woods, her sardonic greeting was, “Hello, Santa!”

“Well before I saw you,” she continued, “I could hear you playing ‘Jingle Bells’. What’s in your pockets?”

A ring of unwrapped keys, that’s what. And deer probably could hear it twice as well as she could.

Although usually not physically taxing, stillhunting is tiring if you do it right. What wears you out is the strain of constant vigilance. And I mean constant! About the time you relax or daydream, up bounds a buck or bull that makes good his escape because you weren’t mentally prepared to react quite fast enough. But if you’re a dedicated hunter, the high-tension hours of stillhunting are memorable whether you score or not.

#8393. Lucky Skier Survives Night in Crevasse With Two Broken Legs

Thirty-nine-year-old Emeric Ossatte was ski touring alone earlier this week in the Savoie area of the French Alps, near the Italian border. He removed his skis to posthole up a steep couloir in his boots and crampons when an avalanche swept him downhill, depositing him into a crevasse and breaking both his legs.

The Grenoble-area man, who wasn’t carrying a beacon or radio, doffed his backpack and crawled 100m through the crevasse, looking for a way out. But the sides of the crack were too steep, and there was no cell service where he was. He remained beside an air pocket in the crevasse overnight. Meanwhile, when he didn’t check in that evening, his family raised the alarm.

According to a Facebook report by France’s CRS Savoie mountain-rescue unit, they saw the slide path but no immediate sign of Ossatte. The search continued until the middle of the night, when avalanche risk forced the rescuers to suspend their operations.

The following morning, a large contingent of 28 ground rescuers, dogs, three helicopters, and a physician to tend to Ossatte, if they found him alive, converged on the site, according to France 3. They found his gear and dug for five hours until they heard him calling out.

“He was really lucky,” said Major Yoann Haberey of the mountain-rescue team. “We knew he was under there. But we didn’t think we’d find him alive.”

By the time they extracted him, he had been there almost a day and a half. The hypothermic but lucky survivor was flown to the hospital for treatment and surgery on one of his legs.

Jerry Kobalenko is the editor of ExplorersWeb. One of Canada’s premier arctic travelers, he is the author of The Horizontal Everest and Arctic Eden, and has just finished a book about adventures in Labrador. In 2018, he was awarded the Polar Medal by the Governor General of Canada and in 2022, he received the Queen’s Platinum Jubilee Medal for services to exploration.

#8391. Weekend Warm-Up: Behind the Climb

As the title suggests, Behind the Climb brings viewers behind the scenes with Colin Haley and Tyler Karow as they take on the Southeast Ridge of Cerro Torre. Colin Haley has tried the route, which he calls a modern classic, three times already without success. He hopes that by teaming up with Tyler Karow, the fourth time will be the charm.

As the short film opens, Haley introduces us to the route in voiceover, while on-screen, he and Karow make the approach, crossing ice fields and fording streams. The route is divided into three main sections: the Haston Crack, Ice Towers, and Headwall. Haley was turned back twice by bad weather on the Ice Towers; neither of them has finished the Headwall.

The Headwall section of Cerro Torre. Photo: Screenshot

Hoping to catch the Ice Towers still frozen and the Headwall still warmed by the sun, they wake up so early that the sun hasn’t actually finished setting yet. At that point, it seems more accurate to say that they took an evening nap and then climbed through the night.

Interspersed with clips of climbing, Haley and Karow review the footage, sharing the little logistical details and impressions of the climb. The Ice Towers, which Karow recalls attempting many years ago, have changed since he last encountered them. Once, as the name implies, thick with ice, much of it has now melted. “In another ten years,” Karow predicts, they will “look drastically different.”

Ice or no, they complete the second section. This time, the weather cooperates. Haley takes over for the final section, the dastardly Headwall which has repeatedly eluded him. The climbing itself is going well, but melting snow above is making the rock run with water. This, he admits, is a significant disadvantage to their carefully timed plan.

Despite the wet holds, Haley makes good progress. With only a few pitches to go, the self-described pessimist Haley admits that he finally thinks they’ll make it to the top. When he does finally reach the summit, grinning widely at the camera, voiceover reveals he has already started worrying about the descent.

Keeping with the promise to go behind the scenes, the video doesn’t stop at the summit, where they stand victoriously and admire the views. We follow the pair as they rappel down.

At the bottom, when asked how they feel about the adventure, both men reply simply that their hands hurt. Not all of us can be poets.

He’s interested in maritime and disaster history as well as criminal history, and his dissertation focused on the werewolf trials of early modern Europe. At the present moment he can most likely be found perusing records of shipboard crime and punishment during the Age of Sail, or failing that, writing historical fiction horror stories. He lives in Dublin and hates the sun.

#8389. Pipeline World Titles On The Line: Every Medina, Dora, Moore and Picklum Scenario Explained

#8388. Kayaking 3,700km Around Britain

Dominic Gribbin, 61, is more than five months into his attempt to circumnavigate mainland Great Britain by sea kayak. But relentless weather delays and mounting costs have made the journey even more challenging than anticipated.

Gribbin launched his expedition from Crinan Harbour in Argyll, Scotland, on June 2. Paddling clockwise, he has covered 2,584km. From Crinan, he continued up the west coast of Scotland and around Cape Wrath and Duncansby Head before heading south along the eastern and southern coasts. His latest reported landing point is Cliff End, near Hastings, Sussex.

The journey has not gone according to plan. Persistent Atlantic weather systems have cost him weeks of paddling time, while the financial demands of keeping the expedition moving have forced him to return to freelance television camerawork so he and his wife, Nicola, can keep it running.

While he paddles, she follows him in their motor home, offering shore support. He now hopes to complete the circle at Crinan in the spring of 2027. ExplorersWeb caught up with him to find out more about the journey. This interview has been edited for brevity and clarity.

How do you approach the daily distances on a journey like this?

Given that circumnavigating the British mainland is at least 3,700km, I have aimed to do at least 40km per day. My longest day so far has been 64km. However, weather, tides and daylight length, particularly in autumn and winter, often curtail one’s best-laid plans. Some days have been necessarily shorter, forcing me to make an earlier-than-envisaged retreat to shore.

But this is very much a journey, not a race, so we will continue into the spring of 2027. It’s an incredible opportunity to explore every nook and cranny of the GB coastline.

How much has the weather affected your progress?

Roughly 85% of delays have been due to poor weather, 5% to Ministry of Defense firing range timetable restrictions, and 10% to the need for very specific tidal windows to navigate particular sections safely and efficiently. I have never had a delay due to fatigue or the need for a break from the sheer physical nature of this endurance challenge. I have used days when I have inevitably been “weathered-off” to recoup my energy reserves.

For most of the trip, it has been extremely rare to have more than four days of consistently good paddling weather. Setting off from Crinan on June 2 meant we did not enjoy the heat wave experienced in southern Britain. Instead, we lost at least six weeks to poor weather. Constant Atlantic westerly and northwesterly fronts brought wind, rain, and high seas that seemed to permanently batter northwest and northern Scotland throughout June, July, and even into early August. It was one of the great untalked-about weather phenomena of summer 2026. It wasn’t all heat waves…at least not in Scotland!

Are you camping or sleeping in the motorhome?

Northwest Scotland and its wild, deeply indented coastline often made road access to the sea impossible. Early on, we accepted that there would be long stretches when Nicola would not see me for over 24 hours. She was often doing double the mileage I was doing and negotiating some very hairy driving conditions on roads way off the beaten track. That resulted in many planned and unplanned wild camps along the coast.

By the time we reached the north coast of Scotland, road access had improved dramatically, and Nicola and I were generally able to coordinate to meet at the end of each day. This has proved an invaluable motivator, especially when the going gets tough. Our little motorhome has been a blessed retreat each night since then: a warm, reassuring space to relax, gather one’s thoughts, and plan ahead.

The route so far. The blue line indicates the paddling route, the red line shows the route taken by the support vehicle. Image: Dominic Gribbin

What kayak are you paddling, and how much equipment do you carry?

I am paddling around Britain in a Rockpool Taran 16 sea kayak. It is a fast, beautifully handmade sea kayak, perfect for putting down the miles and handling heavy seas. The trade-off is stability, but it has a prodigious load capacity in its various watertight bulkheads.

I carry a kayak trolley permanently on the back deck. I have already worn out two and am constantly experimenting with new designs to better negotiate the challenges of gravel and sand beaches. As a minimum, I carry basic wild-camping equipment in case I need to spend an unexpected night ashore.

Food consists of pre-cooked rice bags, canned fish and vegetables, alongside plenty of snacks. I favor savory rather than sweet food. Coffee is my essential luxury, and I normally carry a can or two of beer!

Safety equipment is extensive. I carry a Garmin 86i Marine GPS with InReach satellite tracking, an ICOM marine handheld VHF radio with AIS and GPS, a spare VHF radio, flares and smoke signals, and a McMurdo personal locator beacon. I also carry a fully integrated Garmin Fenix 7 Pro watch, first-aid kit, spare paddles, compass, mobile phone, battery packs, head lamp, repair tape, and a Swiss Army penknife.

The kayak itself weighs 24.9kg, but the overall weight, including my dry suit, buoyancy aid, clothing, and equipment, easily reaches 35kg or more. It is a significant challenge to negotiate all this weight over an often-unfriendly shoreline. However, the kayak benefits from the additional weight when afloat.

What have been the most memorable moments along the way?

We have had numerous encounters with sea otters, hunting pods of bottlenose dolphins, and close-up encounters with bottlenose mothers and calves. There have also been golden eagles and white-tailed sea eagles, the largest raptor in the UK. Some of the most sublime moments have been the inshore sea passages through the sheltered waters of the Inner Hebrides.

Successfully rounding Cape Wrath was a truly intimidating but awe-inspiring milestone. Then there was rounding the infamous Duncansby Head and crossing the Pentland Firth near John O’Groats, finally seeing the compass turn south. The huge breeding seabird colonies off Caithness and much of Scotland’s northeast coast have been incredible. Literally tens of thousands of birds, a deafening cacophony of noise!

Right along the GB coastline, we have also met some of the warmest and most generous people.

Have there been any particularly frightening moments?

The huge seas off Strathy Point, Caithness, are probably the closest call yet. If something had gone wrong there while two kilometers offshore in those seas, I wouldn’t be writing this.

I have experienced plenty of rough and scary seas so far, but that occasion was another order of magnitude. They were enormous seas driven by Arctic swell and spring tides. They were the biggest seas I have ever paddled in. I am not in a rush to return there, despite its beauty.

You have mentioned that you are now combining freelance work with the expedition. How are you managing the financial and logistical side?

We were able to make this happen because I am self-employed. We bankrolled the expedition using our savings. The motorhome for shore support was the biggest investment. We initially calculated finances based on finishing by mid-autumn, but those weather delays cost us dearly. We ran out of money by mid-September, so I have had to return to my freelance work as a TV cameraman and juggle that with keeping this Edge of Britain expedition going until we are finished.

There is no stopping this winter. Instead, we will take advantage of every weather window.

I began paddling at age 11 when I permanently borrowed a friend’s old kayak and started exploring the ankle-deep waters of the River Ver and River Colne around St. Albans and Park Street, Hertfordshire. At 13, I started British Canoe Union training. This began my lifelong involvement with whitewater paddling on rivers around the world.

Back in the 1970s and 1980s, when I began, skills progression was funneled entirely through very actively encouraged participation in canoe slalom. Everybody who learned to paddle at club level was involved in slalom to some degree. I chose men’s C1 (Canadian singles) and competed at a national level, ranking 19th in Great Britain by the age of 18.

University changed my orientation somewhat, and the allure of traveling to exotic whitewater rivers around the world took over. Expedition whitewater paddling then became my life, and still is, alongside expedition sea kayaking. Combining this with filmmaking ultimately led directly to my career as a television cameraman.

Why did you decide to take on a circumnavigation of Britain?

The decision to attempt to paddle around the GB mainland is the culmination of a life spent kayaking. It is hard not to fall in love with the sheer beauty and allure of the world of the river and coastline, particularly here at home in the UK. This country has what is widely celebrated as some of the best sea kayaking in the world. To dream of exploring the whole GB coastline in as much detail as time and weather allow is, to me, a logical conclusion.

The actual decision came extremely late in the day, namely January 2026. This began a frantic period of organization ahead of our June 2 launch. Admittedly, April would have been better, but prior commitments meant we had to begin in June.

I chose a clockwise route around Britain, thinking (naively) that the prevailing winds would vaguely be behind us for at least half the journey. I also wanted to tackle the potentially most technically demanding section of the northwest and north coast of Scotland early, in the height of what I envisaged as long summer days. How wrong I was!

Rebecca McPhee is a freelance writer for ExplorersWeb.

Rebecca has been writing about open water sports, adventure travel, and marine science for three years. Prior to that, Rebecca worked as an Editorial Assistant at Taylor and Francis, and a Wildlife Officer for ORCA.

Based in the UK Rebecca is a science teacher and volunteers for a number of marine charities. She enjoys open water swimming, hiking, diving, and traveling.

#8386. The Pentagon Wants Public Lands ‘Offered Up’ for a New Army Base. Wyoming Is Interested

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 19, 2026 6:00 PM EST

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

U.S. Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth wants to build a new military base, and some public land states like Wyoming say they have just the spots.

The base would house between 15,000 and 25,000 active-duty service members, according to a red, white and blue competition flier for what Hegseth is calling the Next Great American Base contest. And ideally it would include 100,000 to 250,000 contiguous acres.

Hegseth said the base should be a “dynamic, self-sustaining power projection platform” with its own power generation, modern air defenses, and advanced technology, reported Newsweek in early October, when the call for pitches first arrived. He went on to say the best proposals would cut through “the red tape, offering up the land and rolling out the red carpet for our military families.”

But much of the “red tape” associated with building a base on hundreds of thousands of acres of unoccupied land is likely federal government red tape. And no clear path currently exists for how to requisition and build a new military installation on federal public land, said Mark Squillace, a natural resources law professor at the University of Colorado Law School.

The Department of Defense would need to work around current Bureau of Land Management or U.S. Forest Service management plans that include stipulations for wildlife like migration corridors and winter range closures.

It’s also unclear if the base needs to support the U.S. Navy, which would limit public-land options since the bulk of the country’s public lands are in the Interior West like Nevada, Idaho and Utah, though the fact sheet says all locations, even those without water access, are on the table.

The DOD already administers 8.4 million acres in the U.S., but is looking to acquire lands managed by other agencies.

Wyoming, for its part, is eyeing both state and federal land for the base. The state’s adjutant general, Maj. Gen. Greg Porter, told a legislative committee in early November that Wyoming has identified at least four “chunks” of state and federal land, as reported by the Cowboy State Daily last week. Which chunks have not yet been disclosed.

Squillace lined out how building a military base on public land could — or could not — become a reality.

The DOD would first likely need to discuss any public land acquisition with the BLM or Forest Service. If the land use agency agrees that a military base would fit the current land management plan, it would need to prepare a draft Environmental Impact Statement that brings up potential complications and confirms compliance with laws like the Endangered Species Act or National Historic Preservation Act.

“A positive review would likely lead to temporary withdrawal of the land from the operation of certain public land laws (like mining and leasing) that could interfere with the operation of the base,” Squillace says, while also noting that existing rights would be protected. Any particularly valuable mining claims would have to be bought out in what he describes as a “time consuming and expensive” process.

Read Next: Senate Poised to Vote on Affordability Package that Hunters, Anglers Worry Is a Trojan Horse for Public-Land Rollbacks

Ultimately, however, Squillace says any large-scale, long-term use of land would likely need to be approved by Congress, “since such transfers of public land are not authorized under law.”  Whether or not the dramatically different Congress to be sworn in next year would green light a public-land transfer requested by Hegseth also remains uncertain.

Christine Peterson is a freelance writer based in southeast Wyoming covering hunting, fishing, outdoor recreation, wildlife and the environment for Outdoor Life, High Country News, National Geographic and others.

#8385. No-Oxygen Team Will Try Again on Everest

Last week, high winds forced the Russian team to retreat from their third trip up the West Ridge of Everest. Sergey Kondrashkin, Vitaly Shiilov, and leader Andrei Vasiliev were climbing without oxygen, with no one else on the mountain. Winter is quickly approaching, but they have no intention of going home yet.

“We are at Lobuche…resting, analyzing the weather forecast. Still want to try again,” Vasiliev texted to ExplorersWeb over the weekend. The climbers had not intended to reach the summit on that latest attempt up the West Shoulder of Everest via the Lho La Pass. Vasiliev explained that they were not yet acclimatized enough to reach the top without bottled oxygen. They did expect to reach higher than before, but high winds made that impossible.

In a post on social media, member Sergey Kondrashkin recalls the latest trip up. On November 11, the team set off from their tent at the base of the West Ridge, fixing ropes in bitterly cold temperature. Contrary to forecasts, winds were light. The climbers pitched their tent at 6,500m and still had time to fix some ropes above them.

On the following morning, however, the gusts were too strong. After waiting in vain for the gale to abate, and with a forecast of even stronger winds the following day, Kondrashkin returned to the ridge, trying not to be blown away. He advanced between gusts and retrieved his two ice axes and the rope he had left behind the day before. They would need that gear for the descent.

Once at the bivouac, the climbers retrieved their tent, cached some food and fuel, and downclimbed to their Camp 1 at some 5,600m. It lies on the steep section between the Khumbu Glacier and the Lho La. Here, they pitched their tent again.

On Friday, the Russian team rappelled down to the glacier, on a “relatively safe” line to the right, where an avalanche fell as they descended.

Today, the team sits at their lodge in Lobuche and looks intently at weather forecasts. “We have not yet decided when to return to the mountain,” Vasiliev told Explorersweb. “It’s all about the weather, and the forecasts announce high winds on November 20-21.”

#8382. Officials Blame Climate Change for Glacier Collapse That Killed 17

Russian authorities are now attributing the September 6 disaster on 5,047m Mount Mizhirgi in the northern Caucasus to climate-related destabilization of a hanging glacier.

A half-million cubic meter avalanche struck a military group undergoing mountain training, killing 17 and injuring 10. Another 10 were unharmed and made it down the mountain on their own.

The impact generated a powerful blast of displaced air that reportedly threw some victims 200m to 300m down the slope before burying them. As we reported at the time, helicopter pilot Alexander Davydov caught the avalanche on video.

Eldar Khashkhozhev of the Russian Ministry of Emergency Situations explained that warmer temperatures melted the protective blanket of snow, exposing parts of the glacier. This weakened the ice support that had glued enormous masses of snow and ice in place for decades.

A helicopter inspection of the region’s snow conditions the month before found no cause for concern, and some officials insist the catastrophe was unpredictable. However, climber Dmitry Polyakov, who was in the region at the time, disputed the idea that the glacier posed no danger. He said climbers had long recognized its instability and generally avoided approaching it in large groups.

The Mizhirgi Glacier against Mizhirgi East and West peaks, site of September’s catastrophic glacial collapse. Photo: Shutterstock

The key points of Khashkhozhev’s explanation were:

Rising temperatures have raised the altitude of permanent snow cover.

Previously protected sections of the glacier became exposed, weakening the supporting ice beneath the hanging masses.

Eventually, gravity overcame that support and triggered a collapse that struck the training group.

This was the largest climbing disaster in the country since 2002, when another glacier collapse in North Ossetia, located between the Black and Caspian Seas, killed 125 people, including local residents.

With major landslides in summer and the possibility of glacial collapse at other times of the year throughout the world, mountaineers will have to adjust their behavior, as previously safe areas become potentially deadly.

#8380. ‘Complete Shock.’ Long-Haul Trucker Hunter Tags 227-Inch Nontypical During State Park Hunt

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Updated Nov 19, 2026 6:37 PM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

Indiana’s state park system offers a limited draw hunt that allows residents to target the abundant whitetails on park land that doesn’t get much hunting pressure. There are about 25 parks in the popular program, with two firearms hunts allowed on most of them. Each hunt runs just two days.

“I put in for the early two-day hunt on November 16 and 17 at a Northeast Indiana state park and got drawn for it,” Mike Miltroka tells Outdoor Life. “Trail cameras are not allowed on park [land]. But you can scout before hunt days. I scouted the park on Sunday, the day before the first day of hunting on November 16.”

Miltroka found a likely spot and, well before daybreak on Monday, he was in a tree saddle with his Ruger American Generation II chambered in 7mm PRC.

“It was cool and a little windy, but I was seeing deer soon after daybreak,” says Miltroka, a 38-year-old long-haul truck driver from Farmland. “I spotted a good buck far out, but the cover was too thick to shoot.”

As the morning wore on, Miltroka got a chance to take two does, and he dropped them both with his Ruger. He toted both does to his truck using a deer cart, since motorized vehicles are not allowed off-road.

The spot was already paying off, and Miltroka had a friend — who had seen a giant deer using the area before the hunt — to thank for tipping him off about it.

“I’d never seen the deer previously, but a buddy showed me a video [he’d taken] of the buck a month earlier while visiting the park … It was a place no one else was hunting, and I checked to make sure it was okay to hunt there – and it was.”

On the second day of the hunt, Miltroka chose a different tree. He climbed into a big walnut closer to the cover where he’d seen the buck on Monday. But unlike the day before, Miltroka didn’t see much except a coyote. Then, around 8:30, things changed.

“I heard something raking trees behind me, and I didn’t know what it was. I kept trying to see what was making all that racket, then I saw him out about 70 yards,” he says. “I knew it was a good shooter buck, and when I got him in my scope I squeezed the trigger, fired, and the deer took off running.”

Miltroka jacked another round into the chamber, but as soon as he picked up the running buck in his scope again, the deer went down.

It wasn’t until Miltroka studied the dead buck through his scope that he realized how immense the deer was.

“I started shaking, and yelling,” he says. “I got buck fever after it was over … I called a friend of mine and was so excited he asked if I was tied tight to my tree, and I said yes. I was shaking so much I might have fallen out of it.”

Once he was steady enough, Miltroka climbed down and walked up on the deer.

“I was in complete shock,” he says. “I’ve only taken about six bucks, and none even have the size of this one. I was screaming with joy.”

He filled out a transport tag for his buck, which filled his limit of three deer for the park hunt – only one of which can be a buck. Then he wheeled the buck to his vehicle, loaded it, and drove to park headquarters to check it in.

The buck’s field dressed weight was 181 pounds. The 22-point nontypical was green scored by an official Boone & Crockett measurer the day after Miltroka shot it. It gross scored 227 inches, with a net score of 197 6/8s inches. It has an outside antler spread of nearly 25 inches, and one main beam of the rack measures over 27 inches.

He will have the buck scored again after the required 60-day drying period for B&C and will enter the buck into B&C’s nontypical record category. He’ll also submit his buck to the Hoosier Record Buck Program.

“The park hunts are well run and there are plenty of deer,” says Miltroka. “I saw lots of deer being checked in during the hunts and some nice bucks.

Read Next: ‘Over the Moon.’ Ohio Hunter Shoots 215-Inch Buck from Her Favorite Stand on Her Birthday

“I’m stunned at the size of my deer. But there are so many great bucks that have come from Indiana.”
