#8374. I Went Duck Hunting During the Infamous Armistice Day Storm, and Lived to Tell About It

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 20, 2026 9:00 AM EST

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

This story, “Cold as Death,” appeared in the October 1955 issue of Outdoor Life.

There has never been anything along the upper Mississippi, before or since, like the Armistice Day storm of 1940.

It started out as a mild, cloudy morning with the temperature between 50 and 60. Fellows went duck hunting in shirt sleeves. The wind picked up velocity as the day went along, but not enough to get excited about — not until late afternoon. Then, with little warning, a full-scale prairie blizzard screamed in out of the northwest.

By dark the air was smoking with gale-driven snow, and the thermometer plunged near zero. Bitter winds blew all night. The weather experts said afterward that gusts hit 80 miles an hour. The storm laid a track of death and destruction across six states; it pounded ships to pieces on the Great Lakes, blocked roads, isolated towns, paralyzed cities, and buried cars. Hundreds were marooned on highways. A crack streamliner was 27 hours late into Minneapolis. And when the morning of November 12 broke, 20 duck hunters lay dead, frozen in stiff, grotesque poses — scattered on mud bars, sand spits, and willow-grown islands of the Mississippi bottoms between Red Wing, Minnesota, and La Crosse, Wisconsin.

I was among the luckier ones. I spent that terrible night with two companions under an overturned skiff on a mud bar — wet from head to foot, without fire or heavy clothing, pants frozen to my legs, and kept alive only by the body warmth of the men who lay huddled on both sides of me. The storm cost me six weeks of agony in a hospital and half of my left foot. But at least I survived.

I was 14 at the time, attending high school in my home town of Winona, Minnesota. We got out of classes at 11 o’clock because it was Armistice Day. I scurried for home, planning an afternoon of duck hunting.

The wind was already fairly fresh and mother was uneasy. “The storm warnings are up,” she told me while getting my lunch. But I grew up in Winona and knew the river, and at that age, bad weather didn’t worry me. The only thing it meant to me was good shooting. Ducks had been scarce all fall and maybe a blow would bring ’em down. So I hurried through lunch, impatient to get in a blind.

While I was loading my gear in the car, my cousin, Bob Stephens, showed up with a partner, Cal Wieczorek, and they decided to join me. Bob was 20, Cal about 19.

We drove to the Prairie Island spillway at the lower end of the Winona pool, where Dad kept his boat. It was a 16-footer, pointed at both ends, with a homemade bracket for an outboard. We decided to hunt a place known as the Firing Line, along the border of the Upper Mississippi Wildlife Refuge, three or four miles upstream. Hunters lined up there to intercept ducks flying in and out of the refuge. In stormy weather the pass-shooting was exceptionally good, and this looked like that kind of day.

The wind was blowing half a gale now and was beginning to bite. Ominous dark clouds raced overhead, and scuds of cold rain pelted down as we clamped our old three-horse outboard on the boat, cranked it up, and headed out into the choppy pool. The three of us were a heavy load, and the skiff made slow headway against the wind.

We didn’t give it a thought then, but we couldn’t have been more poorly prepared. I was wearing cotton clothes — pants, shirt, socks, and a jacket lighter than a hunting coat. I had overshoes over my leather shoes. Bob and Cal were dressed similarly, except for hip boots. Cal and I had gloves. Bob had a heavy raincoat, and I still believe that made the difference between living and dying for me.

We had one book of matches and no pocketknife. I was carrying a 16 gauge single-barrel Winchester; the other two had pumps. Because we intended to pass-shoot, we took no decoys.

Backed up by a series of low dams, the Mississippi is two to three miles wide in that section, flooding its bottoms and forming open lakes, meandering channels, small islands, bars, and rush beds. We threaded our way through this maze, wallowing in the heavy seas. It took us 1½ hours to cover the distance to the Firing Line.

The sky was full of ducks; they were barreling across the bottoms in crazy skeins, riding the wind, and turning and twisting below treetop height. It was plain they didn’t like the weather. Before the afternoon was over I actually swung at a greenhead with my gun as he hovered above my head, almost stationary, while bucking the gale.

When we finally got to the Firing Line it was too crowded. The blinds were only about 25 yards apart and all occupied. Ducks were rocketing back and forth over the marsh and there was a steady barrage of shooting. We decided to find another spot.

By the time we found it — on a willow-covered island with many rushes in front — we were beginning to lose interest. The wind was howling like a banshee, down off the high bluffs to the west, with sleet and fine snow in its teeth. We hunkered in the willows for almost an hour, killed four ducks, and decided we’d had enough. The cold was already bone-chilling, our hands were so stiff we could hardly handle our guns, and we were starting to worry about getting back to the landing.

Two strangers came by in a little duck boat; they told us they were scared. That settled it for us, but going home was easier said than done. None of us knew exactly where we were, and when we tried to shove away from shore, the seas threatened to swamp us. We finally dragged the skiff to the lee side and managed to get it afloat and start the motor. But before going 200 yards we ran aground on a mud bar, which we couldn’t skirt because of the wind and seas. We dragged the boat across the bar and started on again.

We did that about four times before finding ourselves by a big open slough and admitting we were lost. It was almost dark now and snowing so hard we couldn’t see 100 feet. We couldn’t even look into the wind — the storm cut our eyeballs like a sandblast. And the slough was a welter of dirty foaming water in which our skiff probably wouldn’t last more than a minute.

While we debated what to do, another boat loomed out of the stormy dusk, riding low with four hunters. Two of them were only a little older than I — one from my home room at school. The others were men who had hunted the river bottoms all their lives. When they said they knew the way to Prairie Island and could make it without swamping, we decided to follow.

But I couldn’t get the outboard started. Bob and Cal rowed, handicapped by cold-numbed hands, while I tinkered with it. We managed to keep the other boat in sight for a few minutes. Then, pitching and rolling, it disappeared and we didn’t see it again. When our frantic yells brought no answer we realized we were again on our own. It was days before we learned what happened to the other party. Their skiff had capsized when we lost sight of it. Three of the hunters died there, standing in the icy water, not knowing which way to go to dry land. By some miracle one of the boys survived the night and rescue parties found him on a near-by island at daybreak.

Only a few minutes after we lost that boat we were washed ashore on a sand spit. Bob and Cal leaped clear as we grounded and escaped a wetting. I was in the stern of the skiff, working on the outboard — right in the path of a wicked sea that smashed over us the instant we touched bottom.

I can’t describe the shock that icy dunking gave me. I clawed my way out of the swamped boat, and the wind cut like a braided whip. My legs wouldn’t hold me up, so I crawled across the mud until I was beyond reach of the waves and lay there, cold and weak.

Cal and Bob dragged the boat up on dry land, turned it over, and propped one side up to make a shelter. When they finished I crawled under it. My pants and jacket crackled as I moved. They had frozen stiff in the few minutes I lay exposed to the wind.

We still had only a vague idea of our location, but we knew that large open lake, which we couldn’t cross in our puny skiff, lay between us and any landing. We didn’t talk much about it; we didn’t need to. Live or die, we’d have to spend the night where we were.

Bob and Cal did everything possible. They gathered rushes and put them under the boat to keep me off the wet mud. Then they searched desperately for firewood, but there wasn’t so much as a scrub willow. They finally found a few pieces of water-logged driftwood, heaped them in a pyramid, and turned the outboard motor up to drain gas on it — a risky thing to do, except in a desperate emergency like ours. But the screaming wind whipped the gas into nowhere as fast as it ran out of the tank. They struck one match after another without getting a spark of fire.

The last match flared and died in the darkness, and they crawled under the boat and lay down on the wet rushes, one on each side of me. They spread their hunting coats over us and laid Bob’s raincoat on top. The raincoat kept the worst of the wind out and probably saved me from freezing.

We had little sense of time and hardly talked. Bob told me afterward that I spoke only two or three times during the night, and then I worried about the anxiety my parents must be feeling.

I shivered for what seemed like hours, with my hands wedged between my thighs, since that seemed to be the warmest place on my body. I prayed silently. I didn’t think much about dying. I believed we’d get through the night somehow and in the morning find our way home. I realized finally that all feeling was gone in my feet; they were wooden stumps, no longer a part of me. I must have dozed, for I recall waking up and feeling warm, but that lasted only a minute or two and then I was shivering again.

The thing I remember best was the unending scream of the wind. It never stopped raging — searching under the overturned boat, wailing, screeching, terribly alive, terribly cold.

Sometime in the night Bob remembered that he had two apples and two sandwiches in his hunting coat. They were frozen hard, but we gnawed at them and they seemed to help.

The sound of distant gunshots reached us several times, and once we thought we heard faint, far-off yells for help. But our own guns were so crusted with ice that we couldn’t fire them, and it was useless to shout.

We knew we weren’t the only hunters lost on the river bottom. Others were enduring the same torture. And we felt sure there must be rescue parties trying to find us, but we didn’t think we could count on them while the storm continued.

It was days before we learned all that happened that hard night. Rescue efforts began at dark, and men fought the storm and seas all night in skiffs and powerboats. Some even waded the sloughs to bring lost men to shore. It wasn’t a night for small craft, and no one will ever know how many boats were overturned, swamped, and driven back. But the search went on, hour after hour, heedless of the wind, cold, and snow.

The night was an awful ordeal for the hunters. One party huddled on a marshy island beside a fire started with dead grass and fed with more than 40 cedar decoys. Gas from an outboard was used to rekindle the blaze each time it died down. Another group marched, jumped, and sparred around a fire built from their boats. Still another found a patch of timber, got a fire going, and then shot dead branches off trees for fuel.

They were the lucky ones; others didn’t fare so well. Two died under an overturned boat, unable to see a bonfire 400 yards away. Two more reached shore along a railroad embankment, crawled halfway up the bank, fell and froze to death, too weak to walk to safety. Others gave up, lay down where they were, without fire or shelter, and died quickly. A few drowned. Most froze. When the last of the living were rescued, the grim search for bodies continued. It was three days before all the missing were accounted for and the final toll of 20 dead was tallied.

Toward morning the air cleared enough to let me glimpse lights on the Winona dam, a few miles away. They glimmered through the driving snow, tantalizing and mocking us, but at least we knew now which direction to go when daylight came. About that same time Bob remarked that his hands were frozen and he’d probably lose them. He said it quietly, as if it didn’t really matter.

At first light the wind seemed to lose its gale force. It was still blowing hard, but when we studied the lake in the gray light of morning, we decided our skiff might weather the seas.

We were in bad shape. Cal and Bob could barely stand up; I needed help to get on my feet. Our hands were stiff and useless. The spit where we spent the night was rimmed with a 50-foot belt of ice that made launching our boat a tough job. But we didn’t have much choice. We couldn’t live through the day on that bleak mud bar, and we had no way of knowing when help would come.

I crawled into the boat. Bob and Cal inched it off the bar, broke a path in the ice to open water, and rowed slowly into the lake. Their hands, however, were too numb for the oars and they lost control when the full force of wind and waves hit us.

Spray froze to the skiff, coating it with ice. My clothes were as stiff as armor, my legs lost all feeling, and I no longer minded the wind. In that condition, with the boat growing ever heavier from its load of ice, we were swept across the Winona pool to the Wisconsin side.

That should have ended things, but there was still more torment ahead. The marsh had frozen and we grounded on the new ice, 100 yards offshore. The ice gouged a big hole in the bow of the skiff, but it wouldn’t bear a man’s weight. Luckily the wind drove us up until the hole was above water, and there we sat with no way to get to shore.

It was there a search plane found us an hour later. Part of the feverish search that was under way for 50 miles up and down the river, that plane — a Cub trainer piloted by Max Conrad of Winona — had been in the air since shortly after daylight. It was shuttling across the bottoms, spotting lost hunters, empty boats, deserted gear and stranded dogs, and dropping sandwiches, whisky, matches, and cigarettes. It even dipped low enough to shout encouragement to the men as they were found. Nobody in the rescue teams did a better job.

Conrad flew low over us, wagging his wings, and hollered a message we couldn’t understand. Then he was gone, but before long we heard the steady throb of a powerboat and saw the Army Engineers’ launch, Chippewa.

She couldn’t reach us, for we were out of the river channel in shallow water. It took the crew almost an hour to break ice and get to us with a skiff. Before they were through, my dad and a friend of his, who had been looking for us all night, showed up in a powerful outboard. They carried me to the launch, peeled off my shoes and frozen clothing, and for all I was only 14, gave me a man-size slug of whisky. I took it like a man, too, and never knew I’d had it.

Bob spent a week in a Winona hospital, but his frozen hands came through all right. Cal went home that same afternoon. I had a long bout with gangrene and lost part of a foot. I also lost a lot of weight, dropping from 143 to 85 pounds during my recovery. Christmas was less than a week away when I left the hospital, and I guess that was the best Christmas I ever had.

I’m a teacher in La Crosse now and I still hunt ducks on the Mississippi bottoms whenever I get the chance. But, I wear special boots, even on the warmest days, and take along an emergency kit that contains wool socks, gloves, down-filled hat, compass, knife, matches in watertight container, and a small flare gun. I never go without it.

There are too many mistakes in this world to make the same one twice, and any time I’m tempted to be careless, I remember how the wind sounded that awful November night.

#8370. The Best Gifts for Anglers: The Fishing Gear They Actually Want

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

What do you get someone who loves fishing, but has a ton of gear and is very particular about their rods, reels, and lures? What’s the right gift for someone who wants to start fishing?

I’m a lifelong angler and a gear reviewer so I can help you with both of those scenarios. I put together a list of seven gifts and four stocking stuffers that are guaranteed to make any angler happy. Best of all, they don’t require any special knowledge to buy and start at just $9.

The Victorinox Fisherman is a classic Swiss Army Knife™ designed specifically for anglers. Besides the Fisherman insignia on the side plate, you can tell that this tool was built for  the water by its fish scaler and hook disgorger. Other tools include a large and small knife blade, can opener, 3mm screw driver,  bottle opener, wire stripper, and more. This Swiss-made tool is perfect for throwing in a tackle box or a boat. Besides fishing tasks, this Victorinox will help you with all kinds of everyday jobs. It’s a versatile tool that the angler on your list will use every day. The Fisherman includes a lifetime warranty.

You’ll notice there aren’t rods, reels, and lures in this gift guide. That’s because they’re too specific to techniques and species to even try to pick the right one. But, one thing that every angler needs is more tackle storage. I promise this isn’t the fishing version of a pair of socks. It doesn’t matter if you’re shopping for someone who deep jigs tuna or flips jigs for bass, everyone who fishes has a never ending fascination with lures. We also genuinely enjoy organizing our beloved lures and appreciate smart designs for carrying them to the dock. That’s why the Bass Pro Shops XPS Backpack is a can’t miss gift.

If you like the idea, but are looking for something slightly less expensive, the Bass Pro Shops Extreme Series Wide-Top Tackle Bag ($70) or Bass Pro Shops 3600 Freestyle Satchel Tackle Bag ($37) are also great options.

Did you know you can still buy that singing bass from the 90s? It’s sure to get a laugh from any one who likes fishing and was born before 1990.

These bamboo sun hoodies are incredibly comfortable and protect skin from the sun. They come in a wide variety of colors and sizes too. Free Fly also makes a great selection of toddler and youth-sized clothing that’s worth checking out.

Fishing with kids can be more frustration than fun. The folks at Kid Casters aim to solve that dilemma by reducing tangles and wind knots with their fishing pole designed just for kids. Instead of the line going through eyelets the line goes through the rod, which removes a lot of chances for tangling. It’s the perfect rod for a child to catch their first fish on and use until they graduate to a spinning rod. Read our full review: Kid Casters Review

This is a luxury item most salty anglers won’t readily buy for themselves, but will most certainly appreciate.

Rain gear is a must-have for any serious angler. Most of us have several sets of jackets and bibs and can always use more. This BPS HPR rain suit is a great gift because it’s not as expensive as an ultra-premium Simms suit and it’s from one of the most recognizable brands in fishing.

If you want to get someone a gift they’ll remember for a lifetime, get them a fishing experience. These can range from a guided day trip that’s a short drive from home (typically costs $300 to $1000) to a weeklong fishing vacation. Guides will have all the gear needed to catch fish, which makes this a great option for new anglers too.

#8367. Never-Say-Die Climbers Will Try Everest Again This Weekend

Despite a forecast of high winds on November 21 and 22, Andrei Vasiliev, Sergey Kondrashkin, and Vitaly Shipilov have decided to begin a new attempt on the West Ridge of Everest on Sunday.

The three stubborn Russians will again proceed via the West Ridge without bottled oxygen, support, or any other living soul on the mountain.

The forecasts warn of very high winds all weekend. Nevertheless, they will start up on Sunday, November 22, although that is the day with the strongest winds forecast.

“That day, we will remain low on the mountain and climb to our Camp 1 at 5,600m,” Sergey Kondrashkin wrote.  “From there, we will push upward when the weather improves and the winds die down, aiming to descend around November 28.”

This means the team does not intend to summit quite yet. The goal is to acclimatize by reaching 7,500m and then return to their headquarters in Lobuche.

“The question is, will we manage to complete the summit push within the time frame of our permit, which expires on December 15, and will the weather cooperate?” said Kondrashkin.

The fall season on Everest usually lasts until the end of November, since December 1 marks the start of Nepal’s winter climbing season. However, the permit dates may have been extended for this team, which is climbing off the normal route.

Once winter settles in, it will be the turn of Jost Kobusch of Germany, who will attempt to solo Everest in winter along the same route the Russians are now attempting.

On their third and latest trip up the mountain so far, the team only reached 6,700m before hurricane-force winds up to 130kph forced them down, according to former team member Natalyia Belyankina. Belyankina had to leave the expedition for health reasons but has stayed in touch with the others.

#8364. Hunting Like a Cheesehead Is the Best Way to Spend Your Deer Season

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 20, 2026 10:32 AM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

Author’s Note: This story was originally published in 2020, and Gramps is gone now. After his funeral, we buried his ashes in the woods beneath his stand. A small cross my dad built sits atop the hill where he shot the buck in this story. I think of our hunt that morning, and all of the good times spent hunting and fishing with my grandpa, every time that I pass it.

The boom comes from the southeast, and it’s loud enough to startle me in my treestand. On opening day of deer season in northern Wisconsin, the woods come alive with gunfire: soft pops in the distance, sharp cracks from the neighbors’ properties, and hurried four- or five-shot strings from someone hammering away with a lever gun at deer bounding across a cornfield. But a bang loud enough to reverberate off the tree I’m sitting in? That has to be Grampa’s .300 Win. Mag.

This is my twentieth season hunting our family land—a few hundred acres of mixed timber where we pack in four or five Robinsons each fall weekend to hunt deer. Because there’s limited room to roam, we all have designated stands that change only occasionally from year to year. Gramps has been hunting the same 3 acres for more than a decade.

This deer hunting strategy—sitting in your stand day after day until you kill a deer or the season ends—is the polar ­opposite of the highly mobile trend of tree saddles and cellular trail cameras. Today’s run-and-gun deer hunter must be agile and adaptive, constantly reading conditions and efficiently changing setups. And sure, that style of hunting can be fun and effective. But at our camp, change comes more slowly.

A few years ago, for example, I tried to get everyone to use trail cameras. While they all agreed it was cool to see pictures of deer and other wildlife, the consensus was that the cameras kind of ruined the magic of it all. My dad said that if he knew a big buck was out there, then killed a smaller buck, he might feel disappointed. And when I brought a new pour-over coffeemaker to camp, I upset the long tradition of our blue enamel percolators, which either take too long to perk or heat too quickly and boil over. Even though they’re the source of constant bickering each morning, I was ridiculed as some kind of newfangled French barista. ­(Explaining that the pour-over method was invented by a German woman in the 1900s didn’t help my case.)

Two years ago, Gramps shot a small buck with his .300 but didn’t have the gun mounted solidly to his shoulder. The scope slammed into his face and busted his nose so badly he had to check into the ER to stop the bleeding; that summer, my dad and my uncle Pat tried to talk Gramps into shooting a .243 for deer hunting. But on opening weekend Gramps had his big .300 back at camp.

“Ack, don’t be wimps,” he said, ignoring everyone’s looks of concern. “It’s just a little kick.”

Ack, don’t be wimps. It’s just a little kick.

You could view this never-change ­attitude as a special kind of stubbornness. Or, more generously, the best kind of perseverance. This is the same attitude that has allowed three generations of Robinsons to master the art of sitting in the woods. That might sound unimpressive because, of course, almost all hunters sit in the woods. But when I got older and started deer hunting with folks outside my family, I noticed subtle differences in how other people hunted. Other deer hunters bring books to read in their blinds or look at their phones to fight boredom. They shuffle around in their seats, sneak naps, and check their watches. Anything to pass the time, like they’re trying to fast-forward the hunt to the good parts. I suspect these are the same people who complain that they’re too busy and that the days fly by too quickly.

But when the Robinsons are deer hunting, we don’t want time to pass. We’re happy to watch a squirrel or a grouse, or just listen to the geese migrate until it’s time for lunch, a buck appears, or the sun sets on the day.

I’ve had the opportunity to interview some of the best deer hunters in the country, and they’ve shared mountains of tactics and knowledge with me. But I learned the big lesson, the one that matters most, at our family deer camp as a kid. There’s real value in sitting still, being quiet, and happily waiting for a thing that may never come.

Read Next: The Case for New, Wood-Stocked Deer Hunting Rifles

At lunchtime I climb down, fire up my four-wheeler, and drive to Grampa’s stand. My cousin Shawn pulls up at the same time, and we walk toward the voices we hear echoing through the popple. We find my dad, who beat us there, and Gramps standing over a beautiful 10-point with a golf ball-size hole blown through its chest. Gramps tells the story with a look on his face that I can only describe as, “See, told ya.”

Dad casually mentions that he also killed a buck that morning, so once we get Gramps’ deer to camp, he and I set out to retrieve it. From maybe 80 yards away I can see a massive rack sticking up from the fallen leaves.

“Holy smokes,” I mutter as my dad hoists the antlers of an enormous northwoods 8-pointer. It’s the best buck he’s ever killed, and also the largest buck we’ve ever taken—or even seen—on our land. “He’s a pretty nice one,” he tells me as we lash the buck to his wheeler. “But I didn’t want to take away from Grampa’s big moment.”

By next fall, 80-year-old Gramps will be one year older, and my dad will have major back surgery that limits his hunting for the season. Change will come for the Robinson deer camp whether we like it or not.

Related: This Is How the Boomer Generation Shaped Hunting Culture, for Better and Worse

But now isn’t the time to worry about any of that. We have to hang the bucks on the meat pole and get the brats ready for lunch. That, after all, is our tradition.

Alex Robinson is Outdoor Life’s editor-in-chief. He oversees an ace team of writers, photographers, and editors who are scattered across the continent and cover everything from backcountry sheep hunting to trail running.

#8363. Jost Kobusch to Train For Everest on Difficult, Dangerous Lhotse Shar

Jost Kobusch of Germany has moved to the Khumbu Valley to finish his acclimatization before attempting to solo winter Everest, beginning on December 22. His preparations include reaching 8,000m on a different peak.

Kobusch is now in Chukhung village, from which he climbed 6,165m Island Peak (Imja Tse) yesterday. He spent the night at 6,000m, topped out again today, and went back down, as shown in his live tracker. He told ExplorersWeb that he will now rest for a few days in Lukla and then hike up the valley again to inspect conditions on his next acclimatization goal: the difficult Lhotse Shar (8,383m).

Kobusch’s climb on Island Peak as shown in his tracker today.

“My plan is not to summit but to tag 8,000m up its flank, before heading for Everest,” he said.

Lhotse Shar is one of the main points of the Lhotse ridge, and it is climbed from south side of the mountain, with Chukhung as the starting point. The main options are to climb the southeastern flank of the Shar itself or try a direct line up the formidable south face of Lhotse.

Neither of them is easy, and the upper sections are statistically dangerous. According to Wikipedia, Lhotse Shar has the highest fatality rate of all principal or secondary 8,000m peaks. For every two climbers who summit, one person dies attempting to.

Lhotse and its various points, as seen from the south. Graphic by Gorskie Mapy

The Austrian expedition that first reached the summit of Lhotse Shar (from the Southeast Ridge) in 1970 set four altitude camps, the uppermost at 7,600m. They used supplemental oxygen on their summit push. “Almost the whole of the route from below Camp 1 to Camp 3 was fixed with rope, as climbing was very difficult,” they reported in the AAJ.

Kobusch will not have to deal with the nearly vertical terrain and cornices near the summit, but will have to face difficult mixed terrain and uncertain conditions if he wants to venture up to 8,000m. He will not use bottled oxygen, and as far as we know, he will climb on his own.

Kobusch plans to reach the base of Everest on December 22 and solo the mountain in winter without oxygen, via the West Ridge — the same line currently being attempted by a Russian team.

This is Kobusch’s fourth attempt, and he is willing to try to reach the summit this time. However, when asked about it, he admits he does not know if such a feat is actually possible. “Every expedition, every training session, every difficult decision has been part of preparing myself for what I know will be the biggest challenge of my life,” he admits.

Kobusch’s is not the only confirmed climb this winter. Denis Urubko of Russia is reportedly preparing to climb Broad Peak in winter, possibly starting at the end of this year and continuing through January and February. On social media, Urubko mentioned he will be climbing with Vitaliy Skel. However, details of his route have not been confirmed.

Simone Moro of Italy has not decided whether to try Manaslu again this winter. He told ExplorersWeb he is still considering his plans while observing the “crazy weather this season.” Moro attempted Manaslu in fall to acclimatize and become familiar with the terrain. However, like every other team, he had to call off the attempt due to dangerous conditions following extremely heavy snowfall. Moro wants to climb the peak in alpine style.

Angela Benavides graduated university in journalism and specializes in high-altitude mountaineering and expedition news. She has been writing about climbing and mountaineering, adventure and outdoor sports for 20+ years.

Prior to that, Angela Benavides spent time at/worked at a number of local and international media. She is also experienced in outdoor-sport consultancy for sponsoring corporations, press manager and communication executive, and a published author.

#8360. The Paradox of Seeking First Ascents in Western Nepal

Western Nepal has plenty of unclimbed 6,000m peaks, and Fredrik Strang of Sweden and Sindre Kolbjornsgard of Norway went there planning to climb one. In the end, they settled for a slightly lower but equally memorable experience. It showed again that adventure goes beyond first ascents.

What I remember most clearly about that narrow summit is not the grade, the exposure, or the possibility of being first. It is the silence. No helicopters. No voices from a fixed-rope queue. No machinery, no aircraft, no distant sound of a settlement. Only the two of us, a serrated horizon, and an unfamiliar feeling that the mountain had asked a question rather than supplied an answer.

Sindre Kolbjornsgard, left, and Fredrik Strang. Photo: Fredrik Strang

After 18 expeditions to 8,000’ers and witnessing the evolution (or devolution) of climbing on these peaks’ normal routes, Strang and his partner knew they wanted something different. Quoting Ed Viesturs, Strang said: “The point is to climb, not just to summit.” With that in mind, the climbers set four criteria for their objectives:

Several route options face the climbers’ base camp tent. Photo: Fredrik Strang

Strang noted that there are many unclimbed peaks left in Nepal, but they were looking for a compelling climb, not just a dull summit for the record. They finally found one that satisfied their criteria, but then, as with so many other teams in Nepal this fall, harsh weather put their original objective out of reach. Not to mention that traveling Western Nepal is anything but simple.

“Our intended destination was in the Nyalu Lek area north of Simikot,” Strang revealed. “We planned to cross Nyalu La, a pass around 5,000m, with a support party that included porters and pack animals.

“Getting from Surkhet to Simikot took two rough days by road, with landslides having damaged or obliterated sections of the route. At Simikot, we learned that Nyalu La was probably impassable. Even reaching the place where we planned to start trekking had been uncertain.”

When they did reach the area, they confirmed that their planned peak was not an option. Hiking further down the valley, they pitched their tent at Phendol Kermi Camp. Snow had covered the trails beyond, but they simply had no time or money to move to some other region in Nepal. Instead, they decided to explore whatever was close to their camp.

During their stay, they encountered Mick Fowler and Victor Saunders of the UK. Bad weather had likewise spoiled the British pair’s plan to attempt an unclimbed 6,000m peak farther east, known only as Peak 6010, according to Mount Everest Foundation.. This would have been their second attempt on that peak. Ironically, dry conditions spoiled their 2025 attempt. This time, too much snow forced them back. Read more on the British climbers’ adventure here:

On October 6, Strang and Kolbjornsgard made their first acclimatization climb on a 5,177m peak at 30°20′49.82″N, 82°03′54.82″E. They found no records of any previous ascent.

“From base camp, the mountain looked straightforward, and lower down, it largely was,” Strang recalls. “Steep grass, mud, and broken rock led toward the upper slopes. But at about 4,500m, the route disappeared beneath knee-deep snow, then waist-deep snow, and every step became a small excavation.”

In the following days, they scouted and flew their drone down side valleys, looking for possibilities. Their second peak was 5,227m in altitude, located at 30°07′50.86″N, 81°41′56.58″E. Again, there were no records of previous ascents. Parts of it were far from fun.

“Below 4,500m, progress was tolerable, but above that, we were back to forcing our way through knee-deep, sometimes chest-deep snow,” Strang wrote. “A tremendous amount of work for very little enjoyment.”

Luckily, the climb improved once they reached the summit ridge, on relatively easy mixed rock and ice, which they assessed at PD+.

“Fowler and Saunders climbed the mountain several days later and found an easier way to descend and reascend the step on its western side,” Strang noted. “Their solution was a reminder that the first line a team takes is not necessarily the best one.”

Still, the peak that most drew the climbers’ attention was a remarkable 5,337m tooth of rock above Yangchya Lake at approximately 30°07′09.36″N, 81°41′21.15″E.

“We began calling it Daara Peak — a name inspired by the Nepali word for a projecting tooth or fang and by the mountain’s striking shape,” Strang said. He notes that the name is provisional while they await a response from the authorities about their suggestion.

Strang describes the peak as “a saber-toothed tiger’s canine protruding from a mass of rock. The terminal pinnacle was about eight meters high, overhanging and sharply defined against the sky.”

The climbers about to reach what they called Daara Peak. Drone photo: Brizendra Bhattarai

The problem was approaching the peak in deep, heavy snow from Yangchya Lake. They failed on their first attempt to tackle its northeast headwall. After waiting out another snowy spell, the climbers returned for a second attempt on October 17-18.

The climbers dealt with 250m of rather poor rock, a complex approach to the headwall across a bergschrund with soft snow, and the uncertainty of moving on unfamiliar terrain. They had no idea of the difficulties ahead or whether they could protect the passages ahead on their way to the summit. The route included vertical granite walls, with cracks adequate for cams, some mixed terrain with the crux at M6, a steep couloir, and then the summit ridge: a granite edge with some 30cm of snow on top.

Climbing terrain on Daara Peak. Photo: Fredrik Strang

Rather than technical difficulty, the section was delicate due to exposure and a lack of comfortable belay stations. Yet, the climbers reached the summit on October 18, including the top of the final pinnacle, thanks to a crack in the rock.

Strang admits that the climbing details suggest a rather straightforward ascent: a 5,337m summit via its northeast side in five pitches with difficulties up to M6. However, Strang judges the climb on a different scale. “The technical crux was behind us, but it was the part I remember most vividly,” he explained. “It was the silence.”

Strang and Kolbjornsgard’s route up Daara Peak. Photo: Fredrik Strang

On many of the 8,000m peaks I have climbed, the human presence has become part of the landscape: camps, radio calls, fixed ropes, queues, and the distant sound of aircraft. Up here, none of that reached us. Mountains extended in every direction. There was no audience, no ceremony, no one to confirm that we had accomplished anything at all.

Strang also reflects on the paradox: They enjoyed the wild terrain but, if every climber chases solitude by visiting these last remote valleys, the character of these places will inevitably change. For Strang, the question is not to ban access. However, he says:

Perhaps some summits should remain untouched — not because we lack the skill or courage to reach them, but because future generations deserve to inherit mountains that have not all been reduced to objectives.

The climb from the ridge. Photo: Fredrik Strang

#8355. A Climbing Expedition to 'Central Asia's Patagonia'

A mix of nine young and veteran Croatian climbers spent the month of August in one of the world’s great big-wall destinations — Kyrgyzstan’s Karavshin Range in the Pamir-Alay Mountains. Reaching up to 5,509m, the once-unknown granite cliffs now draw dozens of international climbers each summer. The two core valleys, Ak-Su and Kara-Su, have become known as Central Asia’s Patagonia.

The two leaders, Sinisa Skalec and Lukas Grbac Lackovic, spoke to ExplorersWeb about the place and their expedition. First, a summary of their four weeks.

Though suffering from stomach issues for most of the month (more on this later), the team repeated several granite classics, did a new route on 4,430m Pik Breeze, and attempted another new line.

Three climbers — Bruno Crljenic, Eva Pejic, and Lackovic — identified a potential new line below Pik Breeze’s west ridge. They knew of only one previous route on the mountain, done by Russians Marina Popova and Denis Prokofiev.

Beginning at 5am, the three crossed a broad scree slope, then began climbing a couloir below Pik Breeze’s west ridge. They negotiated loose rock, briefly followed an existing line, then moved left into crack systems.

Canned Horse, 6c A0, top. Below, previous attempts and possible other lines on the face.

They climbed until 9pm, when they bivouacked on a tiny ledge with space for only two people sitting and one lying down. Resuming at 8:30am, they reached a col after three pitches and continued directly up slabs and cracks. They finished on easier IV/V terrain and followed a 200m ridge to the summit.

They called their new line Canned Horse, 930m, 23 pitches, 6c A0. It took them a further eight hours to get back to base camp. During the climb, they found pitons on two pitches. They believe these belonged to another route on the west ridge, but the history of that line, and whether it was finished, is uncertain.

Pejic, Grbac Lackovic, and Crljenic on top of Pik Breeze.

Later in the month, one of those climbers, Eva Pejic, became quite ill and couldn’t leave base camp on her own. Five teammates helped evacuate her, carrying her for 11km, with assistance from other climbers in the area. She later recuperated in the Kyrgyz city of Osh.

On the first crux of ‘Perestroika Crack.’

Three small teams of Croatian climbers also repeated Perestroika Crack, one of the best-known long granite routes in Central Asia. It rises about 900m up the north face of 4,240m Pik Slesova and is graded 7a. Three members reportedly free-climbed the route. One pair, Sinisa Skalec and Ivan Vitez, completed the line in a single marathon day, beginning at 5:45am, summiting at 5:40pm, and returning to base camp at 11pm.

Skalec and Vitez also repeated the French Ridge of 4,510m Kyrkchilta, 2,000m, 6b to 6b+. They needed one bivouac. Toward the end of August, this same pair attempted a first ascent on the Central Pyramid of Ortotyubek, overlooking base camp. Their attempted route mounted slabs and cracks to the left of the established Take It Easy line, which other Croatians had repeated during that month. The pair gave up their attempt on August 26 because of illness.

Base camp with Ortotyubek and Slesova in the background.

Here is our interview with Lukas Grbac Lackovic and Sinisa Skalec about Kyrgyzstan and the Ak-Su Valley.

Lukas Grbac Lackovic repeats the Central Route on a peak called Small Russian Tower.

Was Eva Pejic’s health problem related to altitude or something else? What was the illness you experienced on Ortotyubek?

Eva’s health problem was unrelated to altitude exposure or the expedition environment. However, most of us experienced gastrointestinal infections, with symptoms including diarrhea, bloating, fever, and general malaise.

This significantly hindered our climbing, as everyone in the team got sick at least once, and some even twice. Recovery was slow and never quite complete –- we felt weak for most of the trip.

One thing that helped us recover was having plain rice with some of our meals, which we could get from the cook if we asked nicely. If we were to go again, we would definitely bring our own food and cooking equipment. This can be inconvenient, but it could potentially save you at least a week of being sick and unable to climb.

Another view of base camp and peaks Ortotyubek and Slesova.

Can you describe the Karavshin Valley? How large is it? Are there many side valleys?

The two main valleys are Ak-Su and Kara-Su. Adjacent to them is the Jaupaya Valley, which provides a descent route from Pik Breeze and is home to several peaks around 5,000m high. The rock quality there appears poor, and we are not aware of any established rock routes in the valley.

Another interesting area, west of Kara Su, is the Orto-Chashma Gorge. An Italian team made a first ascent there during our visit.

Here in the West, we first heard about the area following the kidnapping of four American climbers in Kara-Su in 2000. Since then, it seems to have calmed down and become a popular climbing destination. Is the area considered safe nowadays, or are there still things climbers should be aware of?

Currently, the security situation in the valley is good. There is always the possibility of tensions, of course, as the valleys lie close to the border with Tajikistan. However, in recent years, the number of climbers visiting the area has increased exponentially, reaching around 100 climbers this year.

How many other climbing teams were in Ak-Su and neighboring Kara-Su while you were there? Where were they generally from?

During the three weeks we spent in the valley, up to 50 climbers were in BC at the same time. Climbers came from all over the world — Chile, U.S., Spain, Slovakia, Poland, Belgium, Slovenia, France, Italy, Germany, Japan, Russia…

We had originally planned a different expedition, but had to change our destination just a few months before departure. Some friends from Croatia and Slovenia recommended the area because of its straightforward logistics and relatively good security, while still offering impressive big walls and proper crack climbing. It didn’t take much to convince us!

Are there any military checkpoints? Do you need special permission to enter the valleys?

Yes, you have to pass through a military checkpoint when entering the area. However, the outfitter handles the application for the necessary permits and ensures everything goes smoothly.

Some call the Karavshin Valley the Patagonia of Central Asia because of its aesthetic granite walls. Do you think that’s a fair description?

Patagonia is definitely more spectacular, but you pay the price with poor weather. If your goal is to climb almost every day, then the Karavshin Valley is a better choice.

Climbing in Patagonia requires much more logistical planning and preparation, and the approaches themselves are a whole different story. In the Karavshin Valley, on the other hand, there is no snow or ice during the season we visited (August), at least on the walls we climbed. Some of the walls are just a 15-minute walk from BC, and you can reach them all in approach shoes.

Perestroika Crack and some of the classic routes in the Karavshin region are arguably just as good as the best rock routes in Patagonia.

How does the granite compare to Yosemite and Patagonia?

The granite is more similar in texture to that found in Patagonia; it is not as smooth as in Yosemite. The overall rock quality is generally good, although, as everywhere, some sections are of poorer quality. One downside of the mild climate in the Karavshin Valley is the dirt and grass that accumulate in the cracks, which can sometimes detract from the aesthetic appeal of the routes. The more popular and frequently climbed routes have already been cleaned and are pleasant to climb.

You climbed 4-1/2 pitches of the route on Ortotyubek. How much did you have left to complete it?

There were still two-and-a-half pitches left to complete the route up to the junction with Take It Easy. We envisioned the second half of the fifth pitch following the line up to a point just below the overhang that runs diagonally to the left.

From there, the sixth pitch follows a thin crack, from which you need to traverse left and move into another system of thin cracks that runs across the roof and joins the Take It Easy belay (seventh pitch). The last pitch of Take It Easy offers a logical exit, and we don’t think there is any need to come up with an alternative.

At the point where we stopped (first half of the fifth pitch), we left two pitons that we used as a temporary rappel anchor. Repeating parties are welcome to remove them, as they were only left for temporary use. We think a few bolts may be needed in the second half of the fifth pitch, but that obviously depends on the abilities of those planning to complete the route.

All belays are equipped (the first and fourth belays have two 8mm bolts each, while the second and third have two pitons each). In addition, we left two or three pitons in the first pitch and two in the second pitch.

As in the rest of the valley, some sections of the route are filled with dirt and grass. We made an effort to clean the cracks in the first four pitches, but there is still plenty of work to be done, especially on the sixth pitch, which we didn’t climb, where a thin crack is completely filled with dirt.

How much unclimbed rock remains? Are the obvious lines already established, or are there still many promising routes?

The most obvious and accessible lines may have already been climbed, but there is still plenty of potential. Anyone willing to put in the effort can establish a new route. The area is vast, and there is still plenty of room for new routes.

Your website mentions that there are guidebooks to certain routes in that area. Where are these available?

Most of the guidebooks we used are available online. We received some Russian guidebooks from climbing friends, although we didn’t use them very much. We’re not sure how easily they can be found online. Climbers from all over the world bring various materials to the camp, so most route topos and information can be obtained there.

What was the most unexpected experience on your expedition — something that might surprise climbers familiar with other big-wall areas?

We were pleasantly surprised by the kindness and honesty of the Kyrgyz people. When traveling to remote parts of the world, many of us tend to expect that someone will try to rip us off and that we will have to haggle over everything, but that was not the case here.

Although some things took longer than expected, everything we had agreed on in advance was respected, and all the logistics for transportation and accommodation worked very well.

As we mentioned earlier, our only complaint concerns the food and hygiene conditions at the camp, which were most likely responsible for the digestive problems experienced by virtually all the climbers staying there.

Jerry Kobalenko is the editor of ExplorersWeb. One of Canada’s premier arctic travelers, he is the author of The Horizontal Everest and Arctic Eden, and has just finished a book about adventures in Labrador. In 2018, he was awarded the Polar Medal by the Governor General of Canada and in 2022, he received the Queen’s Platinum Jubilee Medal for services to exploration.

#8352. Hiker Dies Trying To Help Son Amid Icy Conditions on Mt. Baden-Powell

A hiker died Sunday morning on Southern California’s Mt. Baden-Powell after slipping on ice and falling roughly 500 feet, according to AV Scanner News, a local Southern California emergency news-monitoring page.

A GoFundMe arranged by the victim’s niece identifies the deceased as ultrarunner Guillermo Alejandro Batres and says he had been attempting to help his son, who had slipped and fallen, when he himself lost his footing and fell down a steep gulch.

Authorities have not confirmed the victim’s identity or cause of death and have not released additional details about the incident. According to AV Scanner News, the fatal accident occurred roughly 3 miles up the trail from Vincent Gap and “family members of the hiker were also reported to be at the scene.”

Los Angeles County Fire Department and Los Angeles County Sheriff’s Department Palmdale Station responded to the incident and an air unit was used to recover Batres’s remains.

Mt. Baden-Powell is a 9,407-foot summit in the Angeles National Forest. The summit lies 0.1 miles off the Pacific Crest Trail at northbound mile 377.9 and is a popular destination for PCT hikers and Los Angeles residents alike. Snow and ice can make the route treacherous during the winter months.

#8351. Avalanche Fatality Warns About Early Ski Conditions In The Alps

Little snow on the mountains doesn’t mean safe conditions. An American off-piste skier died in the Austrian Alps last weekend after he triggered a small slab avalanche. It was far too small to bury him, but it carried him into an open crevasse below.

With the ski season barely underway, this is Europe’s first avalanche victim this season. Last year, 151 people died in avalanches in Europe between October 2025 and June 2026, according to the European Avalanche Warning Service (EAWS), a shockingly high number.

Most victims perished in the Alps: 40 in Italy, 35 in France, 33 in Austria, and 18 in Switzerland. It was the 13th most deadly year in the Alps since record-keeping began. It was also the highest tally ever since the “Winter of Terror” of 1951. Avalanche prevention standards have improved a lot since then, but the number of leisure skiers, climbers, and hikers in the mountains has multiplied as well. (Most of those 1951 victims lived in villages along avalanche paths.)

Last weekend’s accident took place at the Stubai Glacier ski resort. The glacier sections of the Alps typically open for skiing weeks earlier than other mountain resorts, thanks to their higher altitudes and glacial ice, which can provide good October skiing with just a thin layer of fresh snow on top. Stubai’s ski resort opened on October 9 this year.

A map of Stubai Glacier ski resort with the area where the accident happened circled in red. Photo: Stubaier Gletscher website

The skier was on his own, descending in an out-of-bounds area below East Daunkogel Peak at around 11:30am. His tracks on the snow show he eventually abandoned the snow-covered glacier and ventured onto a steeper, rocky area. At some point, he broke a small slab of crusty snow which dragged him some 50 meters into an open bergschrund, where he fatally fell 20 meters down, according to the Lawinen Report.

The accident site, with the skier’s tracks visible,  and the movement of the snow slab — 50m long and 25m wide — marked in pink. Photo: Lawinen Report

Other skiers in the area saw the accident and raised the alarm, but the skier had died by the time they extracted him from the crevasse. The police said the slab was only 10cm to 30cm thick, and the slide was just 25 meters wide. The crevasse was 50 meters below the fracture point.

Avalanche risks are obvious on heavily loaded slopes, but the accident shows that even a thin layer of unstable snow can cause a fatal fall. In this case, the snow had fallen recently on the verglass ice over glacier-polished smooth rocks, and had no time to settle. This is a typical early-season condition in mountain ranges around the world.

The accident occurred at nearly 3,000m of altitude on a shady northeast slope, where the snow hardly transforms and settles during the short days of late fall. To make things worse, the report notes that wind had picked up that morning, creating a weak crust on top of loose, cold, fresh snow, prone to breaking.

The avalanche site on the following day, with most of the terrain covered by freshly drifted snow. Photo: LWD Tirol

The second factor was exposure: the skier might have easily survived the fall if the terrain had been a clean snow slope, but he was on rocky terrain, where a simple fall can result in serious injuries. Most of all, the crevasse below created what the report described as a “terrain trap.”

Fans of backcountry and ski touring are eager to hit the slopes with the first snowflakes, and the scarcely covered terrain may provide a false sense of safety from avalanches. But objective dangers go beyond the big powder avalanches and also include falls on icy slopes. In addition, the glaciers are dry after the summer, and the crevasses are wide open.

After a mild fall, snow is falling heavily in the Alps this week, significantly improving conditions for the region’s ski resorts. Most of them plan to open this weekend. The snow will also offer backcountry opportunities, but conditions will remain highly unstable.

Snowfall up to two meters is expected today in the Alps. Graphic by meteoexploration.com

The avalanche report today by the Tiroler Avalanche Service (LWD Tirol) shows low risk in the Austrian mountains, a remarkable risk (3 out of 5) in the Swiss Alps, and moderate risk (2 out of 5) in France.

The avalanche forecast for today, November 19, in the Alps shows remarkable (level 3-orange) risk, especially in Switzerland. Graphic: LWD Tirol

#8347. Senate Passes BAAJA, the Massive Permitting Bill That Will Expedite Development on Public Lands

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 20, 2026 11:58 AM EST

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

A controversial bill aimed at speeding up infrastructure projects on public lands passed the U.S. Senate Thursday night and now heads to the House.

The Senate declined to vote on a flurry of last-minute amendments to the nearly 500-page text of the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act — including one that would have banned data centers on public land, before voting for the third time late Thursday night. Support in the Senate eroded over the series of votes, from 73-25 on the first vote Nov. 9 to a final vote of 62-33. A bill requires a simple majority of 51 votes in the Senate to pass.

Related: Senate Poised to Vote on Affordability Package that Hunters, Anglers Worry Is a Trojan Horse for Public-Land Rollbacks

BAAJA is co-sponsored by Sen. Martin Heinrich, a Democrat from New Mexico often considered a conservation champion, and Sen. Mike Lee, the Utah Republican and architect of recent attempts to sell public land. Heinrich says BAAJA cuts red tape and will speed up renewable energy projects to help fight climate change, increase jobs, and bring down energy costs for average Americans.

Conservation advocates, however, fear it will also lock in projects many sportsmen and women have spent years fighting, like a copper-sulfide mine by the Boundary Waters Canoe Area Wilderness or the Pebble Mine in Alaska’s Bristol Bay. It also may well open the door for massive data centers on public land, something most of the conservation and sporting community resoundingly oppose.

“I understand this is a big, bipartisan bill — it has some things in it I would rather it not have. But I negotiated the deal based on what was actually possible to get signed into law,” Heinrich wrote in an email to Outdoor Life earlier this week.

BAAJA proponents say it increases project certainty. Right now, one administration can reverse or cancel permits approved by a prior administration. But by limiting the back and forth between administrations the bill also drastically changes how much oversight a project can receive, even under foundational laws like the Endangered Species Act, Clean Water Act and National Historic Preservation Act. The bill will also limit public input into projects and judicial overview.

Many of the nation’s tribes have forcefully spoken out against the bill saying it severely curtails tribal input.

“Overall, the bill, by weakening these bedrock laws, would make it far easier to fast-track AI data centers as well as fossil fuel, mining, and other extractive infrastructure. Once these projects are approved, they would be difficult to unwind,” according to the Western Environmental Law Center. “BAAJA would cause grave, generational harm to the West’s public lands, waters and wildlife, and communities.”

Heinrich has said he opposes data centers on public land.

“To be clear, BAAJA is federal legislation focused on energy infrastructure and grid connectivity,” a spokesperson told OL in an emailed satement. “It does not ‘permit’ data centers on public lands. State and local government approval is still required. The Senator’s legislation addresses the federal energy infrastructure challenges associated with a grid that cannot keep up with demand; it does not circumvent the state and local processes that determine whether specific projects move forward.”

Even conservation groups who support portions of the bill question how fast such a massive piece of legislation is moving through Congress. This is the first major permitting reform bill in decades and was introduced Sept. 30.

Read Next: Public-Land Sales, Energy Dominance, and Data Centers: What Interior’s Master Plan Really Means for Outdoorsmen

The bill now heads to the House. If representatives make any changes to the Senate version, the Senate must vote again on the bill before it heads to President Trump’s desk for his signature. The president has said he supports the bill.

This story was updated on Nov. 20 at 2:30 p.m. CST to include comment from Sen. Heinrich’s office.

Christine Peterson is a freelance writer based in southeast Wyoming covering hunting, fishing, outdoor recreation, wildlife and the environment for Outdoor Life, High Country News, National Geographic and others.

#8346. Table Rock Tenting

My final section in Pennsylvania is on the docket for today. I press along 3 mountains and find a top tier campsite.

Can it be? Just one section of Pennsylvania remaining, and then I’m off to Maryland to attempt the 4 state challenge and get off trail for good. I am starting to feel a tugging at my emotions. Its part relief to be sure, but any time you switch away from something you’ve been doing for a long stretch of time you can’t help but feel that you’ll miss it. I felt the same way when I made the decision to leave my former employer. Sure, it could drive me nuts at times, just like any job can, but it had its moments and its good people. Soon I’ll be off to something new soon, it will have new benefits, and new things to stress over.

I had a short drive to the trail as I attempted to find a quieter place to car camp last night. It was fine until just before 5:00AM when traffic picked up considerably. I will have more time to catch up on sleep tonight on the trail, so getting an earlier than desired wake-up call wasn’t much of a problem. I attempted to jolt myself into action by stopping for a quick coffee on my way back to Swatara Gap. I had a long day of hiking ahead of me with more elevation gain than I had been treated to most of my recent hiking stints. I headed south around 7:30AM with roughly 25 miles to Peters Mountain Road. It had been nearly 4 months since I got trapped at the trailhead there. Ah, fond memories indeed. I recall there being campsites a short walk from the trailhead, but, as always, I would be keeping my pace up and my eyes peeled for other options.

The trail briefly crosses to the far side of Route 443 before re-crossing and heading through a meadow. From there it spends time on 3 parallel mountain ridges. The northernmost is Peters Mountain. To its south is Third Mountain, and then Second Mountain. There is a First Mountain, but the Appalachian Trail doesn’t traverse it. From Second Mountain I could clearly make out Swatara Gap through the trees. Stony Creek Valley sits between Second and Third Mountain and it appears a long gravel road runs for miles along the creek. A sign along the trail stated that the village of Rausch Gap used to occupy the space with about 1,000 people. I couldn’t help but notice how clear Rausch Creek was. I wasn’t sure but there seemed to be water being treated in some manner just north of the bridge over the creek.

Passing through the meadow near Route 443.

Looking at Swatara Gap from Second Mountain.

A bit of info on Rausch Gap beside the AT.

What appears to be some sort of filter or water collection along Rausch Creek.

Crystal clear water under the bridge over Rausch Creek.

The trail heads up Third Mountain, a tree lined ridge without much to see. I did notice a number of trails connecting to the AT. The entire area is quite remote. The long mountain ridges make access tricky, so it makes sense that the section wasn’t as settled as the surrounding areas despite the proximity to Harrisburg.

By early afternoon I was making my way down to route 325 and up to the third and final mountain ridge, Peters Mountain. Through the trees I could make out a familiar sight to the north of spread out farms. As usual, water was quite scarce up on the mountain ridges. My only option for water near the end of my hike was from the spring at Peters Mountain Shelter. “At” is a rather generous preposition to describe the relative location of the spring to the shelter. It requires a 0.2 mile hike down the steep ridge. A sign posted at the shelter indicates 300 steps are required. I’d put it at closer to 400, none of which were easy.

Down at the spring, movement in the distance caught my attention. I caught something that I was sure was a darker, dulled shade of brown than the white-tailed deer I’d been seeing constantly. I thought for sure it must be a coyote and was glad it had decided to trot off in the opposite direction. Upon closer inspection it did seem to be just another deer, the color was certainly unusual though. I climbed back up the steps to the shelter and collected my pack. Staying at the shelter was enticing, it had a loft and loads of space. I figured I would see what the trail ahead would offer and make a decision.

Looking at Third Mountain from Kinter View.

0.9 miles from the shelter is the Table Rock viewpoint. The large rocks at the crest of the ridge provide an open view across to Third Mountain. However, it looked like the sunset was likely going to be just out of view. Sunrise, however, would be quite clear just to the left hand side of the view. Looking ahead, it seemed like the best I could do, and there seemed to be a spot for a 1 person tent. It was sloped, but I was confident I could orient my tent in such away that I would sleep with the slope running down toward my feet. With the campsite boxes checked, I stashed the 2 liters of water I had collected and headed the last 2 miles south to the Peters Mountain trailhead to connect to the point where I’d ended my southern AT hiking back in July.

With almost no minutes to spare I made it back to table rock to catch the last bits of sunlight hitting Third Mountain. I was exhausted and eager to scarf down my dinner and crawl into my tent. My tent theory was spot on, the slope followed the length of my sleeping pad and didn’t wash out right or left. It is not an easy thing to eye since the inside of my Durston tent runs at a funky diagonal angle relative to the rectangular footprint of the rain fly. I didn’t bother setting an alarm for sunrise. There are about 14 hours between sunset and sunrise. Even if I slept 10 hours I’d be up well before the sun would be making its daily appearance.

I’m See You Tomorrow (SYT), a 30-something hiker who loves hiking just for the thrill of seeing things that most people don’t. I have no problem eating the same things every day and, for me, over-analyzing everything is half the fun (sometimes more). I am new to backpacking but that won’t stop me from taking on an unorthodox hike of the Appalachian Trail in 2026. It’s not quite a yo-yo, but I have aspirations of hiking the entire trail twice in one year. For the rest of the story you’ll just have to start reading my blog!

What you saw on Rausch Creek was a diversion well to treat acid-mine drainage, which is a huge problem in coal-mining regions. The tainted water is funneled through limestone gravel, which raises the pH enough to give trout a fighting chance of survival.

#8344. Family Offers $75,000 Reward to Save Climber Missing on Mount Fuji

Today, November 19, the Yamanashi Prefectural Police announced that they had ended the search for Chinese climber Chen Jie, 25, according to the Japanese national newspaper Mainichi Shimbun. Chen disappeared November 8 on the main Yoshida Trail after continuing alone up 3,776m Mount Fuji during its closed season.

His two companions reportedly turned back because of deteriorating weather and inadequate equipment. Chen continued on his own without crampons or sufficient cold-weather clothing. His last video was sent that morning between 2,300 and 2,700m.

His father subsequently traveled to Japan and offered a reward of RMB500,000 ($75,000) for information leading to his recovery alive, or RMB100,000 ($15,000) for recovery of his body.

Ironically, Chen belonged to China’s Blue Sky Rescue Team, a volunteer search-and-rescue organization. His mother told local media that he had participated in rescues in China and had some mountaineering experience, including trekking and climbing in Nepal and Indonesia.

#8341. Climbers Forge New Route on Mount Shiva Despite Limited Acclimatization

Benjamin Bosshard and Philippo Sala will never forget their first experience above 6,000m and the new route they opened with Stefan Siegrist on India’s Bahai Jar, commonly known as Mount Shiva (6,142m).

Expeditions in the Indian Himalaya are usually quiet while they take place, because satellite phones and InReach devices are prohibited. After returning home, however, Stefan Siegrist reported the details, and the team’s cameraman, Christoph Detschmann, posted a video summary of the expedition:

Siegrist had already visited the Chenab River Valley area in 2018 and climbed Shiva with Jonas Schild, but he still had designs on its unclimbed northwest ridge. At the end of September, he, Sala, and Bosshard reached base camp in the Kishtwar massif.

The three Swiss climbers were prepared to endure a long period of bad weather while acclimatizing. To their surprise, a good weather window opened right away. It was too good to let go, despite not being properly acclimatized and Sala and Bosshard’s lack of altitude experience. The window was expected to last just three days. After many “discussions and doubts,” they left base camp on October 3.

Sala had a headache during the first night, but the following morning, they were all totally focused on the climb, and the tension disappeared. “We assumed that we were unlikely to reach the summit on this attempt, and that we would therefore be wasting our only chance before a major storm [brought] the season to an end,” Siegrist explained. “So from then on, the idea was simply to climb higher and acclimatize, without any pressure.”

A moment of the climb, filmed from a drone by Christoph Detschmann

Along the way, they discovered they had left their stove at base camp. No problem in 2026: The expedition photographer used his drone to carry the stove up to them. The use of drones was a hot topic during the recent Piolets d’Or: To what extent are they compatible with alpine-style criteria? While most accepted that drones could scout routes or sections out of view of base camp, many considered using them to carry equipment up a face to be a red line.

On Shiva, Siegrist says the delivery of the missing stove “proved essential to the success of the climb.”

The climbers spent their second night at 5,250m at the base of the northwest ridge. On the following morning, they had to decide whether to continue or retreat. They all felt well, but starting the highly technical ridge was a point of no return. At the same time, Siegrist recalls, they realized they really had a chance to summit. And so on they went.

That night, they stopped at 5,700m in what Siegrist describes as a crucial moment. His partners had not been at that altitude before, and the latest forecast announced a storm the following day. They decided to wait and see what the morning looked like. No one slept at all that night.

The last meters before the summit. Drone image by Christoph Detschmann

The sky was still clear when the team set off again at 4am, so they chose to keep going. “After a while, we finally reached the final slope, just 300m below the summit,” Siegrist wrote. By now, they knew they were going to make it. They reached the top at 7:44am, six days after arriving in Base Camp, and just in time to hurry down as the storm approached.

The climbers descended via the 1985 Japanese route (see topo below), and reached their base camp tents as it started to snow.

#8340. School Teacher’s First Deer with a Bow Is This 226-Inch Illinois Giant

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 20, 2026 1:00 PM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

Southern Illinois school teacher Darin Hicks grew up in a hunting family, but he didn’t start hunting deer himself until just a few years ago. As his interest in deer hunting grew, he took special notice of any big bucks he spotted during his daily drives to and from school as a science teacher.

“I started seeing a large buck, but never in the day [at first],” the 40-year-old Illinois native tells Outdoor Life. “We don’t use trail cameras. But I got plenty of [phone] photos the last couple years of a really good buck — but only a few photos during the day.”

Hicks was mentoring his 9-year-old daughter, Josie, who wanted her first deer to be a buck.

“I had her out in youth season, and she could have taken a lot of does but wanted a buck. One evening we saw a buck, but it was too dark [even though it was still legal light] and Josie didn’t want to shoot in that poor light.”

Hicks thinks the buck that he and his daughter spotted that evening during the youth season may have been the giant buck he arrowed weeks later.

On Nov. 14 he hung a ladder stand near a wooded thicket full of rubs, scrapes, and plenty of trails. He set the stand for his dad, 77, to hunt during the upcoming Illinois gun opener on Nov. 20.

But that Saturday night Hicks’ wife, Katie, asked her husband if he wanted to bowhunt the stand the next day, before his father got in it the following week.

Hicks said there wasn’t much time that next morning on Sunday before they left for church. Katie said he had a good two hours to hunt from dawn until leaving for church with his family.

She had a good point, Hicks decided, and climbed into the stand the following morning of Nov. 15.

He sat for about an hour and spotted a doe with twin fawns at 150 yards. Suddenly the doe with young spooked and out stepped the giant buck Hicks had seen from afar for the last two years.

“I was shaking, found my grunt call and blew it once,” he says. “He turned and started walking toward me and my heart was pounding overtime.”

The buck came steadily closer while Hicks readied for a shot — the first time he would ever draw his bow on a whitetail.

“He walked toward me, stopped, and looked up at me at 15 yards, then he turned to head away,” Hicks says. “He started through some brush quartering away at 35 yards and I drew, released, and I heard a loud smack.”

The buck collapsed in his tracks. Hicks’ arrow had hit a bit high, but his 2-blade mechanical struck the spine and the buck didn’t get up.

Hicks managed to wrestle the nontypical out of the woods and, field-dressed, it weighed 187 pounds. His taxidermist aged the buck at 5 1/2 years, with a gross green score of 226 1/8 inches.

Read Next: Complete Shock.’ Long-Haul Trucker Hunter Tags 227-Inch Nontypical During State Park Hunt

“We all still made it to church that morning,” Hicks says. “And I even got to teach Sunday School.”
