#8339. Ibex Wool Aire Hoodie Review: A New Favorite Piece of Gear After 22,000+ Miles of Hiking

Over the last handful of years, I have completed thru-hikes all across the country, traversing a wide variety of terrain and weather conditions. I have consolidated and eliminated pieces of gear from my pack in order to make it as light as possible while still maintaining my desired comforts.

I choose to carry as few additional pieces of clothing as possible; my midlayer puffy is one of the most important pieces of gear I have. When I first got into thru-hiking, I started out with a standard puffy from REI, which was nice and warm but quite heavy and bulky. Later on, I switched to a Montbell 1000-fill puffy, which is incredibly lightweight at roughly 4.5 oz. For the weight and size, I loved this puffy and used it for over 17,000 miles.

Recently, though, I have been using a new midlayer that I absolutely love.

A couple of months ago, I received the Ibex Wool Aire Puffy, which is quite different from any other puffy I’ve used before. The outer shell and lining are 100% nylon, while the insulation is 70% wool and 30% polyester.

The Ibex Wool Air Puffy weighs around 12oz, depending on the size, and packs down quite small. As someone who has primarily prioritized eliminating pack weight wherever possible, I often shied away from heavier pieces of clothing in favor of more bare-bones styles. However, the features of this puffy are what I’ve enjoyed most about it, along with the incredible warmth and comfort it provides.

MSRP: $315 Weight: 12 oz (women’s XS) Materials: 100% nylon shell; 70% wool / 30% polyester insulation Size range: XS – XXL

Men’s Wool Aire here (our dedicated review of the men’s version is forthcoming).

I’ve taken the Ibex Wool Aire Puffy out for hundreds of miles along the Appalachian Trail in Pennsylvania, as well as on lesser-known trails such as the Laurel Highlands Hiking Trail and Susquehannock Trail in PA. I’m currently using it on the Arizona Trail as temperatures drop.

Most puffy jackets are insulated with either synthetic polyester fill or natural goose or duck down. The Wool Aire, in contrast, is filled with a 90-gram wool blend. Unlike down, which clumps up and loses loft when wet, this wool insulation maintains its loft even when wet and can be worn while you’re actively hiking. This makes it a good option for hikers who want to hike in their puffy and also want to minimize synthetics in their clothing system.

Wool Insulation: Because this jacket is made primarily of wool and nylon fabrics, it is incredibly soft and feels good even against bare skin. In contrast, traditional puffy jackets often stick and cling to sweaty skin and are much less comfortable when damp or sweaty.

I’ve found this wool material does not wet through the same way that other puffy jackets do when worn during active hiking. The Ibex Wool Air puffy also dries out much quicker than puffies that don’t use wicking fabrics such as wool.

Zipper Pockets: There are two zippable front pockets on the jacket, a feature I really enjoy. When thru-hiking it is great to be able to safely carry a wallet, phone, or a room key around town when running errands. Having pockets that zip shut would make it nice and easy to wear the jacket around and not have to carry a fanny pack.

There is also a large, zippered pocket on the interior of the jacket to allow you to safely store away other important items that you might need, like a headlamp or charging brick.

Peg Leg in the Ibex Wool Aire Hoodie (second from left)

Comfortable: I find myself excited at the end of the day to put on such a cozy and warm layer. This jacket feels so good on my skin. It provides fantastic insulation for sleeping in colder conditions and is definitely something I wish I had had on the Continental Divide Trail when I was hiking in much more unpredictable conditions.

Ideal fit for thru-hiking: In my opinion, this puffy has the perfect size and fit, and having the hood is a wonderful feature to provide additional warmth and insulation.

Dispatch from the AZT: “I’ve been loving the Ibex Wool Aire puffy that I’m using out on a longer thru-hike for the first time. That thing is really cozy but doesn’t get me too sweaty because of the wool. I’m surprised how much I’ve hiked in it on the AZT. I think I’ve done more hiking in a puffy on this trail than any other!” – Peg Leg

Weight: In my opinion, this jacket is worth the weight. However, if you want the lightest possible puffy, this isn’t it. The Wool Aire weighs somewhere around the middle of the spectrum for puffy jackets that I have seen. Very minimalist designs with limited pockets and no hood can weigh in somewhere around 5oz, whereas more insulated puffy jackets can weigh more than a pound.

Even though it is a little heavier than some other jackets, I think it is well worth the weight. I would much rather carry a few extra ounces and know that at the end of the day I will be incredibly comfortable and warm regardless of the weather conditions.

Ridge Merino Alpha Wool Insulated Jacket (Women’s | Men’s)

There are very few direct comps for the Ibex Wool Aire; below we’ve linked the other puffies Peg Leg used in the past as an additional comparison. – Eds.

The Ibex Wool Aire puffy was donated for purpose of review.

This hoodie received an update during the testing period. This is the version we tested. This is the latest.

#8337. Two Inexperienced Teens Rescued After 20 Hours on Grossglockner

Two Germans, aged 15 and 18, spent almost 20 hours on Austria’s highest mountain before being evacuated by helicopter on November 15.

Initially, police reported that the pair had reached the 3,798m summit and become lost while descending. The younger climber disputes this, saying they turned back near an alpine hut at 3,454m because his companion felt ill.

Their ordeal began around 11pm on Saturday, November 14. The pair had no previous experience with such a serious mountain but had prepared by reading websites, watching videos, training physically, and asking others for tips. They did carry crampons, an ice axe, a helmet, a rope, a belay device, and extra clothing.

It took them a long time before they decided to turn around. The descent went well at first, but one spot seemed too dangerous to them. To get around it, they followed someone else’s tracks in the snow, but these suddenly disappeared. While looking for a way, they got stuck in deep snow. The older boy called for help at around 6:30pm on November 15, according to Austria’s public broadcaster, ORF.at.

At first, rescuers tried to guide the pair down by phone. More than two hours later, the boys were still stuck, so a helicopter was dispatched. The rescuers found the pair, exhausted, disoriented, and hypothermic, at around 2,800m.

A rescue helicopter brings the teens to safety. Photo: Mountain Rescue KALS

Yesterday, rescue leader Peter Tembler criticized their lack of alpine experience and preparation. He told the German Press Agency that the pair lacked sufficient experience for the terrain and winter conditions. He also criticized their late-night departure.

According to Tembler, a section that normally takes four to five hours took them almost 12. Other climbers reportedly advised them against continuing.

Tembler believes an increasing number of inexperienced people are attempting serious alpine routes, sometimes encouraged by social media accounts that downplay the difficulty. The disagreement has become a public debate about risk-taking, responsibility, and rescue operations.

As Austria’s highest peak, Grossglockner is a magnet for climbers of all stripes and has more than its share of controversy. In January 2025, a man abandoned his less experienced girlfriend high on the mountain. She froze to death, and an Austrian court later convicted him of manslaughter for leaving her behind. This summer, an altercation occurred on Grossglockner in which a guide insulted a passing climber and nearly pushed him off a cliff. At the same time, veteran alpinists are still setting bold new routes here.

According to Der Standard, Grossglockner gets 8,000 to 9,000 ascents each year, with an average of two dozen rescues annually. Between 2005 and 2025, 29 climbers have died on the mountain.

#8335. Day 39: My Birthday in SANTIAGO

Before I even got out of bed, my Italian brother ran to me in the dark of the bunk room and gave me a birthday kiss on the cheek.

As I journaled in the common room, the pilgrim from Germany I met last night walked in with another candle for my birthday.

My Argentinian brother walked in shortly after and gave me a big birthday hug.

It wasn’t even an hour into my day and I already felt surrounded by love.

One last time, the boys and I set out as a family on the last four kilometers under the stars.

My Argentinian brother pointed out how three stars were perfectly aligned with the moon.

It truly felt like the stars had aligned.

When we arrived at the plaza, the first thing we saw was tents lined up for the protests in Europe. Not allowing that to divert our focus, we turned our attention to what mattered:

My brain couldn’t process that this was the cathedral and the town I’d been walking toward.

Part of the reason for getting to town so early was to attend the one mass that was being held in the cathedral at 7:30. Leaving our backpacks and everything to our names outside, we entered the legendary church.

Within seconds of entering the cathedral, our Australian friend was surprised to find us, as it was her last day in Spain. The four of us walked around this significant place, the finish line we’d been working toward.

The first thing that caught my attention was the fragrance of incense. I’d seen so many videos of the big swing of incense that went through the cathedral. Since it was under restoration, we wouldn’t get that experience. Instead, the mass was held in a small chapel. It didn’t feel as significant as other experiences I’d had, but I’m thankful I went.

The cathedral, though, was beautiful. The painted pillars adorning the golden altar caught my attention because they differed from the others I’d seen. My Argentinian brother and I walked around in silence after the mass, entering the legendary tomb of Santiago, which was underneath the choir. To be there in real time felt significant, but it didn’t hold the energy I expected.

Immediately after, we went back to the plaza and found more pilgrim friends. Sharing hugs and tears, we all said our congratulations to each other before a giant group picture.

The brothers and I were hungry, though, so we all squeezed onto a couch in a cafe, classical music playing in the background. After we ate, the waitress brought over a giant piece of chocolate cake with a blue candle. Surrounded by my brothers, I made a wish and blew out the flame.

To make our arrival official, we went to the pilgrims’ office and acquired our Compostela. The Compostela.

The man at the desk helping me was so excited to hear it was my birthday that he awkwardly reached over the wide counter to give me a hug. It was just as special as the piece of paper I had in my hand.

The brothers and I went to our accommodation for the night, a private bedroom with our own bathroom and three beds. It was simple, but the opportunity to not be in a bunk room and to share that space together was bliss.

It was exactly what we needed to charge up before heading into the city for our hard-earned tinto de verano and pinchos. It was the first alcoholic drink I had in a week and a half. Boy, did it taste good. It was well worth the wait.

Migrating to the plaza again, we saw more pilgrim friends and congratulated them on their journey. One of them told us that before we arrived, he had internally asked for a sign to know whether to go home that day or stay another day in Santiago. When he heard it was my birthday, he knew to stay. So a group of us lay on the cobblestone ground, listening to the Galician bagpipes in the distance and taking in the view of the cathedral.

The brothers and I had been talking about pizza for days, a conversation that started with the quote “the end of the Camino is like the last piece of pizza”. So the three of us sought out a restaurant and shared the last pieces of pizza together as a physical representation of our last full day together.

My Italian brother was on a stricter timeline than the rest of us due to his plane ticket. So when he said he wanted to get his Camino-finish tattoo, I decided to go with him. What he got was extremely symbolic. On the first day of the Camino, before I met him, he sped past me with his trekking poles on his shoulders and a Spider-Man hat clipped to his pack. I thought it was hilarious, so I took a picture. That’s the picture he got tattooed on himself. It not only showcased the achievement of his walk, but the bond between us that blossomed over the course of a month.

We regrouped with a group of pilgrims for a night on the town and my birthday quickly became the theme of the night. One of my friends disappeared from the bar we were in just to return from the cold outside 45 minutes later with the legendary cake of the Camino, a Tarta de Santiago. I pulled out the candle from breakfast, had someone light it, and sat there basking in the love as the group sang happy birthday to me in Spanish, English, Portuguese, and Italian, all at the same time. That moment was a memory that would live with me forever.

The rest of the night was full of celebration.

Darts at the legendary pilgrim bar, Pub Momo.

An underground bar with traditional Galician dancing and a jam session from the locals.

And at 3:45 am, I finally lay down; the brothers and I stopped laughing, closing off the day of a beautiful birthday.

#8333. Wildfires Closed 189 Miles of the Pacific Crest Trail in 2026: What 2027 Hikers Need To Know

According to the end-of-year wildfire recap from the Pacific Crest Trail Association (PCTA), seven fires closed 189 miles of the trail in 2026, burning directly across six miles of the trail itself.

California experienced a below-average fire year, while Oregon suffered a record-setting fire year. However, the fires in Oregon largely avoided the PCT corridor. In California, 329,000 acres burned, with the PCT seeing brief closures from the Corral and Talbot fires totaling 32.5 miles of trail.

In Oregon, over 2 million acres burned, but only 6.1 miles of trail were closed from the Badger Butte and Lemiti fires in Mount Hood National Forest.

The Badger Butte Fire in Oregon. Photo via PCTA

Washington, in contrast, experienced the brunt of this year’s closures, especially later into the summer. Trail crews are still working to evaluate the extent of the damage along the trail.

Statewide, over 650,000 acres burned, leading to 150 miles of closed trail from the Ptarmigan, Miners, Three Queens, King, and Sisi fires. Most importantly, the Ptarmigan Fire led to the closure of the northernmost 50 miles of trail, including the Northern Terminus.

Fire has occurred across landscapes along the PCT for thousands of years, often burning at low intensity every few years to a few decades. While low-intensity burns clear brush, replenish soil nutrients, and support new growth, burned trail corridors take years to recover.

Impacts from the older Dixie and Bear fires continue to drop dead timber and sprout heavy brush each year. Across 2025 and 2026, PCTA staff and volunteers cut 3,100 fallen logs in the Bear Fire scar alone, part of more than 23,000 downed trees cleared across the entire PCT since 2024.

Anyone mapping out a 2027 hike on the PCT should prepare for hazards that last well beyond the timeline of an active fire. Closure orders sometimes extend through winter and into the next season, so hikers need to check the PCTA closures page when making plans. Inside these scars, burned timber can topple without warning, especially from wind, making it unsafe to camp or take long breaks under dead trees.

Burned timber is prone to toppling. Photo via Katie Jackson

Winter weather can also reshape recent burn scars before summer arrives. Scorched slopes are unstable, making them much more prone to landslides. Heavy winter rains could still wash out additional sections before the start of the 2027 hiking season.

Hikers should expect downed trees, overgrown brush, and missing or rough tread. Lost forest canopy also brings more sun exposure and can change water sources, so carrying extra water and checking updated water reports will still be very important.

Even with the ill-timed, unfortunately placed closures, a whole group of 2026 hikers still put together rewarding seasons by staying flexible, taking detours, or planning return trips for the missing miles.

“We got off trail at Snoqualmie in August due to the fires. Our only free weekend in October had snow in the forecast, but we went for it! Feeling incredibly lucky to get to the border this year. 2426 miles, what a journey!”  Photo and quote via Brisket & Lamb

Bringing that same adaptability to the Pacific Crest Trail in 2027 will make navigating the post-fire terrain much easier.

#8330. Weekend Warm-Up: Hurricane Chase

This episode of the storm-chasing TV series Storm Rising is titled “High Plains Insanity.” Produced by National Geographic, it follows Reed Timmer and Mike Theiss as they attempt to navigate and document an increasingly unpredictable storm season.

The storm-chasing segments are filmed like an action movie but, as Timmer makes clear, thrills are not the primary objective. As the climate changes and disrupts weather patterns, tornadoes are no longer limited to traditional seasons and ranges.

“That’s why we need to be on the ground directly in the path of these tornadoes,” Timmer explains, “to record data that no one else is getting.”

Driving into a potential tornado in Montana. Photo: Screenshot

With hurricane and tornado seasons now overlapping, the duo is forced to split up to cover the widest possible area and collect the most data. For the tornadoes, this means deploying a probe into the funnel.

Up north in the High Plains of the U.S. West and Midwest, Timmer and his dog Gizmo narrowly miss storm after storm. Gizmo is better company but is a very poor navigator, while Timmer isn’t much better. He constantly makes wrong turns as he tries to navigate, drive, and prepare equipment. He does take the time to explain the basic weather science behind what he is failing to catch, such as the conditions that created the baseball-sized hail pummeling his windshield.

Timmer and Gizmo drive through the aftermath of a powerful tornado in the Black Hills of North Dakota. Photo: Screenshot

In Florida, Theiss sleeps in his car to maintain a constant vigil along the coast. Our current scientific abilities, he explains, allow us to track the movement of hurricane systems with decent accuracy. But predicting their strength is much more difficult. Systems can suddenly turn deadly or fizzle out with equally short notice. In this case, the terrible-looking storm comes to nothing. He heads back up north to rejoin Timmer and Gizmo.

Gizmo buffeted by fierce winds. Photo: Screenshot

The reunited trio prepares for a final effort, complete with a windshield-replacement sequence. They immediately drive into the gathering storm, passing through a hail core which, of course, destroys the new windshield. The Lord giveth and taketh away, et cetera. Soon they’re racing on a near-empty tank, as the powerful storm threatens to touch down at any moment.

It’s their last chance this season to deploy the probe. When, after a long, tense chase, they actually manage to do it, both erupt into cheers. Gizmo appears unmoved, despite the fact that this ground probe, deployed in a storm of this type, in this area, has never been deployed before and is sure to yield valuable scientific data.

Lou Bodenhemier holds an MA in History from the University of Limerick and a BA in Creative Writing from the University of Arizona. He’s interested in maritime and disaster history as well as criminal history, and his dissertation focused on the werewolf trials of early modern Europe. At the present moment he can most likely be found perusing records of shipboard crime and punishment during the Age of Sail, or failing that, writing historical fiction horror stories. He lives in Dublin and hates the sun.

#8326. Video: Bird Dog Falls 60 Feet into Basalt Chasm, Prompting Special Rescue Mission

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 20, 2026 3:00 PM EST

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

Two unbelievable sights in one spot: a dog trapped in a deep fissure in the ground, and a crew lowering a man into that fissure to save her.

This scene unfolded in southern Idaho on Nov. 8. After a traveling bird hunter’s pointer fell some 60-feet into a basalt crack while hunting wild birds, he had to call in a professional rescue team for help.

“1,200 miles from home hunting a new bird,” wrote Kyle Stokke in an Instagram post on Nov. 12. “[I] was naively unaware of the unique dangers in these new environments.”

A post shared by Kyle Stokke (@kyle.stokke)

Stokke posted a four-minute rescue video last week. Earlier posts show him hunting upland birds with two dogs: a German shorthaired pointer and a Labrador retriever. The trio camped while traveling 1,200 miles cross-country from the Midwest to Idaho. Trouble started when the GSP, Esther, fell into a deep fissure.

“Good gracious!” an unseen rescuer says in the video. “She is down there a freaking long ways.”

Esther fell at least 60 feet. The fissure went deeper than that, but she landed on a ledge above the true bottom. In the video, you can hear her whines echo up the crack as Stokke, filming from above, reassures her.

“It’s okay Esther,” he says. “You’re a good girl.”

Magic Valley Paramedics has a dedicated unit called the Special Operations Rescue Team. SORT is a highly trained, 20-person volunteer crew specializing in low- and high-angle rope rescues.

“The biggest challenge was finding exactly where she was,” Chad Smith, SORT team leader, tells Outdoor Life. “Sound travels differently in the canyon. You could hear her so she seemed close, but she wasn’t. How sound travels in there is really deceiving. It threw us off a little bit.”

SORT averages 20 rescues a year in Twin Falls and Jerome counties. In Smith’s 15 years with SORT, the team has rescued four dogs from fissures, including another bird dog stuck in a similar crack during a previous hunting season. Esther’s rescue happened to fall on the first day of SORT’s semiannual, three-day training exercises.

“The rescue truck is pulled out and everyone is practicing anchors when the supervisor asks if we want to rescue a dog,” reads a post by the organization. “Who can say no to a dog? Training shifts gears real quick.”

The video shows the team straddling a 2-foot-wide black void hidden in flat sagebrush country.

“Wow! I can see her,” says someone offscreen as one rescuer, Trevor Churchman, is slowly lowered into the tight divide on a harness, ropes, and straps rigged to a large tripod set over the opening.

“Tell Kyle she’s doing good,” says Churchman. “She’s walking around and she’s wagging her tail.”

A few minutes later, both man and dog are hoisted out of the crack, and Esther, apparently unharmed, is handed to Stokke. He’s visibly shaking in the video and sounds emotional, but manages a “thank you” for the crew and a “whoa” to keep Esther steady, since everyone is standing near the edge.

“Okay guys, remember there’s an edge there,” a rescuer says. “We don’t have a lot of you guys on ropes.”

The two-hour rescue took place in terrain shaped by prehistoric volcanic activity. Tunnels, lava tubes, cliffs, caves, and crevices run through much of the area’s undeveloped public land. (If that’s hard to picture, think of the better-known volcanic landscape at Craters of the Moon National Monument and Preserve, 90 miles north of Twin Falls.)

Water filters through the porous basalt rock formations, creating seeps, springs, and waterfalls that feed the Snake River, which has carved a deep canyon that’s home to the dramatic spray of Shoshone Falls.

“When hiking around the canyon, cracks in the rock can be quite deep,” the rescue team warns. “This one was deeper than 45 feet. We could not see the bottom, and they can fill with tumbleweeds making them look like level ground. So watch your step.”

Read Next: Idaho Wildlife Commissioner Finally Resigns as Plea Deal in Elk Poaching Scandal Draws Hunter Outrage

It’s rough, remote country that attracts extreme adventurers like BASE jumpers. And it can surprise even experienced bird hunters, such as Stokke, who assume they’re hunting in relatively flat country.

Kris Millgate is a multimedia journalist based in eastern Idaho. She’s a fly fisher, a bird hunter and an avid seeker of awkward moments. Read and watch more of her work at tightlinemedia.com

#8325. American Climber Dies at 5,800m After Summiting Mera Peak

Richard Carlos Skidmore, 66, of the United States, was found dead in his tent on Mera Peak on Sunday, November 22, after summiting the 6,476m mountain the previous morning.

According to Solukhumbu district police, he reached the summit at approximately 10am on Saturday, November 21, and descended to the high camp at around 5,800m, where he spent the night.

He was found dead the next morning. Past Mera Peak climbers have suggested on social media that it was unusual to overnight in the high camp after summiting so early in the day.

His body was evacuated by helicopter to Kathmandu for an autopsy. The cause of death remains undetermined. An initial report raised the possibility of altitude-related illness or a cardiac event, but neither has been established medically.

Skidmore previously summited Lobuche Peak in 2024, according to the Everest Chronicle and the Italian Alpine Club’s publication Lo Scarpone. So he had previous Himalayan climbing experience above 6,000m.

The Himalayan Database does include Mera Peak, but its coverage of popular trekking peaks is selective. However, it notes that since 2018, four people have died on Mera Peak, not counting Skidmore.

#8322. Simms Rip Line PRO Review: We Tested This Heavy-Duty Fishing Rain Gear in Downpours and High Winds

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 20, 2026 3:54 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

Simms has long been an industry staple for fishing apparel, most notably waders and outerwear. I’ve tested and reviewed many Simms products, including its Challenger lineup of rain gear. While that gear has always performed well, its target market is clear: dedicated recreational anglers. I’ve never bothered to test how Simms outerwear handles the trials and tribulations of everyday use by commercial deckhands or saltwater captains because, frankly, why would I? That’s not the audience it’s intended for, and I wouldn’t expect rain gear designed for landlocked fly anglers to hold up for someone used to hauling crab traps in salt spray and freezing rain all day.

Enter Simms’ new Rip Line Pro series of rain gear, the company’s first attempt at a truly hardcore jacket and bibs designed specifically for watermen. The Rip Line Pro series forgoes much of the aesthetic and, shall we say, fanciness of Simms’ standard recreation-oriented rain gear in favor of pure, rugged functionality. From the moment you see the Rip Line Pro jacket and bibs, it’s clear they aren’t meant for a hike down to your favorite blue-ribbon trout stream. Pick them up and feel them in hand, and that point is driven home. This is not casual rain gear, and it doesn’t sacrifice performance for looks. It’s designed for abuse of the highest order, the kind dished out by Mother Nature on the open sea.

So, how does it handle that? The look and feel right out of the box make a promise, but does the Rip Line Pro series deliver? To find out, I spent a few weeks putting this gear through the wringer in one of the best testing grounds I could think of: the Chesapeake Bay in late fall and early winter. Read on to see how the Rip Line Pro performed.

When it comes to testing gear, sometimes the timing doesn’t line up. Given the monthslong drought in the Mid-Atlantic leading up to my test, I was worried that might be the case here. As it turned out, that worry was misplaced. Exactly one day after the Rip Line Pro gear arrived, the forecast called for an epic deluge paired with extreme wind and storms. It was the perfect blend of miserable fishing conditions for testing rain gear. Lucky me.

The forecast proved accurate, and the weather was extreme, almost to a fault. I spent the first few hours of the morning sheltering from lightning under a dockhouse on the water. The Rip Line Pro gear will protect you from a lot, but I don’t think “lightning strike” is on the list. Thankfully, the electrical portion of the storm passed, and I had the rest of the day to fish in a constant downpour and gusting wind.

After a few hours of fishing from a kayak in the rain and getting sprayed with salt water, the first thing I checked was the areas where most rain gear performs worst: the wrists, neck, and ankles. These are typically the hardest spots to protect because they offer a direct path for water to get beneath the rain gear, and they still need to allow some comfort and range of motion. The Rip Line Pro did an outstanding job of keeping moisture out of all three. The wrists and forearms were the most impressive. They feature an extended 1-millimeter neoprene cuff beneath the outer layer that lets this jacket perform almost like a full dry top. Not only does it keep rain out, but the neoprene cuff creates a seal that kept water from flowing down the sleeve even when my arm was fully submerged. I was equally impressed by the neck. While it doesn’t have the full neoprene seal of the sleeves, the hood design and zipper-and-Velcro combination created an impressively form-fitting seal that kept water from dripping down my back or chest. Finally, the ankle cuffs on the bibs did an excellent job of keeping spray and rainwater away from my feet. I was wearing ankle-height deck boots, and the leg cuffs paired perfectly with them to create an angled seal where no water could enter.

Next, I inspected the storage, namely the various dry pockets on both the jacket and bibs. Each has a well-designed, easily accessible exterior pocket sealed by a watertight zipper and tucked beneath a Velcro storm flap for extra protection. Exterior pockets on rain gear are always a gamble, but accessible waterproof storage is important. The pockets on the Rip Line Pro jacket and bibs performed exceptionally well, even after I opened and closed them many times throughout the day to reach various items. Nothing seeped through the pockets to the interior of the suit, and the pockets kept everything inside them dry and functional.

Dry storage and adjustable cuffs matter, but ultimately the most important job of rain gear is keeping you dry all day long. While I was impressed with how well the Rip Line Pro kept water out of various areas throughout the day, the real test would be how wet and cold I got after fishing in rain and wind for upward of 12 hours.

The answer? Not very. For me, the most impressive part of this gear is the overall protection it offered from the elements. After we returned to the boat ramp and loaded our gear, it was time to strip off the rain suit and inspect the damage. No matter how dry you think you are, a bit of unforeseen moisture usually gets through any rain gear after a long day. Apart from a few wet spots where my shirt had been exposed during the day, I was basically bone-dry. The jacket and bibs also did a great job of blocking wind chill. I had underestimated how well that was working during the day, but looking back, I realized how much the gear kept the wind from affecting my comfort. Not that I needed confirmation, but watching my fishing partners shiver miserably and wring out sopping undershirts provided a sense of warm relief, both physically and emotionally.

Protection from the elements, plain and simple. I’ve worn many bibs and rain jackets over the years, some designed for comfort and others for function. The Rip Line Pro does the best job I’ve found of marrying the two, though function is still clearly the priority.

Once you’re fully suited up, this rain gear makes you feel practically invincible, protected by an impenetrable shell of armor. I’m not saying it’s bulletproof, but I could feel an unrealistic sense of confidence building while I wore it. The materials not only kept me bone-dry through a full day of torrential rain but also provided exceptional wind protection during gusts upward of 30 knots. When it comes to protection from extreme water and wind, the Rip Line Pro series is as good as it gets.

With extreme weather protection as the focus, it’s easy to understand why some other features take a back seat. That said, there are still a few areas I’d like to see Simms improve.

The most noticeable is the lack of mobility. The four-layer construction and fully waterproof material make this gear weatherproof, but they also make it rather cumbersome. Simms added articulation points and adjustable areas where possible, but there’s still room for improvement. At times, I felt a bit like a piece of vacuum-sealed meat, or a video game character bolted into a mech suit. Yes, I felt invincible, but I also had a harder time than I care to admit squatting to grab a sandwich from the cooler.

Insulation is another area that could be better. The Rip Line Pro gear does an incredible job of protecting you from water and wind, but it doesn’t have much interior insulation for true winter use. That’s easy to offset by layering proper cold-weather clothing underneath, but I could see a built-in cold-weather liner helping. Perhaps Simms will release a “winter” edition of the Rip Line Pro in the future with a wool liner or something similar.

The Rip Line Pro series of rain gear is not for everyone. In fact, I would say the target market for this gear is fairly narrow. Most casual anglers and outdoor enthusiasts can get by with less extreme, less expensive rain gear and never have an issue. While this gear is fantastic at keeping you dry, I could see it being overkill for an after-work outing during a light summer shower.

However, what the Rip Line Pro series is designed to do, it does extremely well, and the people who need this type of gear will appreciate it. The Rip Line Pro series is built for serious anglers and people who spend as much time on the water as on dry land. As with any gear in the fishing space, the more purpose-built something becomes, the less universal appeal it tends to have. That said, if you fish as much as I do, serious rain gear becomes less a matter of convenience and more a matter of necessity. The Rip Line Pro jacket and bibs offer the type of performance and functionality I value, and I’m sure many other anglers, deckhands, and charter captains who try them will agree.

#8321. New Route on Classic Tien Shan Peak

Three Polish climbers have established a new line on the north face of one of the Tien Shan’s best-known climbing peaks.

Michal Makowski, Robert Paluch, and Rafal Zając took 11 hours to climb their new 850m line on 4,777m Free Korea Peak, in Kyrgyzstan’s northern Tien Shan Mountains. They then spent a further 11 hours returning to base camp.

They named the route The Polish Barber, after pioneer American climber Henry Barber, who soloed the wall in 1976. Their line shares the first 250–300m with Barber’s, Makowski told ExplorersWeb.

“According to the information I have, [the remaining 600-650m] was previously unclimbed and had never been attempted by another team,” said Makowski. “We reached the summit ridge and then began rappelling down from there.

Corona Hut, the team’s base camp. Photo: Polski Związek Alpinizmu

Free Korea Peak — Pik Svobodnaya Koreya in Russian — was already famous among mountaineers in the former Soviet Union because of its 900m north wall. in 2018, Russian climber Valery Balesin suggested that it was no longer possible to find new lines on this well-known face, although advances in steep ice-climbing techniques have clearly opened new possibilities.

“My friends and I researched Free Korea Peak and realized that part of the north face still has unclimbed terrain,” said Makowski.

On the Polish Barber route. Photo: Polski Związek Alpinizmu

Their route was almost completely ice climbing. The crux came midway up the wall, when they faced a particularly steep section of ice that they graded at Alpine Ice 4+. The rest of the route was 3/3+. Fortunately, “protection on the Polish Barber was really good, except for the last 150m, where ice was pretty thin and crusty,” said Makowski.

Climbing by moonlight. Photo: Polski Związek Alpinizmu

Despite its former popularity, they had the valley almost to themselves during their week in camp. There was just one Russian group doing a tourist trek up nearby Corona Peak.

A scrambly section on Free Korea Peak. Photo: Polski Związek Alpinizmu

#8320. Chasing the Camino Coast

With nearly 14 miles to hike today, we started early at 6:30 AM.

Because Vigo is such a massive port city, our morning walk began right along the waterfront. We passed a mile-long fish distribution facility where workers offloaded fresh seafood from ships into warehouses before loading it onto smaller delivery vans.

Standing guard near the loading zone was a man carrying a live falcon. He explained that his job is to scare away seagulls that constantly try to swoop in and steal the fish.

From here, we officially transitioned onto a mix of the Coastal Camino and the parallel Senda Litoral (Litoral Route). While both follow the ocean, the Litoral Route hugs the shoreline much more closely—even winding all the way out around various peninsulas.

After two miles of walking beside roaring city traffic, the trail finally turned into a quiet, intimate medieval village. We took advantage of the peaceful atmosphere and stopped at a local café for pastries and caffeine.

Leaving the café, we wound through narrow village streets and stepped onto a coastal boardwalk stretching over a marshy area beside the bay. We were officially on the Litoral Route now. Because Vigo is a popular starting point for pilgrims walking the minimum 100 km needed to earn their Compostela certificate, we expected to see far fewer crowds on this quieter coastal stretch.

However, the trail wasn’t very well marked, so we had to rely heavily on digital maps to stay on track. Continuing along the waterfront, we passed through a long, tree-lined park dotted with beachfront bars and nightclubs overlooking the sand—an area that must come alive after dark. Even early in the morning, green parakeets were already out grazing in the grass.

The Camino continued right along the water. A cool breeze blew off the ocean, though the bright sun kept us warm as we passed steep, rocky cliffs broken up by small pockets of sandy beaches. By 11:00 AM, we had already covered 9 miles.

We continued bouncing back and forth between the Coastal and Litoral paths. Eventually, the route ran alongside a busy road at the end of a large bay before leading us down a beachside road past an expansive stretch of sand. Crossing over a river, we finally arrived in the village of A Ramallosa, where our albergue was waiting.

Our accommodations for the night were in a family-run apartment building—the family lived on the top two floors and rented out the bottom two. Conveniently, it sat right behind a beachfront bar where we grabbed dinner. We sat outside enjoying the ocean views and a few drinks until the evening chill pushed us indoors, where we exhaustedly turned in for a great night’s sleep.

#8318. Ukrainian Solos New Route in Gokyo Valley, Near Everest

Mykyta Balabanov of Ukraine has soloed a new route on the northeast face of Nepal’s Phari Lapcha (6,017m). Also known as Machhermo Peak, it sits 24km west of Everest in the Gokyo Valley.

Balabanov is still trekking back to Lukla, but has given ExplorersWeb some details and photos about this impressive new route.

The Ukrainian set off on his own up an unclimbed line on the northeast face on November 19. He made two bivouacs on the way up and one more on the descent.

“The level of difficulty is hard to tell, as always when solo, but I’d say it was around 80° on ice, M5+, 5c on rock, and lots of poor snow!” Balavanov said.

He estimates the new route is 1,200m long, and spans some 850 vertical meters from base to summit.

Rough topo of Balabanov’s solo route on the northeast face of Phari Lapcha. Photo: Mykyta Balabanov

Another side of Phari Lapcha saw action just one day before Balabanov, when U.S. guide Bennie Lieber led Canadian Emma Deguara up the Stone(d) Yeti route on the south face.

“I was not related to that climb, as I climbed alone after approaching from a different valley,” Balabanov explained. “However, I saw their tracks on the summit and spotted their bivy place at the bottom of the south face.”

Lieber and Deguara made the second ascent of their chosen route. “While the technical grade (M3, AI2+) is easily digestible, the room for mistakes, due to exposure, is zero,” Lieber said.

Mykyta Balabanov and fellow Ukrainian Mikhail Fomino opened a new route on Ama Dablam in 2024, but they first became widely known for their new route on the Southeast Ridge of Annapurna III in 2021.

#8315. Watch: I Camped and Knocked on Doors to Bowhunt Big Prairie Whitetails — and Shot a Giant

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 20, 2026 4:00 PM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

This fall, I bought a private-land-only tag to hunt deer with my bow in South Dakota — even though I had no access to private land, nor had I ever been to the area that I planned to hunt. My plan was to knock on doors asking for permission to hunt and camp on a landowner’s property. While this sort of adventure — relying on permission for an entire hunt in unknown territory — occurs less frequently than it did years ago, I was pleased to discover that it is still possible.

Once I secured permission, the next challenge would be getting within bow range of a deer.

Over the course of three days in mostly wide-open country, I found myself moving through the perfect puzzle-solving experience that deer hunters are so fond of: locating deer, then figuring out how to hunt them. On the first day, I found deer. On the second day, I nearly pulled the trigger on a shooter buck. And on the third day, I sent an arrow at the biggest buck I’d ever shot.

And while the hunt unfolded perfectly, the recovery was a different story. To see how it all played out, watch the video from my hunt above.

#8314. Road Walking, Fine Dining, and Day Drinking

For about 80 miles near its halfway point, the Ice Age Trail is split in two. When planning my hike, I decided to go east. It’s five miles longer than the west branch, but has 12 more miles of trail and better overnight options.

At Karner Meadow, not far south of the Pleasant Lake bifurcation, I pitched my tent just in time to shelter from a thunderstorm. It was my first of five nights on the east branch.

The Baraboo Hills contain the four biggest climbs on the IAT. I started my day with number one: almost 700 feet of elevation gain. (Numbers two and four were next. I’d conquered number three the previous afternoon without realizing.) At some point during the uphill, I crossed the boundary into Devil’s Lake State Park, but it was still early, and the trail was empty.

At the first campground, there were few signs of life. I stopped at the small store for a drink and a snack, and in that time, Wisconsin woke up. At the next campground, there were cars and pedestrians everywhere. On the north shore of the lake, there were plenty of people swimming and soaking up the mid-morning sun. (The east and west branches rejoin nearby, fyi.)

Things quietened down during the hike up the West Bluff, and I didn’t meet anyone on the summit ridgeline. For almost a mile, the views were among the best of the entire IAT.

Back down at the lake, I found the south shore even busier than the north. I ate an early lunch at the concession stand, then took an extended break before I tackled the East Bluff. The views at the top were also spectacular, and lots of people were enjoying them.

The throngs thinned for a mile or so, and disappeared completely after another mile. I completed my final descent of the day, headed for the Merrimac Ferry, and arrived just in time to see it leave. Still, that gave me time to buy an ice cream. The ferry returned less than 10 minutes later, pulling itself across Lake Wisconsin using three underwater cables.

The half-mile ferry ride only took a few minutes, but it was enough time for my first ice cream to settle. I bought another ice cream on the south shore, replenished my water supply, and walked one more mile to my campsite. A 2700 foot day might not be huge, but it was my biggest on the IAT. It was also my favorite.

The morning marked the beginning of another three day town-to-town relay. I started by admiring the view from Gibraltar Rock, then made a beeline for Lodi. Piggly Wiggly was my first stop, followed by Dollar General. As I continued south along Main Street, I grabbed the opportunity for a spontaneous game of charades with a nearby motorist.

A Mazda CX5 was pulling out of a parking lot, and I made what I thought was the universal sign for “Roll your window down.” In fairness to the driver, she was on her own, and car windows haven’t been cranked by hand since before the turn of the century. She shook her head. Undeterred, I strung together a sequence of three more signs. She shook her head again and drove away. In fairness to me, “Your passenger-side rear tire is underinflated” is a difficult message to get across without words. I hope she had second thoughts before starting the drive back to Texas.

I repacked my food on a picnic table in the park, visited a Kwik Trip on my way out of town, and reached my campsite by late afternoon.

My next destination was Cross Plains. It was a low mileage day, and I arrived in time for a late lunch: sandwich and Cheetos for my main course, apple fritter and Snickers ice cream for dessert. Afterwards, I visited the IAT Alliance’s office to say hello, then the pavilion beside the library to charge my phone. The afternoon was warm, so I waited in the shade before eventually hiking up a small hill to a dispersed camping area (DCA).

I stopped at the local Kwik Trip again for breakfast. Afterwards, the trail led me through a park, along the edge of some cornfields, and on to quiet country roads. There were lots of speedy Saturday cyclists enjoying the cool, overcast weather.

The rain, when it inevitably began to fall, wasn’t worthy of a rain jacket. I continued following the road along the perimeter of a sprawling residential area until the yellow blazes directed me to cut through the neighborhood. For the next few miles, I squeezed and sidled and slunk between houses and back yards and construction sites. I peered furtively out from between hedges and trees before darting across roads. Similar behavior, I imagined, to foxes and raccoons. I crossed a golf course, passed a handful of soccer fields, and entered Badger Prairie County Park without a moment to spare. Drizzle became downpour, and a park pavilion once again saved me from a soaking. I’d made it to Verona.

The rain stopped an hour later. I spent another hour in a nearby Culver’s, bought snacks and dinner at Festival Foods, then returned to the park. I waited for the last visitors to leave before putting up my tent at the DCA, in the dark.

I got up at first light, broke camp quickly, and walked 200 feet to the pavilion. The sun wasn’t fully over the horizon when the first visitor arrived. I charged my phone while eating breakfast, then made use of the drinking fountain and toilet facilities. It was, without a doubt, the most luxurious DCA of the entire trail. Writing a couple of emails delayed me by 40 minutes, and by the time I left, more people had arrived by car and on foot.

There were plenty of hikers enjoying Verona’s extensive trail system as I headed south, but I soon had to return to the road. I also had to make a quick trip to Kwik Trip before leaving town.

During the next few miles, I left noisy county highway for quiet country road. Eventually, I picked up the trail again. Where the IAT joins the Badger State Trail, I met a friendly cyclist who gave me a recommendation: Dot’s Tavern. An hour later, passing the tiny community of Basco, I made the necessary detour. I was eight miles from where I intended to camp, and it was still morning, so I figured I could spare an hour for a beer and some socializing.

Dot’s is a tiny place, 100 yards from the trail, and was busy that particular Sunday. About 20 people were sitting around a small bar and a handful of tables in what looked like someone’s back yard.

Well, long story short: one hour turned into five, one beer turned into six, and yet again, I ended up racing to a campsite while trying to outrun a thunderstorm. Thank you, Dave, Paul, and Laura, for your conversation. The best days sometimes contain the fewest miles.

Cornfield south of Cross Plains, Wisconsin.

#8313. High Winds Thwart Latest Everest Attempt

The Russian team has returned to Everest, aiming to reach at least 7,500m, but high winds have again dashed their plans.

The three-man team of Andrei Vasiliev, Sergey Kondrashkin, and Vitaly Shipilov set off yesterday and reached their cached tent at 5,600m. As leader Vasiliev explained, they expected strong winds at summit altitude until Wednesday, but they intended to keep themselves in lower areas, where the wind speed was acceptable.

However, the wind was too strong to go higher than 5,600m today, so they decided to stay put. MountainClub.com, which has access to the team’s InReach tracker, has posted a partial view of the climbers’ current location:

We asked Jost Kobusch of Germany (also planning to climb the West Ridge of Everest this winter) about that location. He confirmed that they are midway up the vertical mixed section leading from the Khumbu Glacier to the Lho La Pass.

This is the expedition’s fourth foray up the mountain. According to Vasiliev, it is also their last chance to acclimatize before a potential summit push. The Russians’ climbing permit is reportedly valid until December 15.

Their future progress will depend on the wind. Multi-model forecasts indicate that winds are expected to decrease on Thursday but not to disappear. The chart by High Asia Avalanche Watch, which compares seven different weather models, predicts that winds will not drop below 45kph at the summit (on Thursday and Friday). They will then pick up again.

Wind speed at the summit of Everest over the next two weeks, by High Asia Avalanche Watch.

#8310. German Women Climb New Route on Sharphu VI

Last month, five women with the German Alpine Club established a new route on the northwest ridge of Sharphu VI in eastern Nepal. It was only the second overall ascent of the 6,076m peak near Kangchenjunga.

In October, Steffi Feistl, Anna Gomeringer, Kristin Hinkelmann, Kathi Huber, and Fenja Kochl set up their base camp at 4,000m. A huge amount of snow had fallen earlier that season, guaranteeing arduous slogging in some areas.

Their preliminary plan had been to attempt either Sharphu II or Sharphu VI, but Sharphu II’s condition looked too dangerous due to unstable rock, threatening seracs, and hazardous snow conditions.

Sharphu II and VI. Photo: Xtreme Climbers/labels by Gunter Seyfferth

On the other hand, the northwest ridge of Sharphu VI seemed feasible. The team acclimatized over several reconnaissance trips, overnighting up to 4,800m. They then set up their advanced base camp on a moraine ridge at that altitude. Then Kochl, Hinkelmann, and Huber set off over initially easy but deep snow-covered terrain.

They eventually reached a steep section at around 5,300m where powder snow overlying bare rock made placing protection difficult. “The mixed pitches had looked easy, but were steeper than expected,” recalled Huber. “Nasty, granular snow, and no hold on the rock underneath.”

Their work complete, the trio rappelled down, and the following day, Feistl and Gomeringer took their turn. They inched up a difficult mixed pitch followed by 200 vertical meters of burrowing through waist-deep powder.

The route up the northwest ridge of Sharphu VI. Photo: German Alpine Club

After a rest day, the five women set off for the summit. They reached a glacier plateau via a 5,800m, previously unclimbed sub-peak. Here, they bivvied. On October 21, they began the final two kilometers to the main summit. The recent sun had hardened the snow, so they sank at most knee-deep. Eventually, they reached the summit ridge. They had to take turns standing on the summit, because that final ridge was so narrow.

Gomeringer and Kochl on the summit of Sharphu VI. Photo: German Alpine Club

Sharphu VI was first climbed on March 25, 2025, by a Chilean-Nepalese team who thought they’d climbed a different mountain. They went up via the east flank. Before that, Sharphu VI had been attempted only once, in 2023.

The German women’s route featured 1,300m of elevation gain, graded M5, 55°. The expedition was the culmination of the German Alpine Club’s three-year women’s training program.

Jerry Kobalenko is the editor of ExplorersWeb. One of Canada’s premier arctic travelers, he is the author of The Horizontal Everest and Arctic Eden, and has just finished a book about adventures in Labrador. In 2018, he was awarded the Polar Medal by the Governor General of Canada and in 2022, he received the Queen’s Platinum Jubilee Medal for services to exploration.

#8307. Historic Drought Looms Over Arkansas Duck Opener. What Few Ducks Show Up Face Heavy Pressure

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 20, 2026 4:45 PM EST

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

If you asked any serious waterfowl hunter to name their dream duck-hunting destinations, Arkansas would be at the top of nearly every list. Its classic flooded hardwoods and bayous along with the state’s rich rice and grain fields, lure massive flights of puddle ducks and geese to its hallowed waters every year. This has made Arkansas stand out above other southern states as the beating heart of the Mississippi Flyway.

Yet in 2026, Arkansas’ waterfowl season is suddenly looking bleak. A historic drought has many duck hunters worried ahead of the Nov. 21 opener, as the serious lack of water could bring duck hunting to a standstill. Duck hunters on both public and private land may have some serious decisions to make to safeguard ducks and future duck seasons.

For 14 months Arkansas has been without any serious rainfall, leaving rich croplands and, more importantly for ducks, wetlands, drier than they’ve been in decades. Every corner of the state is under a severe drought status, with some areas being more than 20 inches behind in cumulative rainfall totals.

To put the drought in perspective, this is slated to be the state’s third-driest October through January since record keeping began in 1895.

This leaves the Arkansas Game and Fish Commission with very little water to spare, to the point where the agency expects it will only be able to flood around 40 of its more than 70 moist-soil public-land hunting units.

“In my 50 years of duck hunting in Arkansas, I don’t remember it ever being as dry as this,” says Brent Birch, founder of Arkansas’ Greenhead magazine. “It’s been this way all summer and well into the fall. And even with the prospects of a Super El Niño, we have nothing the forecast that says it’s going to resolve anytime soon.”

Without an incredible amount of rainfall in a short amount of time, Birch and other waterfowlers around the state are worried not only about not having places to hunt, but that the ducks may not even show up.

“So far, we are seeing a few groups of gadwalls, pintails, and other early migrating ducks but not in anything close to their usual numbers,” Birch says. “And when it comes to incoming mallards, you can pretty much forget it. It’s just too dry for them. The fact is the closer it gets to 2027 under these conditions, the worse the entire duck season is going to be. And if we don’t start getting some wet weather soon to push ducks down here, we’re looking at a poor outlook for the entire season.”

The reason that Arkansas waterfowl hunting is so good is that it lies at the north end of the historic feeding grounds for ducks and geese in the Mississippi Flyway. Once the cold sets in around their spring and summer breeding grounds in Canada, the birds come south to binge on Arkansas’ rich wetlands throughout the winter. However, with the current drought, the fear is that the incoming ducks will be forced to fly elsewhere.

“Ducks coming south to Arkansas aren’t like ducks in northern states,” says Ducks Unlimited Arkansas regional biologist Jake Spears, who lives in Jonesboro. “Northern birds are more apt to feed in dry fields so long as they have a body of water to roost on nearby. However, in the Southern U.S., they stop feeding in fields and primarily feed in water. They look for things like water plants and waste grains in flooded fields, which Arkansas currently just doesn’t have.”

Spears worries that many ducks are going to fly to Arkansas and then be forced to turn northward again or keep driving south.

“The ducks need food so they may go to Louisiana, or Kansas, or Missouri — wherever they can find that food source,” says Spears. “Which is going to leave both public-land duck hunters and even most hunting clubs just completely out of luck.”

Private-land duck-hunting clubs are a popular option for many Arkansas hunters and visiting nonresidents, as even during droughts, most clubs can pump water to create duck habitat and attract birds. The 2026 drought has been so bad, however, that many clubs won’t even have the water to pump.

This is especially troubling to some public-land waterfowl hunters, who worry that the private hunting clubs that can still pump water may end up attracting what few ducks do make it to Arkansas. This could result in a season that benefits the haves over the have-nots.

“I think hunting clubs are the only thing that’s going to make Arkansas appealing for ducks right now,” Spears says. “Most of the smaller rivers, sloughs, and backwaters we have on public land can be walked across in flip-flops right now. So the clubs that have the option to flood fields and create habitat are where the birds are going to be. But with the water table so low, clubs with the ability to pump will be few and far between, so any ducks that do show up during the season are going to have to be babied quite a bit.”

The fact is that if the drought continues in Arkansas, duck hunters on both public and private land alike are going to have to weigh their priorities. Drought conditions are going to be tough on ducks that migrate through Arkansas, and hunting pressure will make surviving the migration harder even for birds that aren’t killed.

This leaves duck hunters with a choice to make.

“It’s going to come down to responsible management on all sides because if we want to maintain the ducks we have, we have to be careful with them,” Birch says. “There are several components to management. It’s not just hunting and the noise of guns, we must be careful disturbing the ducks in any capacity. Your vehicle is an issue because you’re going to bump ducks going in and out of your hunting area, and you’re going to have to be real careful with how many days you hunt and how many hours you hunt in a day, because you’re going to have to let the birds rest.”

With Arkansas’ duck season lasting a total of 51 days with two relatively short splits, hunters looking to limit out consistently are going to be putting serious pressure on what will already be a limited resource.

So instead of hunting hard, both Birch and Spears recommend self-imposed bag limits and time on the water so that the resource can continue to both be conserved and shared.

“My suggestion to any duck hunter in Arkansas is that if you have ducks on your property, take care of them,” says Spears. “With the limited resources, they aren’t going to be able to handle a lot of pressure. You’re going to have to limit how much you hunt and how many birds you shoot, and not be afraid to cut your hunt short. Get a few birds and then leave so the ducks can get back to the water to feed.”

Read Next: Has Hot Cropping Ruined Duck Hunting? Here’s What the Data and Biologists Say About How Flooded Corn Affects Waterfowl Migration

While there is hope that conditions will change, the fact is that the whole of Arkansas’ 2026 duck season may be a nonstarter for many hunters. That short-term pain, however, could help future duck seasons look a little brighter. Spears and Birch are hopeful for a future where water and the waterfowl will eventually return, filling the skies and waterways like flowing veins and once again making Arkansas the beating heart of the Mississippi Flyway itself.

“We need to be good stewards of the resource,” urges Spears. “Hunters should do everything they can to keep [ducks] around and not shoot them off what water we do have.”

#8305. Resuming the Story

I got to watch THIS spring gradually bloom before me during my AT attempt.  The inexorably slow pace I moved through the woods allowed me to enjoy the year being reborn daily, sometimes hourly and for that I’m grateful.  I say attempt because in August in the Whites I shattered my ankle and had to come off trail for surgery,  recovery, and a recheck of my consitution.

As THIS fall (thanks to a glorious Indian Summer) also progressed incredibly slowly, oozing towards winter.  Again!  I am grateful for the sheer slowness of natural life, matching my pace as I watched the world unfold.  I felt in my core every arduous step this year has presented me.  It felt as if nature itself journeyed with me towards uneasy peace.

After I got back on trail in July I had the absolute best time through Mass, VT & New Hampshire.   The whole vibe of the trail was so different than when I left her in VA.  Coming from the droughty south; these woods felt ALIVE!  Maybe it’s the humidity,  the sheer density of flora here amazed me.

There is a HUGE difference between VT & NH.  Growing up in the Midwest,  VT & NH to me were basically South Maine with less trees.  So, once I sauntered through VT, it’s neighbor punched me in the face.

I see you New Hampshire!!  Like Vermonts angry older & bigger brother who feels he has to one up you EVERY TIME.  Solace Vs. Challenge.  As with most things on the trail; rewarding in it’s own way.

17 Aug I slipped and fell coming down Mt. Garfield in the rain.  Shattered both bones in three separate places.   I called 911, got Pemmigewasset SAR on their way to get me.  I met Knights of the Appalachian Trail while I waited for my heroes to come rescue me.  I was formally Knighted in a beautiful ceremony complete with the requisite “please rise”.  Which i one-leggedly honorably obliged.

My mom and son drove the 17 hours to North Woodstock, NH.  She didn’t like the thought of me doing planes, trains & busses to get home.  Navigating all the above on crutches with a backpack was not my idea of fun either.  Special THANK YOU to The Old Colony Ski Club for taking such great care of me & my family those few days after my accident.   Jeff and Jen:  I will forever be grateful for your hospitality and friendship.

I got home, had surgery, recovering well enough.   Talk about post hike depression!!! Damn ya’ll could’ve warned me i was in for a ride not only coming off trail, but immobile & feeling useless.   It’s humbling to not even be able to clear your dishes from the table after dinner.

North was my warm, cozy & inviting friend this whole journey.   Now directionless I get to navigate to a new star.  What’s the difference between aimless and desultory anyways?  I’m tired of fighting the current,  so I’m choosing to float along a bit.

Thank You all for reading and accompanying my journey.   I may write some excerpts of memorable occurrences in the future.  There are machinations at play,  desires for completion and the acceptance that the trail will provide.   My journeys continue in the next chapter.
